Cutting γ \gamma -Liouville quantum gravity by Schramm-Loewner evolution for κ ∉ { γ 2 , 16 / γ 2 } \kappa\notin\{\gamma^{2},16/\gamma^{2}\}
Morris Ang Columbia University Ewain Gwynne University of Chicago

Abstract
There are many deep and useful theorems relating Schramm-Loewner evolution (SLE κ ) and Liouville quantum gravity ( γ \gamma -LQG) in the case when the parameters satisfy κ ∈ { γ 2 , 16 / γ 2 } \kappa\in\{\gamma^{2},16/\gamma^{2}\} . Roughly speaking, these theorems say that the SLE κ curve cuts the γ \gamma -LQG surface into two or more independent γ \gamma -LQG surfaces. We extend these theorems to the case when κ ∉ { γ 2 , 16 / γ 2 } \kappa\notin\{\gamma^{2},16/\gamma^{2}\} . Roughly speaking we show that if we have an appropriate variant of SLE κ and an independent γ \gamma -LQG disk, then the SLE curve cuts the LQG disk into two or more γ \gamma -LQG surfaces which are conditionally independent given the values along the SLE curve of a certain collection of auxiliary imaginary geometry fields, viewed modulo conformal coordinate change. These fields are sampled independently from the SLE and the LQG and have the property that that the sum of the central charges associated with the SLE κ curve, the γ \gamma -LQG surface, and the auxiliary fields is 26. This condition on the central charge is natural from the perspective of bosonic string theory. We also prove analogous statements when the SLE curve is replaced by, e.g., an LQG metric ball or a Brownian motion path. Statements of this type were conjectured by Sheffield and are continuum analogs of certain Markov properties of random planar maps decorated by two or more statistical physics models. We include a substantial list of open problems.



Acknowledgments.

This work has benefited from enlightening discussions with many people, including Amol Aggarwal, Jacopo Borga, Ahmed Bou-Rabee, Nina Holden, Minjae Park, Josh Pfeffer, Guillaume Remy, Scott Sheffield, Xin Sun, Jinwoo Sung, and Pu Yu.
M.A. was supported by the Simons Foundation as a Junior Fellow at the Simons Society of Fellows.
E.G. was partially supported by a Clay research fellowship and by NSF grant DMS-2245832.

1
Introduction

1.1
Overview

Schramm-Loewner evolution
(SLE
κ
) for
\kappa>0
is a one-parameter family of random fractal curves in the plane originally introduced by Schramm
[
Sch00
]
. SLE describes or is conjectured to describe the scaling limits of various discrete random curves which arise in statistical mechanics. See, e.g.,
[
Law05
,
BN
]
for introductory expository works on SLE.

Liouville quantum gravity
(
\gamma
-LQG) for
\gamma\in(0,2]
is a one-parameter family of random fractal surfaces (2d Riemannian manifolds) which arise, e.g., in string theory and conformal field theory
[
Pol81
]
, and as the scaling limits of random planar maps. LQG surfaces are too rough to be Riemannian manifolds in the literal sense. Instead, a
\gamma
-LQG surface can be represented as a random metric measure space parametrized by a domain in
\mathbbm{C}
(or more generally a Riemann surface), viewed modulo a conformal change of coordinates rule. See Definition
1.1
for a precise definition and, e.g.,
[
BP24
,
Gwy20
,
She22
]
for introductory expository articles on LQG.

Instead of the parameters
\kappa
and
\gamma
, it is often useful to instead describe SLE and LQG in terms of the
central charge
parameters, which are related to
\kappa
and
\gamma
by
1
1

1

Some works associate LQG with the
matter central charge

{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}
instead of our
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
. Our
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
is the central charge of Liouville conformal field theory and is related to
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}
by
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}=26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
.

{\mathbf{c}}_{\operatorname{SLE}}=1-6\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\kappa}}-\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{2}}}\right)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}=1+6\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}\right)^{2}.

(1.1)

Each of SLE and LQG can be associated with conformal field theories with their respective central charges, see, e.g.,
[
KM13
,
DKRV16
]
.

Figure 1:
A Liouville quantum gravity surface parametrized by the disk together with an SLE
κ
-type curve between two marked boundary points. Previous works (see in particular
[
She16
,
DMS21
,
AHS23
]
) have shown that if
\kappa=\gamma^{2}
and the precise variant of SLE is chosen appropriately, then the sub-LQG surfaces parametrized by the regions to the left and right of the curve are conditionally independent given their LQG boundary lengths (see Definition
1.1
for a precise definition of LQG surfaces). In Theorem
1.9
, we show that if
\kappa\not=\gamma^{2}
, then the sub-LQG surfaces parametrized by the left and right sides of the curve are conditionally independent given information about the values along the curve of certain auxiliary random fields, sampled independently from the SLE and the LQG. We also prove variants of this statement for other types of SLE
κ
curves, including ones with multiple complementary connected components.

In this paper we will study the relationships between SLE and LQG. The first such relationship, called the
quantum zipper
, was established by Sheffield in
[
She16
]
. Roughly speaking, this result and its many extensions (including the
mating of trees
theorem
[
DMS21
]
) say the following. Suppose we have a certain SLE
κ
-type curve and a certain
\gamma
-LQG surface, sampled independently from each other, and that the parameters are
matched
in the sense that

\kappa\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\gamma^{2},\frac{16}{\gamma^{2}}}}\right\},\quad\text{equivalently}\quad{\mathbf{c}}_{\operatorname{SLE}}=26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}.

(1.2)

Then the sub-LQG surfaces parametrized by the complementary connected components of the SLE
κ
curve are conditionally independent given the LQG lengths of their boundaries, and their laws can be described explicitly. See Figure
1
. This independence property is the continuum analog of certain Markovian properties for random planar maps decorated by statistical physics models. See the survey article
[
GHS23
]
for more explanation.

Results of the above type have a huge number of applications, to such topics as SLE and LQG individually, conformal field theory (see, e.g.,
[
ARS21
]
), the geometry of random planar maps (see, e.g.,
[
GHS20
]
), random permutations (see, e.g.,
[
Bor23
]
), and the moduli of random surfaces (see, e.g.,
[
ARS22
]
). Particularly noteworthy consequences include the equivalence of
\gamma=\sqrt{8/3}
LQG and the Brownian map
[
MS20
]
and convergences of conformally embedded random planar maps to LQG
[
GMS21
,
HS23
]
. Many of these applications are surveyed in
[
GHS23
]
.

In this paper, we will establish the first relationships between SLE and LQG in the case when the parameters are
mismatched
, meaning that
\gamma
and
\kappa
are not related as in (
1.2
). Roughly speaking, we prove the following. Suppose we have an appropriate SLE
κ
-type curve and a
\gamma
-LQG surface (specifically, an LQG disk), sampled independently from each other as above, but whose parameters do not satisfy (
1.2
). Then the sub-LQG surfaces parametrized by the complementary connected components of the SLE
κ
curve are not independent, but they are
conditionally
independent if we condition on certain extra information along the SLE
κ
curve. The necessary extra information is described by one or more random generalized functions, sampled independently from the SLE and the LQG, with the property that
{\mathbf{c}}_{\operatorname{SLE}}+{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
plus the central charges associated with the extra generalized functions is equal to 26. These extra random generalized functions are described in terms of the theory of
imaginary geometry

[
MS16a
,
MS16b
,
MS16c
,
MS17
]
, which we review in Section
2.5
. See Theorem
1.9
for a precise statement.
Conditional independence statements of the above type were conjectured by Sheffield in private communication.

We also prove similar conditional independence statements when the SLE curve is replaced by other interesting random sets, such as a conformal loop ensemble gasket, a Brownian motion path, or an LQG metric ball. See Theorems
1.11
,
1.13
and
1.14
.

The condition that the total central charge should be 26 dates back to Polyakov’s seminal path integral formulation of bosonic string theory. For the conformal field theory corresponding to LQG coupled with conformal matter, the central charge condition is equivalent to the Weyl invariance of the theory
[
Pol81
,
DP86
]
, i.e., invariance when the underlying Riemannian manifold
(\Sigma,g)
is replaced by
(\Sigma,e^{2\sigma}g)
.
This perspective, together with the ansatz that the conformal field theory should correspond to random planar maps decorated by statistical physics models (see the next paragraph), gives physical context and justification for the results of this paper.
The relationships between SLE and LQG in the matched case (
1.2
) can be viewed as Markov properties for Liouville CFT with central charge
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
coupled to a single matter field of central charge
26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
.
This paper shows that one also has Markov properties when instead Liouville CFT is coupled to multiple matter fields with total central charge
26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
.

Analogously to the matched case, our results are continuum analogs of certain Markovian properties for random planar maps decorated by
multiple
statistical physics models.
Roughly speaking, these properties say the following. Suppose we have a random planar map decorated by a two or more statistical physics models (e.g., a uniform spanning tree and two discrete Gaussian free fields) and we construct an interface from one of the models (e.g., a branch of the spanning tree). Then the planar maps in the complementary connected components of the interface are conditionally independent given the information about other statistical mechanics models along the interface (in our example, this corresponds to the restrictions of the discrete Gaussian free fields to the spanning tree branch). Similar Markovian properties also hold for related objects, such as uniform meanders.
See Appendices
A
and
B
for further explanations.

Furthermore, just like in the matched case, our results have a large number of potential applications and extensions, some of which are discussed in Section
5
.

The proofs in this paper involve several novel ideas which we expect to be useful elsewhere. These include a conditional independence statement for uniform meanders (Appendix
B
) and a “rotational invariance” property for the central charges associated with a collection of independent Gaussian free fields in the setting of imaginary geometry (Proposition
1.17
). This rotational invariance property is the source of the “total central charge 26” condition in our results. See Remark
1.10
and Section
1.5
for further discussion of the main ideas of the proofs.

Finally, a potential extension of the present work would allow us to rigorously construct a
Markovian string trajectory
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
corresponding to bosonic string theory, from a Liouville quantum gravity surface together with
d
independent Gaussian free fields. See Section
1.4
for further discussion.

1.2
LQG and imaginary geometry surfaces

1.2.1
Liouville quantum gravity

For
\gamma\in(0,2)
,
\gamma
-Liouville quantum gravity (LQG)
is, heuristically speaking, the random geometry described by the random Riemannian metric tensor

e^{\gamma\Phi}\,(dx^{2}+dy^{2})

where
dx^{2}+dy^{2}
denotes the Euclidean metric tensor on a domain
D\subset\mathbbm{C}
and
\Phi
is a variant of the Gaussian free field (GFF) on
D
. This Riemannian metric tensor does not make literal sense since
\Phi
is a generalized function.
Following
[
DS11
,
She16
,
DMS21
]
, we rigorously define LQG surfaces as equivalence classes of field/domain pairs modulo a conformal coordinate change formula depending on the parameter
Q:=\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma}
. Equivalently,

Q({\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}})=\sqrt{\frac{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}-1}{6}}\quad\text{ for }{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25,\qquad\qquad\qquad{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}(Q)=1+6Q^{2}\quad\text{ for }Q>2.

Let
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
be the Riemann sphere.

Definition 1.1
.

Consider pairs
(D,\Phi)
where
D\subset\mathbb{C}
is a bounded open set and
\Phi
is a distribution defined on
D
.
For
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
,
a
(generalized)
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
-LQG surface

is an equivalence class of such pairs under the equivalence relation
\sim_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
where
(D,\Phi)\sim_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Phi})
if there exists a homeomorphism
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
with
f(D)=\widetilde{D}
that is conformal on
D
such that
\Phi=\widetilde{\Phi}\circ f+Q({\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}})\log|f^{\prime}|
.

We emphasize that the domain
D
in Definition
1.1
need not be simply connected or even connected.
Definition
1.1
differs slightly from the definitions of LQG surfaces found elsewhere in the literature (e.g., in
[
DMS21
]
), in that we require that
D
is bounded and we require that
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
instead of just
f:D\to\widetilde{D}
or
f:\overline{D}\to\overline{\widetilde{D}}
. The reason for these modifications is that we will eventually consider LQG surfaces coupled to imaginary geometry fields, so we need to ensure that there is a well-defined notion of the argument of
f^{\prime}
. See Section
2.1
for further discussion.

We call an equivalence class representative
(D,\Phi)
an
embedding
of the LQG surface. When
\Phi
is a variant of the Neumann (free-boundary) GFF on
D
, one can define the LQG area measure
\mu_{\Phi}
on
D
, which is a limit of regularized versions of
e^{\gamma\Phi(x+iy)}\,dx\,dy

[
DS11
,
Kah85
,
RV11
,
Ber17
]
.
Similarly, one can define the LQG boundary length measure
\nu_{\Phi}
on
\partial D
and the LQG metric (distance function)
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
on
\overline{D}

[
DDDF20
,
GM21c
]
(see
[
HM22
]
for the extension of the metric from
D
to
\overline{D}
). See Section
2.4
for more background on the LQG metric. These are compatible with the LQG coordinate change, i.e., in Definition
1.1
we have
\mu_{\widetilde{\Phi}}=f_{*}\mu_{\Phi}
,
\nu_{\widetilde{\Phi}}=f_{*}\nu_{\Phi}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\widetilde{\Phi}}=f_{*}\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
, and are thus intrinsic to the LQG surface.

Remark 1.2
.

If
(D,\Phi)
is an embedding of an LQG surface and
U\subset D
is open, we will often slightly abuse notation by writing
(U,\Phi)/{\sim_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}}
instead of
(U,\Phi|_{U})/{\sim_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}}
for the LQG surface obtained by restricting
\Phi
to
U
. Moreover, if instead
D\subset\mathbb{C}
is a bounded set, not necessarily open, then
(D,\Phi)/{\sim_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}}
refers to the LQG surface
(\mathrm{int}(D),\Phi)/{\sim_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}}
. These conventions also apply for imaginary geometry surfaces.

1.2.2
Imaginary geometry

Imaginary geometry (IG)
is, heuristically speaking, the random geometry described by the vector field

e^{i\Psi/\chi}

(1.3)

where
\chi>0
and
\Psi
is a variant of the Gaussian free field on a domain
D\subset\mathbbm{C}

[
MS16a
,
MS16b
,
MS16c
,
MS17
]
.
We associated IG with the central charge
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}<1
which is related to
\chi
by

\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}})=\sqrt{\frac{1-{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}}{6}}\quad\text{ for }{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}\leq 1,\qquad\qquad\qquad{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}(\chi)=1-6\chi^{2}\quad\text{ for }\chi\geq 0.

(1.4)

IG surfaces with central charge
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}
play a central role in the study of SLE
κ
when
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}(\kappa)={\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}
. Roughly speaking, the flow lines of the vector field (
1.3
), i.e., the solutions to the formal differential equation
\frac{d}{dt}\eta(t)=e^{i\Phi(\eta(t))/\chi}
, are SLE
{}_{\kappa}(\rho)
curves where
\kappa\in(0,4)
satisfies
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}(\kappa)={\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}
.
If
\kappa^{\prime}=16/\kappa>4
is the other solution to
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}(\kappa)={\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}
, then SLE
{}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\rho^{\prime})
curves can instead be viewed as “counterflow lines” of this same vector field.

We will also consider IG surfaces in the case when
\chi=0
, i.e.,
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}=1
and
\kappa=4
. In this case, we view SLE
4
curves as level lines of the field
\Psi
(as in
[
SS13
]
), instead of as flow lines of the vector field (
1.3
). See Section
2.5
for background on
\SLE_{\kappa}(\rho)
, flow lines, counterflow lines, and level lines.

Similarly to the case of LQG, we define imaginary geometry surfaces rigorously in terms of a conformal coordinate change rule. Previous works using imaginary geometry have considered IG surfaces described by a single field. In this paper we will need to consider IG surfaces described by multiple fields, which are associated with a vector of central charge values.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n}\leq 1
and write
\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}=({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})
.
Consider tuples
(D,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
where
D\subset\mathbb{C}
is a bounded open set and
\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}
are distributions defined on
D
. A
\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}
-IG surface
is an equivalence class of such tuples under the equivalence relation
\sim_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}
where
(D,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})\sim_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\Psi}_{n})
if there exists an integer
m
and a homeomorphism
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
with
f(D)=\widetilde{D}
that is conformal on
D
such that
\Psi_{i}=\widetilde{\Psi}_{i}\circ f-\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{i})\arg f^{\prime}+2\pi\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{i})m
for all
i
.

Here, when
D
is simply connected,
\arg f^{\prime}
is continuous version of the argument, so
\arg f^{\prime}
is uniquely specified up to an additive constant in
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. For general
D
,
\arg f^{\prime}:D\to\mathbb{R}
can still be uniquely defined up to an element of
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
because
f
is a homeomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
, see Section
2.7
for details.
Definition
1.3
does not depend on the choice of
\arg f^{\prime}
since
m
can take any integer value.

Remark 1.4
.

The original definition of IG surface in
[
She16
,
MS16a
]
only considers
n=1
and makes the integer
m
implicit in the choice of the
\arg
function. We make
m
explicit to clarify the
n>1
case.
A single IG field can be understood as being defined modulo
2\pi\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}})
, but this is no longer the case when there are multiple IG fields. For instance, the difference in boundary values for two IG fields with
{\mathbf{c}}={\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}
is well defined as a real number and not just modulo
2\pi\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}})
. See Section
2.1
for more discussion on differences between our Definitions
1.1
and
1.3
and other definitions in the literature.

1.2.3
LQG surfaces decorated by imaginary geometry fields

We now extend Definition
1.1
to the setting where a
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
-LQG surface is decorated by IG fields, that is, distributions that transform according to the IG coordinate change rule.

Definition 1.5
.

Let
\underline{\mathbf{c}}=({\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},\mathbf{c}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n})
where
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}\geq 25
and
\mathbf{c}_{i}\leq 1
for each
i=1,\dots,n
. Consider tuples
(D,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
where
D\subset\mathbb{C}
is a bounded open set and
\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}
are distributions defined on
D
. We define an equivalence relation where
(D,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})\sim_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Phi},\widetilde{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\Psi}_{n})
if there exists an integer
m
and a homeomorphism
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
with
f(D)=\widetilde{D}
that is conformal on
D
such that

\Phi=\widetilde{\Phi}\circ f+Q({\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}})\log|f^{\prime}|\quad\text{and}\quad\Psi_{i}=\widetilde{\Psi}_{i}\circ f-\chi(\mathbf{c}_{i})\arg f^{\prime}+2\pi\chi(\mathbf{c}_{i})m,\quad\forall i=1,\dots,n.

(1.5)

We call an equivalence class under
\sim_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}}
a
(generalized)
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
-LQG surface decorated by IG fields with central charges
\mathbf{c}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n}
.

An LQG surface decorated by
n
IG fields can be thought of as a continuum analog of a random planar map decorated by
n
statistical physics models. Indeed, for certain decorated random planar maps of this type, the LQG surface should describe the scaling limit of the underlying random planar map and the IG fields should describe the scaling limit of some notion of “height function” associated with the statistical physics models. See, e.g.,
[
BLR20
]
for an example of a situation where an imaginary geometry field arises as the scaling limit of a height function.

We can extend Definition
1.5
to additionally keep track of sets and curves, by requiring that the homeomorphism identifies corresponding objects. Precisely, consider
n+4
-tuples

(D,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},(K_{i})_{i\in I},(\eta_{j})_{j\in J})
where
(D,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
is as in Definition
1.5
,
(K_{i})_{i\in I}
is a collection of subsets of
\overline{D}
, and
(\eta_{j})_{j\in J}
is a collection of parametrized curves in
\overline{D}
. For two such
n+4
-tuples, we say that

(D,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},(K_{i})_{i\in I},(\eta_{j})_{j\in J})\sim_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Phi},\widetilde{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\Psi}_{n},(\widetilde{K}_{i})_{i\in I},(\widetilde{\eta}_{j})_{j\in J})

if for some
f
and
m
as in Definition
1.5
, we have that (
1.5
) holds and also

f(K_{i})=\widetilde{K}_{i},\quad\forall i\in I\quad\text{and}\quad f\circ\eta_{j}=\widetilde{\eta}_{j},\quad\forall j\in J.

(1.6)

In the case where
K_{i}=\{z_{i}\}
is a single point, we simply write
z_{i}
rather than
\{z_{i}\}
.

We will often omit the subscript in
\sim_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
and
\sim_{\underline{\mathbf{c}}}
and just write
\sim
when the choice of
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
or
\underline{\mathbf{c}}
is clear from context.

The main LQG and IG surfaces we will work with in this paper are as follows.

Definition 1.6
.

For
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
, let
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
be the law of the
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
-LQG disk
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i})/{\sim}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
conditioned to have unit boundary length and having one marked boundary point sampled from its boundary length measure. This LQG surface describes the scaling limit of planar maps with the disk topology. It was introduced in
[
DMS21
, Section 4.5]
, and can alternatively be defined via Liouville CFT
[
HRV18
,
Cer21
]
; see Definition
2.2
for a precise description. Similarly, let
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
be the law of the LQG surface
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i},0)/{\sim}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
where
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i})/{\sim}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
is sampled from the re-weighted probability measure
\frac{\mu_{\Phi}(\mathbb{D})}{\mathbb{E}[\mu_{\Phi}(\mathbb{D})]}\cdot\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
(where
\mu_{\Phi}
is the LQG area measure) and 0 corresponds to a point sampled from
\mu_{\Phi}
, normalized to be a probability measure.

In this paper we will not need to work with the precise descriptions of
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
or
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}
be the harmonic function whose boundary value at
z\in\partial\mathbb{D}
is
\arg(\mathbbm{i}z)
, where arg takes values in
[0,2\pi)
. On the boundary,
\mathfrak{h}(z)
equals the counterclockwise tangent direction at
z
, with a discontinuity at
-\mathbbm{i}
.

Definition 1.7
.

For
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}\leq 1
, let
\mathrm{IG}_{{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}}
denote the law of the
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}
-IG surface
(\mathbb{D},h+\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}})\mathfrak{h},-\mathbbm{i})
on the one-pointed domain
(\mathbb{D},-\mathbbm{i})
where
h
is a zero boundary GFF on
\mathbb{D}
. Similarly, for
\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}=({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})
, let
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
be
n
independent zero boundary GFFs on
\mathbb{D}
, and let
\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}
be the law of the
\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}
-IG surface
(\mathbb{D},h_{1}+\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{1})\mathfrak{h},\dots,h_{n}+\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{n})\mathfrak{h},-\mathbbm{i})/{\sim_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}
.

See Lemma
2.7
for a description of
\mathrm{IG}_{{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}}
in other one-pointed domains. In particular, for the unbounded one-pointed domain
(\mathbb{H},\infty)
the IG surface corresponding to
\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}
would be
(\mathbb{H},h_{1},\dots,h_{n},\infty)/{\sim_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}
where the
h_{i}
are independent zero boundary GFFs, see Section
2.1
for details.

Remark 1.8
.

The zero boundary GFF, viewed modulo conformal maps, is the special case of
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}
where
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}=1
. Indeed,
\chi(1)=0
so the
({\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}=1)
-IG coordinate change rule is
(D,\Psi)\sim_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}=1}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Psi})
if
\Psi=\widetilde{\Psi}\circ f
for some homeomorphism
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
which is conformal on
D
. Thus, for a Jordan domain
D
and
x\in\partial D
, if
\Psi
is a zero boundary GFF on
D
then the law of
(D,\Psi,x)/{\sim_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}=1}}
is
\mathrm{IG}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{M}}}=1}
.

1.3
Main results

The main results of this paper take the following form. Suppose we have an LQG disk decorated by a vector of imaginary geometry fields and a set
K
(which could be an SLE curve, an LQG metric ball, etc.), as in Definition
1.5
and the discussion just after.
If the sum of the central charges of all of these objects is 26, then the IG-decorated LQG surfaces obtained by restricting our fields to the complementary connected components of
K
are conditionally independent given, roughly speaking, the IG-decorated LQG surface obtained by restricting the fields to an infinitesimal neighborhood of
K\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
.

For concreteness, we only state our results for the LQG disk, but we expect that similar statements hold for LQG surfaces with other topologies; see Problem
5.3
.

Our first main result gives a conditional independence statement of the above type when we cut by a chordal SLE curve. See Figures
1
and
2
(a). We first discuss the setup.

Suppose
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
and
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}},{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n}\leq 1
satisfy

{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}+{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}+{\mathbf{c}}_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbf{c}}_{n}=26.

(1.7)

Let
(\mathbb{D},\widetilde{\Phi},-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a central charge-
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
LQG disk with unit boundary length (i.e., a sample from
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
; see Definition
1.6
), let
x\in\partial\mathbb{D}
be the point such that the two boundary arcs from
-\mathbbm{i}
to
x
each have LQG length
\frac{1}{2}
, and apply a conformal map fixing
-\mathbbm{i}
and sending
x\mapsto\mathbbm{i}
to get an embedding
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i})
of the same LQG surface. For this embedding, the LQG lengths of the boundary arcs from
-\mathbbm{i}
to
\mathbbm{i}
are equal. Equivalently,
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i},\mathbbm{i})
is an embedding of a doubly marked LQG disk with left and right boundary lengths each equal to
1/2
.

Independently from
\Phi
, let
(\mathbb{D},\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of an imaginary geometry surface sampled from
\mathrm{IG}_{({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}
(Definition
1.3
).
Also let
\kappa>0
satisfy
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}(\kappa)={\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}
and let
K
be the trace of an independent
\SLE_{\kappa}(\rho_{L};\rho_{R})
curve in
(\mathbb{D},-\mathbbm{i},\mathbbm{i})
(with force points immediately to the left and right of its starting point) such that

\min(\rho_{L},\rho_{R})>-2\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{L}+\rho_{R}\in\{-2,\kappa-6\}.

(1.8)

This constraint on
\rho_{L},\rho_{R}
ensures that
K
can be realized as a flow line or counterflow line of an imaginary geometry surface as in Definition
1.7
embedded into
(\mathbbm{D},-\mathbbm{i})
, see
[
MS16a
, Theorem 1.1]
or Section
2.5
.

Recall that we want to condition on the IG-decorated LQG surface obtained by restricting
\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}
to an infinitesimal neighborhood of
K\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
.
To make sense of this, we will define the
\sigma
-algebra we want to condition on as the intersection of
\sigma
-algebras obtained by restricting our fields to neighborhoods of
K\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
.
This is similar to how one defines the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the restriction of the GFF to a closed set when talking about local sets (see
[
SS13
, Section 3.3]
).

The LQG metric
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
provides a convenient way of defining neighborhoods of
K\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
in a way which is intrinsic to the LQG surface.
We write
\mathcal{B}_{r}(z;{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})
to denote the
{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}
-metric ball centered at
z
having radius
r
, and more generally, for a set
S\subset\mathbb{D}
, we write

\mathcal{B}_{r}(S;{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})=\{z\>:\>{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}(z,S)<r\}.

(1.9)

We then define (using the notational convention of Remark
1.2
) the
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{F}=\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{B}_{0+}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}),\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}}}\right)/{\sim}}}\right):=\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\sigma((\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}),\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},K,-\mathbbm{i})/{\sim}).

(1.10)

See Section
2.2
for a precise definition of
\sigma
-algebras generated by decorated LQG surfaces.
We use the LQG metric
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
to give a concise definition of
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.10
), but there are also other equivalent definitions. For example,
\mathcal{F}
can equivalently be defined using space-filling SLE segments rather than the LQG metric, see Lemma
3.14
.

We emphasize that
\mathcal{F}
contains much less information than
\sigma(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D},\Phi|_{K},\Psi_{1}|_{K},\dots,\Psi_{n}|_{K})
since we are viewing everything modulo conformal maps.
For example, in the closely related case where
\kappa=\gamma^{2}\in(2,4)
,
\Phi
is an embedding of the so-called weight 4
\gamma
-LQG disk with left and right boundary lengths both equal to 1,
K
is an independent
\SLE_{\kappa}
curve, and we have no auxiliary imaginary geometry fields (i.e.,
n=0
), then it is possible to show
2
2

2

This follows from two inputs. Firstly, conditioned on
\nu_{\Phi}(K)
, cutting by
K
gives two independent LQG disks with two marked boundary points and boundary arcs of lengths 1 and
\nu_{\Phi}(K)

[
AHS23
]
. Secondly, if
(\mathbb{D},\widetilde{\Phi},-\mathbbm{i})/{\sim}
is an LQG disk, then
\sigma((\partial\mathbb{D},\widetilde{\Phi})/{\sim})=\sigma(\nu_{\widetilde{\Phi}}(\partial\mathbb{D}))
; this can be obtained from
[
MSW21
, Theorem 5.1]
by exploring a small neighborhood of
\partial\mathbb{D}
using conformal percolation interfaces.
that
\mathcal{F}=\sigma((\mathcal{B}_{0+}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}),\Phi)/{\sim})
equals
\sigma(\nu_{\Phi}(K))
, where
\nu_{\Phi}
denotes
\gamma
-LQG length.
We expect that similar statements also hold in other situations where
\kappa=\gamma^{2}
and there are no auxiliary imaginary geometry fields. See also Problem
5.5
.

Figure 2:
For (a)–(e),
K
is the green set. Our main results state that conditioned on an infinitesimal LQG neighborhood of
K\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
viewed as an LQG surface decorated by IG fields
\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}
, the complementary LQG surfaces decorated by IG fields are conditionally independent.
(a)

K
is an
\SLE_{\kappa}
-type curve for
\kappa>0
(Theorem
1.9
); in the figure we have
\kappa>4
.
(b)

K
is the gasket of a
\mathrm{CLE}_{\kappa}
for
\kappa\in[4,8)
(we have drawn simple loops for clarity, but in actuality CLE
κ
loops intersect themselves and each other for
\kappa\in(4,8)
).
(c)

K
is the union of a pair of
\SLE_{\kappa}
-type flow lines of an IG field started at a bulk point where
\kappa<4
(Theorem
1.12
).
(d)

K
is the image of a Brownian motion run until it hits the boundary (Theorem
1.13
).
(e)

K
is an LQG metric ball grown until it hits
\partial\mathbb{D}
(Theorem
1.14
).
(f)
A conditional independence statement (Theorem
1.16
) also holds for the LQG-IG peeling processes
(K_{\cdot})_{[0,T]}
of Definition
1.15
.
For each
t,\delta>0
, conditioned on
T\geq t
, let
t^{\prime}
be the earlier of
T
and the time the process hits
\partial\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})
. Here
K_{t}
is dark green,
K_{t^{\prime}}\backslash K_{t}
is light green, and
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K_{t};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})
is gray and green.
For LQG-IG peeling processes,

(\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}),\Phi,(K_{\cdot})_{[0,t^{\prime}]})/{\sim}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
given
(\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}),\Phi,(K_{\cdot})_{[0,t]})/{\sim}
.

Theorem 1.9
.

In the setting described just above, the IG-decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
parametrized by the connected components
3
3

3

To be precise, one assigns indices to the connected components
U_{i}
in some way measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}
, for instance in decreasing order of their central charge-
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
LQG boundary lengths with respect to
\Phi
. Then Theorem
1.9
states that the IG-decorated LQG surfaces
(U_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}
.

U
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash K
(Definition
1.5
) are conditionally independent given the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
of (
1.10
).

Remark 1.10
.

Conditional independence statements of the type proven in this paper have been expected to be true at least for several years, e.g., based on Markovian properties of decorated random planar maps (see Appendix
A
).
We first heard about such properties from Sheffield in private communication.

However, proofs of such statements turned out to be rather elusive, in large part because it is not clear where the condition that the total central charge is 26 appears in the continuum setting (except in the matched case (
1.2
)).
In this paper, the total central charge condition arises by combining the relationship between SLE and LQG in the matched case; a certain locality property for imaginary geometry fields with central charge
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}=0
(Theorem
3.10
), which allows us to ignore such fields when proving conditional independence statements; and a “rotational invariance” property for the total central charge associated with a vector of IG fields (Proposition
1.17
). See Section
1.5
for details.

Another way to see the central charge in the continuum, which may provide an alternative route to results of the type proven in this paper, is to weight the law of LQG surfaces by the Brownian loop soup partition function, in an appropriate regularized sense. See
[
APPS22
]
for details.

Instead of using
\SLE_{\kappa}
, we can cut by a
conformal loop ensemble
(CLE
κ
), a random countable collection of non-nested loops in a simply connected domain
D\subset\mathbb{C}
which locally look like SLE
κ
curves
[
She09
,
SW12
]
. See Figure
2
(b).
The
gasket
of the CLE
κ
is the closure of the union of the loops.

Theorem 1.11
.

Theorem
1.9
holds if we replace
K
by the gasket of a
\mathrm{CLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbb{D}
for
\kappa\in[4,8)
, sampled independently from everything else.

The full parameter range for
\mathrm{CLE}_{\kappa}
is
\frac{8}{3}<\kappa<8
. We expect that Theorem
1.11
can also be proven for the remaining case
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
by similar arguments, see Remark
3.15
.

Our next theorem applies to SLE started at the bulk marked point of
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
which we sample via imaginary geometry, see Figure
2
(c). This setup is particularly interesting since the outer boundary of space-filling SLE run until it hits the marked point is a pair of flow lines started at that point
[
MS17
, Section 1.2.3]
, see Section
2.5
for IG background.

Theorem 1.12
.

Suppose
\kappa<4
.
Let
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
and
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n}\leq 1
satisfy
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}+{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}(\kappa)+{\mathbf{c}}_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbf{c}}_{n}=26
. Let
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,0,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a unit boundary length LQG disk with a marked interior point, i.e., a sample from
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
(Definition
1.6
). Independently, let
(\mathbb{D},\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Psi,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{IG}_{({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n},{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}(\kappa))}
(Definition
1.7
). Let
K
be the union of a pair of flow lines of
\Psi
started at
0
in
\mathbb{D}
and run until they hit the boundary, with two distinct specified angles
[
MS17
, Theorem 1.1]
. Let
\mathcal{F}=\sigma((\mathcal{B}_{0+}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}),\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},0)/{\sim})
be defined as in (
1.10
) (with an extra marked point at 0). Then the decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
parametrized by the connected components
U
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash K
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}
.

In Theorem
1.12
we exclude the
\kappa=4
case since the natural analog of
K
in this case is not a pair of curves, but rather a collection of nested “level loops”. This is related to the fact that the
\kappa=4
analog of space-filling
\SLE_{\kappa}
is not a curve
[
AHPS21
]
.
Next, in the case when
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}=0
, instead of cutting using an
\SLE_{\kappa}
-type curve, we can use Brownian motion, see Figure
2
(d). We understand Brownian motion to have central charge 0, see Remark
A.3
for details.

Theorem 1.13
.

Suppose
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
and
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n}\leq 1
satisfy
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}+{\mathbf{c}}_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbf{c}}_{n}=26
. Let
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,0,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
(Definition
1.6
). Independently, let
(\mathbb{D},\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{IG}_{({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}
(Definition
1.7
). Let
K
be the trace of a Brownian motion started at
0
and stopped when it hits
\partial\mathbb{D}
, sampled independently from everything else. Let
\mathcal{F}=\sigma((\mathcal{B}_{0+}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}),\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},K,0)/{\sim})
be defined as in (
1.10
). Then the decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
parametrized by the connected components
U
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash K
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}
.

Our next result applies when we cut by an LQG metric ball, and is illustrated in Figure
2
(e).

Theorem 1.14
.

Theorem
1.13
holds if we instead let
K
be the
{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}
-metric ball centered at
0
grown until it hits
\partial\mathbb{D}
, i.e.,
K=\overline{\mathcal{B}_{{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}(0,\partial\mathbb{D})}(0;{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})}
.

There are also analogs of Theorem
1.13
and
1.14
for other growth processes started from 0 and stopped upon hitting
\partial\mathbb{D}
which depend on the LQG surface in a local manner. Some examples include
LQG harmonic balls
defined in
[
BG22b
]
, which describe the scaling limit of internal DLA on random planar maps
[
BG22c
]
; and finite unions of Brownian motion paths, LQG metric balls, and harmonic balls. See Theorem
3.19
for a general statement.

Finally, we can define exploration processes that depend only on an infinitesimal neighborhood of the explored region; see Figure
2
(f). Conditional independence results also hold for such processes.

Definition 1.15
.

Suppose
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
and
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n}\leq 1
satisfy
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}+{\mathbf{c}}_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbf{c}}_{n}=26
. Let
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
(Definition
2.2
) and independently let
(\mathbb{D},\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{IG}_{({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}
(Definition
1.7
). Let
(K_{t})_{t\leq T}
be an increasing process of compact connected subsets of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
with random duration
T<\infty
, such that
K_{0}=\partial\mathbb{D}
, and the process is continuous with respect to the Hausdorff metric. We call
(K_{t})_{t\leq T}
an
LQG-IG peeling process
if the following holds.

Let
\delta>0
. For
t\leq T
define
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\delta}=\sigma(({\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t}\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},(K_{s})_{s\leq t})/{\sim})
. Let
\tau
be any stopping time for
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\delta}
. Let
\tau^{\prime}=T\wedge\inf\{t\geq\tau\>:\>K_{t}\cap\partial\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{\tau};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})\cap\mathbb{D}\neq\emptyset\}
. Conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}^{\delta}
, the decorated LQG surface
({\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{\tau}\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},(K_{t})_{t\leq\tau^{\prime}})/{\sim}
is conditionally independent from
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
.

Roughly speaking,
(K_{t})_{t\leq T}
is an LQG-IG peeling process if the growth of
K_{t}
depends locally on
\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}
, modulo LQG / IG coordinate change. Examples of LQG-IG peeling processes include LQG metric balls started at boundary points (due to the locality property of the LQG metric, see Section
2.4
), chordal SLE
6
curves sampled independently from everything else (due to the locality property of SLE
6

[
Law05
]
), and flow lines or counterflow lines of
\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}
started from boundary points (since flow and counterflow lines depend on the IG field in a local way). The reason for the name “LQG-IG peeling process” is that these processes are in some sense a continuum analog of “peeling processes” for random planar maps
[
Ang03
]
, except that the growth is allowed to depend on the IG fields
\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}
, instead of just on the LQG field
\Phi
.

Theorem 1.16
.

In the setting of Definition
1.15
, conditioned on
\mathcal{F}:=\bigcap_{\delta>0}\mathcal{F}_{T}^{\delta}
, the decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
parametrized by the connected components
U
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash K_{T}
are conditionally independent.

1.4
Towards a Markovian string theory

A potential application of the present work is to construct a
Markovian string theory
corresponding to bosonic string theory, a problem posed by Sheffield in private communication. To motivate this, we begin with a lower-dimensional analogy, where a particle is moving in
d
-dimensional space. In classical mechanics, the trajectory
\phi:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the particle is deterministic and minimizes the action
S(\phi)
. In quantum mechanics, however, one has to consider a formal “sum over all possible trajectories” called a path integral, given by
e^{\mathbbm{i}S(\phi)}D\phi
where
D\phi
is the formal “uniform measure on the space of functions
[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
”. Performing Wick rotation corresponds to considering the real-valued “measure”
e^{-S_{\mathrm{Euc}}(\phi)}D\phi
where
S_{\mathrm{Euc}}
is the Euclidean action corresponding to
S
. When
S_{\mathrm{Euc}}(\phi)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{2}(\frac{d\phi}{dt})^{2}\,dt
, this measure can be interpreted as the law of Brownian motion, a canonical model for the trajectory of a particle in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We now turn to the setting of bosonic string theory in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for integer
d\geq 1
. A string is a map from the circle
\mathbbm{S}^{1}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and its trajectory is described by a map
\phi:\mathbbm{S}^{1}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The resulting surface
\phi(\mathbbm{S}^{1}\times[0,1])
is called the
worldsheet
. Polyakov
[
Pol81
]
proposed that after Wick rotation, the worldsheet is a random surface described by LQG with central charge
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}=26-d
decorated by
d
independent Gaussian free fields. Namely, the worldsheet is the embedding of the LQG surface in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
using the
d
fields as coordinates. The physics and mathematics literature largely considers this construction at the level of partition functions (see e.g.
[
Nak04
,
GRV19
]
): rather than studying an actual embedding in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, one simply weights the law of the LQG surface by
(\det\Delta)^{-d/2}
, the partition function of
d
free fields. On the other hand, in analogy with the previous paragraph, one could attempt to construct a random string trajectory in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
from the worldsheet, and show that it satisfies a natural Markov property, analogous to the Markov property of Brownian motion.

The present work represents substantial progress since it gives Markov properties when LQG is coupled with multiple random fields.
The main additional step needed to get from the results of this paper to the construction of a Markovian string theory in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
d\in\{1,\dots,25\}
is to extend our results to the case of LQG with
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}\in[1,25)
(see Problems
5.1
and
5.2
).
Indeed, for
d\in\{1,\dots,25\}
, we also have
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}=26-d\in\{1,\dots,25\}
.
This means that the LQG involved in Polyakov’s bosonic string theory is in the supercritical (a.k.a. strongly coupled) phase. This phase is much more mysterious than the subcritical or critical cases when
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}\geq 25
, even at a physics level of rigor. However, recent mathematical progress on supercritical LQG has been made, e.g., in
[
GHPR20
,
DG23a
,
Pfe21
,
DG23b
,
AG23
]
.

Defining Markovian string trajectories in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
may also be of interest in the probabilistic study of Yang-Mills theory, due to various manifestations of gauge-string duality and formulas expressing Wilson loop observables in terms of sums over discrete surfaces in
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
. See
[
Cha19
,
MP19
,
CPS23
]
for some recent rigorous results along these lines.

1.5
Main ideas of the proof

Theorems
1.9
–
1.16

are all special cases of Theorem
3.3
for
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
and Theorem
3.19
for
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
.
The proof of Theorem
3.3
can be broken down into three steps, each generalizing the result of the previous. Theorem
3.19
is similarly shown.
These steps are carried out in Sections
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
respectively.

Step 1.

We prove a discretized version of Theorem
3.3
with
n=1
(a single IG field with central charge
26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
) using the seminal
mating-of-trees
theorem which describes a
\gamma
-LQG surface decorated by an independent space-filling
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}=16/\gamma^{2}}
curve in terms of planar Brownian motion
[
DMS21
]
. The desired conditional independence is a consequence of the Markov property of Brownian motion and a combinatorial independence property for submaps of a planar map arising from discretizing LQG decorated by SLE (a minor variant of the
mated-CRT map
).
The details of this part of the argument are given in Section
4
.
The combinatorial independence property is a generalization of a certain conditional independence property for uniform meanders, which is of independent interest and which we prove in Appendix
B
.

Step 2.
We use the
locality
of
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}=0}
to extend to the setting of
n\geq 1
IG fields with central charges
(\mathbf{c}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n})=(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},0,\dots,0)
. This locality property, stated as Theorem
3.10
, is due to
[
Dub09b
]
and can be viewed as an IG field variant of the locality of
\SLE_{6}
.

Step 3.

Suppose we have a vector of
n
independent IG fields with central charges
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n}\leq 1
satisfying
{\mathbf{c}}_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbf{c}}_{n}=26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
. We
rotate
this vector of IG fields to get another vector of
n
independent IG fields with central charges
(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},0,\dots,0)
. We then apply the case treated in Step 2 to conclude.

The rotation is accomplished by means of the following elementary but extremely useful observation, which states that
n
independent IG fields can be linearly transformed into another collection of
n
independent IG fields with the same total central charge.

Proposition 1.17

(Rotation of IG fields)
.

Fix
n\geq 1
and let
A
be an
n\times n
orthogonal matrix. Let
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n}\leq 1
and let
\chi_{i}=\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{i})
. Suppose
(\mathbb{D},\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},-\mathbbm{i})
is an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{IG}_{({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}
(Definition
1.7
). Define

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\Psi}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\widehat{\Psi}_{n}\end{array}}}\right)=A\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Psi_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\Psi_{n}\end{array}}}\right),\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\chi}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\widehat{\chi}_{n}\end{array}}}\right)=A\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\chi_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\chi_{n}\end{array}}}\right)

(1.11)

and suppose
\widehat{\chi}_{i}\geq 0
for all
i
.
Let
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{i}=1-6\widehat{\chi}_{i}^{2}
. Then
\sum_{i}\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{i}=\sum_{i}{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
, the IG surface

(\mathbb{D},\widehat{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\Psi}_{n},-\mathbbm{i})/{\sim_{(\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}}
is well defined (independently of the choice of embedding
\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}
) and its law is

\mathrm{IG}_{(\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}
.

Proof.

Since orthogonal matrices preserve the Euclidean norm, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{i}=n-6\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\chi}_{i}^{2}=n-6\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{c}}_{i}.

Next, we verify that multiplication by
A
as in (
1.11
) transforms a
({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})
-IG surface into a
(\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{n})
-IG surface; that is, multiplication by
A
is compatible with the coordinate change rule in Definition
1.3
. If
(D,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})\sim_{({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\Psi}_{n})
and
f
is a homeomorphism witnessing this equivalence with
f(D)=\widetilde{D}
, then

A\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Psi_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\Psi_{n}\end{array}}}\right)=A\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\widetilde{\Psi}_{1}\circ f-\chi_{1}(\arg f^{\prime}-2\pi m)\\
\vdots\\
\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}\circ f-\chi_{n}(\arg f^{\prime}-2\pi m)\end{array}}}\right)=A\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\widetilde{\Psi}_{1}\circ f\\
\vdots\\
\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}\circ f\end{array}}}\right)-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\chi}_{1}(\arg f^{\prime}-2\pi m)\\
\vdots\\
\widehat{\chi}_{n}(\arg f^{\prime}-2\pi m)\end{array}}}\right).

Finally, we identify the law of
(\mathbb{D},\widehat{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\Psi}_{n},-\mathbbm{i})/{\sim_{(\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
\Psi_{i}=h_{i}+\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{i})\mathfrak{h}
for
i=1,\dots,n
where
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
are independent zero boundary GFFs on
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathfrak{h}
is the harmonic function on
\mathbb{D}
with boundary data given by
\arg(\mathbbm{i}\cdot)
, as in Definition
1.7
.

Let
(\widehat{h}_{1},\dots,\widehat{h}_{n})
be the vector of fields obtained by multiplying
(h_{1},\dots,h_{n})
by
A
, as in (
1.11
). We claim that
(\widehat{h}_{1},\dots,\widehat{h}_{n})
also has the law of
n
independent zero boundary GFFs. Indeed, a zero boundary GFF can be sampled as
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}g_{i}f_{i}
where
(f_{i})
is an orthonormal basis of the Hilbert space completion of compactly supported functions on
\mathbb{D}
with finite Dirichlet energy and
g_{i}
are independent standard Gaussians
[
She07
]
. If
\underline{g}
is a standard Gaussian in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
then
\underline{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}A\underline{g}
. Applying this to the coefficients in the orthonormal basis expansions of
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
gives the claim.

From (
1.11
) we have
\widehat{\Psi}_{i}=\widehat{h}_{i}+\widehat{\chi}_{i}\mathfrak{h}
for all
i
, so the law of
(\mathbb{D},\widehat{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\Psi}_{n})/{\sim_{(\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}}
is
\mathrm{IG}_{(\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}
.
∎

Remark 1.18
.

The idea of rotating a vector of fields via an orthogonal matrix to get a new vector of fields with the same total charge is also used in
[
AG23
]
. In that paper, a key idea is to rotate the field describing a critical (
\gamma=2
) LQG disk and a zero-boundary GFF to get two LQG disks of central charges
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}\in(1,25)
. See also
[
AG23
, Appendix A]
for a rotational invariance property analogous to Proposition
1.17
for vectors of fields sampled from the infinite measure on LQG disks or spheres.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Comments on conformal coordinate change

Our definitions of LQG and IG surfaces (Definitions
1.1
,
1.3
and
1.5
) differ slightly from definitions elsewhere in the literature
[
She16
,
MS16a
,
DMS21
]
. In this subsection we will explain the extent of the difference and the reason why we use a different definition.
For concreteness we will focus on the case of IG surfaces, but a similar discussion also applies for LQG surfaces.

In most other works on imaginary geometry, one only considers simply connected IG surfaces, and defines an IG surface as an equivalence class of pairs
(D,\Psi)
where
D\subset\mathbb{C}
is a
simply connected
and
possibly unbounded
open set and
\Psi
is a distribution on
D
, and
(D,\Psi)\sim_{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\prime}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Psi})
if there is a conformal map
f:D\to\widetilde{D}
such that
\Psi=\widetilde{\Psi}\circ f-\chi({\mathbf{c}})\arg f^{\prime}+2\pi\chi({\mathbf{c}})m
for some integer
m
. Note that
f
need not extend to a homeomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
.

For the set of pairs
(D,\Psi)
where
D
is a Jordan domain, the relations
\sim_{\mathbf{c}}
and
\sim_{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
are equivalent:
(D,\Psi)\sim_{\mathbf{c}}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Psi})
if and only if
(D,\Psi)\sim_{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Psi})
. On the larger set of pairs
(D,\Psi)
where
D
is bounded and simply connected,
(D,\Psi)\sim_{\mathbf{c}}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Psi})
implies
(D,\Psi)\sim_{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\Psi})
(but the reverse implication does not hold, since
\partial D
can have “exterior” self-intersections). Consequently,
for any bounded simply connected
D
, any object determined by
(D,\Psi)/{\sim_{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}}
is also determined by
(D,\Psi)/{\sim_{\mathbf{c}}}
.
In particular, flow and counterflow lines of a simply connected IG surface
(D,\Psi)/{\sim_{\mathbf{c}}}
are determined by
(D,\Psi)/{\sim_{\mathbf{c}}}
(see Section
2.5
). To discuss these objects, we will sometimes want to embed in the upper half-plane
\mathbb{H}
via
\sim_{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
to match the convention of
[
MS16a
]
.

We now explain why we use
\sim_{\mathbf{c}}
rather than
\sim_{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
.
For non-simply connected domains,
in order to define an IG surface as an equivalence class of pairs
(D,\Psi)
, we need
\arg f^{\prime}
to be well-defined modulo a single global additive multiple of
2\pi\chi
. This is not necessarily true if we only require
f:D\to\widetilde{D}
to be conformal, but as we see in Proposition
2.6
there is a canonical way to define
\arg f^{\prime}
(modulo additive multiple of
2\pi
) when
f
extends to a homeomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
. This explains the condition on
f
in Definition
1.3
. The homeomorphism condition distinguishes the point
\infty
on the Riemann sphere, and we choose to work with bounded domains to avoid interacting with this point.

We similarly work with a modified definition of LQG surface, so as to make them compatible with IG surfaces (as in Definition
1.5
).

Remark 2.1
.

An important configuration that arises in
[
MS16a
,
She16
,
DMS21
]
is the slitted domain
\mathbb{H}\backslash\eta
where
\eta
is a simple curve from
0
to the interior of
\mathbb{H}
arising as a segment of a flow line of an IG field
\Psi
. Crucially, one can embed the slitted surface in
\mathbb{H}
using
\sim_{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
, i.e.,
(\mathbb{H}\backslash\eta,\Psi)\sim_{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H},\widetilde{\Psi})
for some
\widetilde{\Psi}
. This is not possible using our equivalence relation
\sim_{\mathbf{c}}
. We will not need to consider slitted domains in this paper since we will always cut our domains by curves which disconnect the domain (usually into connected components which are each Jordan domains), and we view the connected components as parametrizing separate LQG/IG surfaces.

2.2
\sigma
-algebras generated by LQG surfaces

One can define a topology (and hence a
\sigma
-algebra) for LQG surfaces with specific conformal structures (e.g., simply connected LQG surfaces) by conformally mapping to a canonical reference domain, see, e.g.,
[
GM21a
, Section 2.2.5]
. It is not obvious how to define a topology for, say, LQG surfaces parametrized by domains with infinitely many complementary connected components. However, it is straightforward to define the
\sigma
-algebras generated by such LQG surfaces, as we now explain.

Consider the set
\mathcal{X}
of pairs
(D,\phi)
where
D
is a bounded open set and
\phi
is a generalized function belonging to the local Sobolev space
H^{-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D)
. We equip
\mathcal{X}
with some reasonable topology. For concreteness, we use the topology whereby
(D_{n},\phi_{n})
converges to
(D,\phi)
if and only if
(\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\})\setminus D_{n}\to(\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\})\setminus D
with respect to the Hausdorff distance for the spherical metric on
\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
; and, for each bounded open set
U
with
\overline{U}\subset D
, we have
\phi_{n}\to\phi
with respect to the metric on
H^{-1}(U)
(i.e., the one induced by the operator norm on the dual of
H^{1}(U)
). One can check that this topology is separable and metrizable.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the Borel
\sigma
-algebra for this topology on
\mathcal{X}
, and let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be the sub-
\sigma
-algebra generated by Borel measurable functions
F:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
F(D,\phi)=F(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\phi})
whenever
(D,\phi)\sim_{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{\phi})
, where
\sim_{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
is as in Definition
1.1
. For a random LQG surface
\mathcal{S}
, we define
\sigma(\mathcal{S})
to be the collection of events of the form
\{(D,\Phi)\in E\}
where
E\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
(D,\Phi)
is any embedding of
\mathcal{S}
. Informally, the information carried by
\sigma(\mathcal{S})
is precisely the values of the functions of
(D,\Phi)
which are invariant under LQG coordinate change.

More generally, this same approach allows us to define the
\sigma
-algebras generated by LQG surfaces decorated by IG fields, sets, and/or curves as in (
1.6
).

2.3
Unit boundary length LQG disk

We will not need to use the precise definition of the law
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
of the unit boundary length LQG disk, but we include one possible definition (which comes from
[
Cer21
, Corollary 1.2]
) for completeness.
See
[
DMS21
,
HRV18
,
AHS21
]
for alternative definitions.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
and let
\gamma\in(0,2)
satisfy
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}=1+6(\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma})^{2}
.
Let
h
be a Neumann GFF on
\mathbb{D}
normalized to have average zero on
\partial\mathbb{D}
and let

\widehat{\Phi}_{0}=h-\gamma\log|\cdot+\mathbbm{i}|-\gamma\log|\cdot-1|-\gamma\log|\cdot+1|.

Let
\widehat{\Phi}
be sampled from the law of
\widehat{\Phi}_{0}
weighted by
\nu_{\widehat{\Phi}_{0}}(\partial\mathbb{D})^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-2}/\mathbbm{E}[\nu_{\widehat{\Phi}_{0}}(\partial\mathbb{D})^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-2}]
where
\nu_{\widehat{\Phi}_{0}}
is the
\gamma
-LQG boundary length measure, and let
\Phi=\widehat{\Phi}-\frac{2}{\gamma}\log\nu_{\widehat{\Phi}}(\partial\mathbb{D})
. Then
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
is the law of the
\gamma
-LQG surface
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i})/{\sim_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}}
.

2.4
LQG metric

Let
D\subset\mathbbm{C}
be an open domain such that Brownian motion started at any point of
D
a.s. exits
D
in finite time. We say that a random generalized function
\Phi
on
D
is a
GFF plus a continuous function on
D
if there is a coupling of
\Phi
with a zero-boundary GFF
\Phi^{0}
on
D
such that a.s.
\Phi-\Phi^{0}
is a continuous function on
D
(with no assumption about the behavior of
\Phi-\Phi^{0}
near
\partial D
). We say that
\Phi
is a
free-boundary GFF plus a continuous function on
\overline{D}
if there is a coupling of
\Phi
with a free-boundary GFF
\Phi^{\mathrm{Free}}
on
D
such that a.s.
\Phi-\Phi^{\mathrm{Free}}
is a continuous function on
\overline{D}
. It is clear from Definition
2.2
that if
\Phi
is an embedding of the LQG disk, then
\Phi
is a free-boundary GFF plus a continuous function plus finitely many functions of the form
\gamma\log|\cdot-z|
, for
z\in\partial D
.

Let
D\subset\mathbbm{C}
, and let
\Phi
be a GFF plus a continuous function on
D
. For each
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
, there is a unique (up to deterministic multiplicative constant) metric
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}:D\times D\to[0,\infty)
(i.e., a measurable assignment
\Phi\mapsto\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
) satisfying a list of natural axioms
[
DDDF20
,
GM21c
]
, called the
LQG metric
. Heuristically,
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}(z,w)
corresponds to the infimum over paths from
z
to
w
of “the integral of
e^{\xi({\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}})\Phi}
along the path”, where
\xi({\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}})>0
is a constant defined in terms of the fractal dimension of
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
-LQG. See
[
DDG21
]
for a survey of the LQG metric and its properties. The LQG metric can also be defined for
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}\in(1,25]

[
DG23a
,
DG23b
]
, but we will not need this case here.

We will now review the basic properties of
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
which we will need in this paper.

Topology.
Almost surely, the LQG metric
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
induces the Euclidean topology on
D
. Furthermore, a.s.
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
is a length metric, i.e.,
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}(z,w)
is the infimum of the
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
-lengths of paths joining
z
and
w

[
GM21c
, Axiom I]
.

Coordinate change.
If
f:D\to\widetilde{D}
is a conformal map and
\Phi=\widetilde{\Phi}\circ f+Q\log|f^{\prime}|
, then
[
GM21b
]
almost surely

\mathfrak{d}_{\widetilde{\Phi}}(f(z),f(w))=\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}(z,w),\quad\forall z,w\in D.

(2.1)

Locality.
For any open set
U\subset D
, the internal metric
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\cdot;U)
is the metric on
U
defined by

\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}(z,w;U):=\inf\mathrm{len}(P;\mathfrak{d}_{\phi}),\quad\forall z,w\in U

(2.2)

where the infimum is taken over paths
P
from
z
to
w
in
U
and
\mathrm{len}(P;\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})
denotes the
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
-length of
P
. Note that
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}(z,w;U)\geq\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}(z,w)
.
We will frequently use the locality property of
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}

[
GM21c
, Axiom II]
: for any deterministic open set
U\subset D
, the internal metric
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\cdot;U)
is a.s. given by a measurable function of
\Phi|_{U}
.
In particular, for
z\in U
and
r>0
the event
\{\mathcal{B}_{r}(z;\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})\subset U\}
is measurable with respect to
\sigma(\Phi|_{U})
, and
on this event the set
\mathcal{B}_{r}(z;\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})
is measurable with respect to
\sigma(\Phi|_{U})
.

Boundary extension.

When
\Phi
is a free-boundary GFF plus a continuous function on
\overline{D}
, the LQG metric
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
extends by continuity to a metric
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}:\overline{D}\times\overline{D}\to[0,\infty)
which induces the Euclidean topology on
\overline{D}

[
HM22
, Proposition 1.6]
. The same is true if we add finitely many functions of the form
\alpha\log|\cdot-z|
for
\alpha<Q
and distinct deterministic points
z\in\partial\mathbbm{D}
. In particular, the LQG metric associated with an embedding of the quantum disk extends to a metric on
\overline{D}
which induces the Euclidean topology. It is immediate that for this extended definition of
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
, the locality property stated above still holds for relatively open
U\subset\overline{D}
.

2.5
SLE and imaginary geometry

For
\rho_{L},\rho_{R}>-2
, there is a natural variant of
\SLE_{\kappa}
called
\SLE_{\kappa}(\rho_{L};\rho_{R})

[
LSW03
,
Dub09a
,
MS16a
]
which is locally absolutely continuous with respect to
\SLE_{\kappa}
away from the boundaries. It keeps track of two force points, which we will always assume are immediately to the left and right of the starting point of the curve.
\SLE_{\kappa}(\rho_{L};\rho_{R})
hits the left boundary if and only if
\rho_{L}<\frac{\kappa}{2}-2
, and similarly for the right boundary.

Imaginary geometry
[
MS16a
,
MS16b
,
MS16c
,
MS17
]
studies SLE via its coupling with IG fields, building on
[
Dub09b
]
. We only discuss a few special cases of the general theory here. Let
\kappa\neq 4,{\mathbf{c}}={\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}(\kappa)
and let
\rho_{L},\rho_{R}>-2
. Let
\Psi
be a zero boundary GFF in
\mathbb{H}
plus the harmonic function with boundary values
-\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa}}(1+\rho_{L})
on
(-\infty,0)
and
\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa}}(1+\rho_{R})
on
(0,\infty)
. Then there is a random curve
\eta
in
\overline{\mathbb{H}}
measurable with respect to
\Psi
whose marginal law is
\SLE_{\kappa}(\rho_{L};\rho_{R})
, such that conditioned on
\eta
, the restrictions of
\Psi
to the connected components
U
of
\mathbb{H}\backslash\eta
are conditionally independent. The conditional law of
\Psi|_{U}
is that of a zero boundary GFF plus a harmonic function with boundary conditions on
\partial U\cap\mathbb{R}
as above and boundary conditions on
\partial U\cap\eta
given by a constant plus
-\chi({\mathbf{c}})\arg f^{\prime}
where
f:U\to\mathbb{H}
is a suitable conformal map.
The boundary conditions on
\eta
are measurable with respect to
\eta
and are compatible with
{\mathbf{c}}
-IG coordinate change (Definition
1.3
).

In this coupling,
\eta
is a
local set

[
SS13
, Section 3.3]
of
\Psi
: for any relatively open set
U\subset\overline{\mathbb{H}}
containing neighborhoods of
0
and
\infty
, the event
\{\eta\subset U\}
is measurable with respect to
\Psi|_{U}
, and on this event
\eta
is measurable with respect to
\Psi|_{U}
.

When
\kappa<4
, the curve
\eta
is called a
flow line
of
\Psi
, and similarly we say an
\SLE_{\kappa}(\rho_{L};\rho_{R})
curve is a flow line of angle
\theta
if it is a flow line of
\Psi+\theta\chi({\mathbf{c}})
. When
\kappa>4
these curves are instead called
counterflow lines
4
4

4

By convention, in the above coupling
\eta
is a counterflow line of
-\Psi
rather than
\Psi
.
. Flow lines can also be started at interior points
z\in\mathbb{H}
and run until they hit
\partial\mathbb{H}
, and satisfy properties analogous to those stated above
[
MS17
]
. The case
\kappa=4
(
\chi=0
) falls outside the scope of the imaginary geometry framework, but the above statements for chordal
\eta
still hold, and
\eta
is called a
level line

[
SS13
,
WW17
]
.

For
\kappa>4
, given a zero boundary GFF
\Psi
in
\mathbb{H}
there is a counterclockwise space-filling
\SLE_{\kappa}
loop based at
\infty
measurable with respect to
\Psi
defined as follows. For each point with rational coordinates
z\in\mathbbm{Q}^{2}\cap\mathbb{H}
, let
\eta^{z,L}
and
\eta^{z,R}
be the flow lines of
\Psi-\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa}}
started at
z
with angles
\theta^{L}=-\frac{\pi}{2}
and
\theta^{R}=\frac{\pi}{2}
respectively. We define a total order
\preceq
on
\mathbbm{Q}^{2}\cap\mathbb{H}
by saying
z\preceq w
if
z
lies in a connected component of
\mathbbm{H}\backslash(\eta^{w,L}\cup\eta^{w,R})
whose boundary is traced by the left side of
\eta^{w,L}
and the right side of
\eta^{w,R}
.
This ordering is well defined due to properties of interacting flow lines, and gives rise to a space-filling loop in
\mathbbm{H}
. See
[
BG22a
, Appendix A.3]
for details.
Since this coupling is constructed via the flow lines of
\Psi
viewed as a
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}(\kappa)
-IG field, in any simply connected domain with one boundary point we have a coupling of the counterclockwise space-filling
\SLE_{\kappa}
loop with
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{SLE}}(\kappa)}
.

Let
\eta
be a counterclockwise space-filling
\SLE_{\kappa}
loop in
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
, and let
z,z^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}
be distinct points chosen independently of
\eta
. Let
a<b
be the two times when
\eta
hits
\{z,z^{\prime}\}
. When
\kappa\geq 8
, a.s. the interior of
\eta([a,b])
is simply connected. On the other hand, when
\kappa\in(4,8)
, a.s. the interior of
\eta([a,b])
has countably many connected components.
See Figure
4
.

2.6
Total curvature and winding

We recall the notion of total curvature, also known as winding in some probability literature such as
[
MS16a
]
. This will be used in Section
2.7
to make sense of LQG / IG coordinate change as in Definitions
1.3
and
1.5
for domains which are not necessarily connected, and in Section
4.5
for the main combinatorial argument of the paper (see also Appendix
B
).

Suppose
\eta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
is a regular curve, meaning it is continuously differentiable with nonvanishing derivative. Let
\theta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be the unique continuous function such that
\theta(0)\in[0,2\pi)
and
e^{-\mathbbm{i}\theta(t)}\eta^{\prime}(t)>0
for each
t\in[0,1]
. The
total curvature
of
\eta
is defined to be
\theta(1)-\theta(0)
, and more generally we define the total curvature of
\eta|_{[t,t^{\prime}]}
to be
\theta(t^{\prime})-\theta(t)
. This name comes from the fact that when
\eta
is twice-differentiable, if
\widehat{\eta}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{C}
is the parametrization of
\eta
according to arc length and
k(t)=\frac{\widehat{\eta}^{\prime\prime}(t)}{\mathbbm{i}\widehat{\eta}^{\prime}(t)}\in\mathbb{R}
is its signed curvature, then

\theta(1)-\theta(0)=\int_{0}^{T}k(t)\,dt.

A regular simple closed curve
\eta:[0,T]\to\mathbb{C}
is a simple loop (
\eta(0)=\eta(T)
) with a continuous nonvanishing derivative such that
\eta^{\prime}(0)=\eta^{\prime}(T)
. The following classical fact is often called
Hopf’s Umlaufsatz
, see e.g.
[
Tu17
, Theorem 17.4]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The total curvature of a regular simple closed curve is
\pm 2\pi
, with the
+
sign if the loop is oriented in the counterclockwise direction and
-
sign otherwise.

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}
are regular simple curves such that for some
\varepsilon>0
we have
\eta_{0}|_{[0,\varepsilon]}=\eta_{1}|_{[0,\varepsilon]}
and
\eta_{0}|_{[1-\varepsilon,1]}=\eta_{1}|_{[1-\varepsilon,1]}
, and
\eta_{0}|_{[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]}
and
\eta_{1}|_{[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]}
are homotopic in the twice-slitted domain
\mathbb{C}\backslash(\eta_{0}([0,\varepsilon])\cup\eta_{0}([1-\varepsilon,1]))
. Then the total curvatures of
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}
agree.

Proof.

Let
\eta
be a simple regular curve from
\eta_{0}(1)
to
\eta_{0}(0)
which concatenates with
\eta_{0}
to form a regular simple closed curve. By Lemma
2.3
, the concatenation has total curvature
\pm 2\pi
with sign depending on the orientation of the loop. Thus, if such an
\eta
can be chosen such that the loop obtained by concatenating
\eta
with
\eta_{1}
is also simple, then
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}
have the same total curvature, as needed.

We now address the general case where no such
\eta
can be chosen. Our homotopy hypothesis implies that there exists
\varepsilon>0
and a homotopy of
simple
curves
(\eta_{t})_{[0,1]}
from
\eta_{0}
to
\eta_{1}
such that
\eta_{t}|_{[0,\varepsilon]}
and
\eta_{t}|_{[1-\varepsilon,1]}
are continuously differentiable and do not depend on
t\in[0,1]
, see e.g.
[
FM11
, Section 1.2.7]
for details on why we can take the curves to be simple.
Pick a finite set of times
0=t_{0}<\cdots<t_{k}=1
such that for each
j=1,\dots,k
the points
\eta_{0}(0)
and
\eta_{0}(1)
lie on the boundary of the same connected component of
\mathbb{C}\backslash(\eta_{t_{j-1}}([0,1])\cup\eta_{t_{j}}([0,1]))
. Let
\widetilde{\eta}_{0}=\eta_{0}
and
\widetilde{\eta}_{k}=\eta_{1}
. For
j=1,\dots,k-1
let
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}
be a simple regular curve such that
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}|_{[0,\varepsilon]}=\eta_{0}|_{[0,\varepsilon]}
and
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}|_{[1-\varepsilon,1]}=\eta_{0}|_{[1-\varepsilon,1]}
for all
j
, and
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}|_{[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]}
is homotopic to
\eta_{0}|_{[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]}
in
\mathbb{C}\backslash(\eta_{0}([0,\varepsilon])\cup\eta_{0}([1-\varepsilon,1]))
. Moreover we assume
\eta_{0}(0)
and
\eta_{0}(1)
lie on the boundary of the same connected component of
\mathbb{C}\backslash(\widetilde{\eta}_{j-1}([0,1])\cup\widetilde{\eta}_{j}([0,1]))
for
j=1,\dots,k
. Such curves
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}
can be chosen by picking simple regular curves that stay sufficiently close to
\eta_{t_{j}}
in the Hausdorff topology for each
j=1,\dots,k-1
. By the first paragraph, the total curvatures of
\widetilde{\eta}_{j-1}
and
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}
agree for
j=1,\dots,k
, and hence the total curvatures of
\widetilde{\eta}_{0}=\eta_{0}
and
\widetilde{\eta}_{k}=\eta_{1}
agree, as needed.
∎

By Lemma
2.4
, the following definition makes sense.

Definition 2.5
.

Suppose
\eta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
is a (possibly non-smooth) simple curve such that
\eta|_{[0,\varepsilon]}
and
\eta|_{[1-\varepsilon,1]}
are regular for some
\varepsilon>0
. Define the total curvature of
\eta
to be that of any regular simple curve
\eta^{\mathrm{reg}}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\eta|_{[0,\varepsilon]}=\eta^{\mathrm{reg}}|_{[0,\varepsilon]}
and
\eta|_{[1-\varepsilon,1]}=\eta^{\mathrm{reg}}|_{[1-\varepsilon,1]}
, and
\eta|_{[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]}
and
\eta^{\mathrm{reg}}|_{[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]}
are homotopic in the twice-slitted domain
\mathbb{C}\backslash(\eta([0,\varepsilon])\cup\eta([1-\varepsilon,1]))
.

2.7
IG coordinate change for general domains

Suppose
D
and
\widetilde{D}
are bounded open subsets of
\mathbb{C}
and
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
is a homeomorphism with
f(D)=\widetilde{D}
which is conformal on
D
. When
D
is simply connected (which in particular means that it is connected), one can define
\arg f^{\prime}:D\to\mathbb{R}
uniquely up to an additive constant in
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. In this section we extend the definition of
\arg f^{\prime}
to general
D
. Roughly speaking the condition that
f
is a homeomorphism rules out the possibility of “spinning” a connected component an arbitrary number of times. This ensures that the definition of an IG surface (Definition
1.3
) makes sense even if
D
is not simply connected, or even connected.

Here is the extended definition of
\arg f^{\prime}:D\to\mathbb{R}
. First, fix any point
z_{0}\in D
and define
(\arg f^{\prime})(z_{0})
to be any real
\theta
satisfying
f^{\prime}(z_{0})e^{-\mathbbm{i}\theta}>0
; this specifies it up to an element of
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. For
z\in D\backslash\{z_{0}\}
, pick a curve
\eta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
with
\eta(0)=z_{0}
and
\eta(1)=z
and which is regular in neighborhoods of its endpoints. Let
\widetilde{\eta}=f\circ\eta
, then we define

(\arg f^{\prime})(z)=(\arg f^{\prime})(z_{0})+w(\widetilde{\eta})-w(\eta),

(2.3)

where
w
denotes total curvature as in Definition
2.5
. The following result states that this definition is valid. It uses Lemmas
2.8
and
2.9
which we state and prove at the end of this section.

Proposition 2.6
.

The above definition of
\arg f^{\prime}:D\to\mathbb{R}
does not depend on the choices of
z_{0}
and
\eta
, and agrees with the usual definition of
\arg f^{\prime}
when
D
is simply connected.

Proof.

Agreement with the usual definition in the simply connected setting follows from Lemma
2.8
. The non-dependence on
\eta
is stated as Lemma
2.9
. For different choices of base point
z_{0}
and
z_{0}^{\prime}
, the consistency of the above definition can be shown by concatenating with a curve from
z_{0}^{\prime}
to
z_{0}
.
∎

We now use (
2.3
) to explain how total curvature relates to IG boundary conditions.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}\leq 1
.
Suppose
D\subset\mathbb{C}
is a simply connected domain whose boundary is a regular simple closed curve, and let
x\in\partial D
. Let
\theta_{0}\in[0,2\pi)
be the angle such that the tangent to
D
at
x
in the counterclockwise direction is parallel to
e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta_{0}}
. Let
\mathfrak{h}_{D}:D\to\mathbb{R}
be the harmonic function whose boundary value at
y\neq x
is the total curvature of the counterclockwise boundary arc from
x
to
y
. If
h
is a zero boundary GFF in
D
, then the law of
(D,h+\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}})(\mathfrak{h}_{D}+\theta_{0}),x)/{\sim_{\mathbf{c}}}
is
\mathrm{IG}_{{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}}
.

Proof.

Recall the harmonic function
\mathfrak{h}:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}
in Definition
1.7
whose boundary value at
y\in\partial\mathbb{D}
is the total curvature of the counterclockwise boundary arc from
-\mathbbm{i}
to
y
.
Let
f:\mathbb{D}\to D
be a conformal map sending
-\mathbbm{i}\mapsto x
. By the definition of an IG surface (Definition
1.3
), we must show that

\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}})\mathfrak{h}_{D}\circ f+\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}})\theta_{0}-\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}})\arg f^{\prime}=\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}})(\mathfrak{h}+2\pi m)

for some integer
m
. Since both functions are harmonic and
\arg f^{\prime}(-\mathbbm{i})-\theta_{0}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, it suffices to show
\arg f^{\prime}(y)=\arg f^{\prime}(-\mathbbm{i})+\mathfrak{h}_{D}(f(y))-\mathfrak{h}(y)
for all
y\in\partial\mathbb{D}
with
y\neq-\mathbbm{i}
. Let
\eta
(resp.
\widetilde{\eta}
) be the counterclockwise boundary arc of
\mathbb{D}
from
-\mathbbm{i}
to
y
(resp. arc of
D
from
x
to
f(y)
). Since
\mathfrak{h}(y)
(resp.
\mathfrak{h}_{D}(f(y))
) is the total curvature of
\eta
(resp.
\widetilde{\eta}
), the claim would follow from (
2.3
) if that equation applied to the curves
\eta
and
\widetilde{\eta}
. Instead, we approximate
\eta
and
\widetilde{\eta}
by smooth curves in
\mathbb{D}
and
D
, where the approximation is with respect to the supremum norm for the curve and its first derivative, apply (
2.3
), and take a limit to conclude.
∎

In the rest of this section, we state and prove the lemmas needed for the proof of Proposition
2.6
above. Lemma
2.8
is also stated in
[
BLR20
, Remark 2.5]
; we give its proof here for completeness.

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose
D
is a bounded simply connected open set and let
\arg_{0}f^{\prime}:D\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function such that
f^{\prime}(z)e^{-\mathbbm{i}(\arg_{0}f^{\prime})(z)}>0
for all
z\in D
. Let
\eta:[0,1]\to D
be a regular curve (not necessarily simple) and
\widetilde{\eta}=f\circ\eta
. Then
(\arg_{0}f^{\prime})(\eta(1))=(\arg_{0}f^{\prime})(\eta(0))+w(\widetilde{\eta})-w(\eta)
.

Proof.

Let
\theta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function such that
\eta^{\prime}(t)e^{-\mathbbm{i}\theta(t)}>0
for all
t
, and similarly define
\widetilde{\theta}
for
\widetilde{\eta}
. Since
\widetilde{\eta}=f\circ\eta
we have
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}(t)=f^{\prime}(\eta(t))\eta^{\prime}(t)
, so
