\widetilde{\theta}(t)
and
(\arg_{0}f^{\prime})(\eta(t))+\theta(t)
agree up to an additive integer multiple of
2\pi
. The function
\widetilde{\theta}(t)-(\arg_{0}f^{\prime})(\eta(t))-\theta(t)
is continuous in
t
and takes values in
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, thus is constant. Identifying its values at
0
and
1
gives the result.
∎

In Lemma
2.9
below we do not assume
D
is simply connected.

Lemma 2.9
.

For
j=1,2
let
\eta_{j}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
be a simple curve which is regular in neighborhoods of its endpoints. Suppose
\eta_{1}(0)=\eta_{2}(0)
and
\eta_{2}(1)=\eta_{2}(1)
, and these endpoints lie in
D
. Let
\widetilde{\eta}_{j}=f\circ\eta_{j}
for
j=1,2
. Then
w(\widetilde{\eta}_{1})-w(\eta_{1})=w(\widetilde{\eta}_{2})-w(\eta_{2})
.

Proof.

Call an intersection point of two curve segments
generic
if at that point a curve crosses from one side to the other side of the other curve.
For
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
as in Lemma
2.9
, we use the notation
\eta_{1}\perp\eta_{2}
to mean that
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
intersect only finitely many times, each intersection is generic, the tangent vectors of
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
at their starting point are not parallel, and similarly their tangent vectors at their ending point are not parallel.

We first prove the result under the assumption that
\eta_{1}\perp\eta_{2}
. Let
p=\eta_{j}(0)
and
q=\eta_{j}(1)
, and let
\eta_{2,r}(t)=\eta_{2}(1-t)
be the time-reversal of
\eta_{2}
.
Let
\eta_{p}
be a regular curve which intersects itself only at its endpoints
\eta_{p}(0)=\eta_{p}(1)=p
, is disjoint from
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
, lies in a simply connected neighborhood of
p
in
D
, and satisfies
\eta_{2,r}^{\prime}(1)=\eta_{p}^{\prime}(0)
and
\eta_{p}^{\prime}(1)=\eta_{1}^{\prime}(0)
.
Let
\eta_{q}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
be a regular curve which intersects itself only at its endpoints
\eta_{q}(0)=\eta_{q}(1)=q
, is disjoint from
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
, lies in a simply connected neighborhood of
q
in
D
, and satisfies
\eta_{1}^{\prime}(1)=\eta_{q}^{\prime}(0)
and
\eta_{q}^{\prime}(1)=\eta_{2,r}^{\prime}(0)
. See Figure
3
.

Then
\eta:[0,4]\to\mathbb{C}
obtained by concatenating
\eta_{p},\eta_{1},\eta_{q},\eta_{2,r}
is a loop which intersects itself finitely many times, all generically. By standard topological arguments, there exists a homeomorphism
g:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
fixing simply connected neighborhoods of
\eta_{p}
and
\eta_{q}
in
D
, which sends
\eta
to a regular loop
\eta^{\mathrm{reg}}=g\circ\eta
which intersects itself finitely many times, each intersection being transversal.
Decompose the loop
\eta^{\mathrm{reg}}
into four segments
\eta_{p},\eta_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}},\eta_{q},\eta_{2,r}^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

Let
\widetilde{\eta}_{p}=f\circ\eta_{p},\widetilde{\eta}_{q}=f\circ\eta_{q},
and
\widetilde{\eta}=f\circ\eta
. Let
\widetilde{g}
be a homeomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
fixing simply connected neighborhoods of
\widetilde{\eta}_{p}
and
\widetilde{\eta}_{q}
in
\widetilde{D}
, and sending
\widetilde{\eta}
to a regular simple closed curve
\widetilde{\eta}^{\mathrm{reg}}
with finitely many self-intersections, all of which are transversal. Decompose this closed curve into four segments

\widetilde{\eta}_{p},\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}},\widetilde{\eta}_{q},\widetilde{\eta}_{2,r}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Since the orientation-preserving homeomorphism
\widetilde{g}\circ f\circ g^{-1}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
sends
\eta^{\mathrm{reg}}
to
\widetilde{\eta}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, and these regular loops only intersect themselves finitely many times, each intersection being transversal and a double-point,
by
[
Whi37
]
the total curvatures of
\widetilde{\eta}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\eta^{\mathrm{reg}}
agree
5
5

5

[
Whi37
, Theorem 2]
gives a formula for the total curvature of a loop which is invariant under orientation-preserving homeomorphism, see e.g.
[
BP11
, Theorem 2]
for the particular formulation we use.

.
In other words,

w(\widetilde{\eta}_{p})+w(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}})+w(\widetilde{\eta}_{q})+w(\widetilde{\eta}_{2,r}^{\mathrm{reg}})=w(\eta_{p})+w(\eta_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}})+w(\eta_{q})+w(\eta_{2,r}^{\mathrm{reg}}).

By Lemma
2.8
we have
w(\widetilde{\eta}_{p})=w(\eta_{p})
and
w(\widetilde{\eta}_{q})=w(\eta_{q})
, so

w(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}})-w(\eta_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}})=w(\eta_{2,r}^{\mathrm{reg}})-w(\widetilde{\eta}_{2,r}^{\mathrm{reg}})
. Applying Definition
2.5
gives
w(\widetilde{\eta}_{1})-w(\eta_{1})=w(\eta_{2,r})-w(\widetilde{\eta}_{2,r})=w(\widetilde{\eta}_{2})-w(\eta_{2})
as desired.

Now we address the case where we do not assume
\eta_{1}\perp\eta_{2}
. By Lemma
2.10
just below, we can find a third curve
\eta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\eta_{1}\perp\eta
and
\eta_{2}\perp\eta
. Then the previous paragraph implies
w(\widetilde{\eta}_{1})-w(\eta_{1})=w(f\circ\eta)-w(\eta)=w(\widetilde{\eta}_{2})-w(\eta_{2})
as needed.
∎

Figure 3:

Figure for the proof of Lemma
2.9
. We introduce curves
\eta_{p}
and
\eta_{q}
in simply connected neighborhoods of
p
and
q
(gray) which join with
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2,r}
to yield a loop
\eta
whose restriction to these neighborhoods is regular. Let
g:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
be a homeomorphism fixing these neighborhoods such that
g\circ\eta
is regular. One can similarly choose
\widetilde{g}
for the loop
f\circ\eta
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\perp
be as defined in the proof of Lemma
2.9
. For any curves
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
as in Lemma
2.9
, there exists a third curve
\eta
such that
\eta_{1}\perp\eta
and
\eta_{2}\perp\eta
.

Proof.

By applying a suitable homeomorphism if necessary, we may assume that
\eta_{1}
is a line segment.

We first choose the initial and ending segments of
\eta
. Let
\eta|_{[0,1/3]}
be a line segment with
\eta(0)=\eta_{1}(0)
such that
\eta_{1}^{\prime}(0)
and
\eta_{2}^{\prime}(0)
are not parallel to
\eta^{\prime}(0)
, and
\eta((0,1/3])
is disjoint from
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
. This is possible since
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are regular in neighborhoods of their starting points.
Similarly, let
\eta|_{[2/3,1]}
be a line segment ending at
\eta_{1}(1)
such that
\eta_{1}^{\prime}(1)
and
\eta_{2}^{\prime}(1)
are not parallel to
\eta^{\prime}(1)
, and
\eta([2/3,1))
is disjoint from
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
.

Next, we say an interval
[a,b]
is
separating
if
\eta_{2}(a)
and
\eta_{2}(b)
each lie on
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}((a,b))
is disjoint from
\eta_{1}
, and the loop obtained as the union of
\eta_{2}([a,b])
and the segment of
\eta_{1}
joining
\eta_{2}(a)
and
\eta_{2}(b)
separates
\eta((0,1/3])
from
\eta([2/3,1))
.
If
[a,b]
is separating, then
\mathrm{diam}(\eta_{2}([a,b]))>\min(\mathrm{diam}(\eta((0,1/3])),\mathrm{diam}(\eta([2/3,1))))
. Since
[0,1]
is compact and
\eta_{2}
is continuous, the curve
\eta_{2}
is uniformly continuous, thus the diameter lower bound implies there are at most finitely many separating intervals. The initial and ending segments of
\eta
can then be extended to a simple curve
\eta:[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\eta((0,1))
is disjoint from
\eta_{1}
and crosses
\eta_{2}
once for each separating interval. This is the desired curve.
∎

3
Proofs of main theorems

In this section, we prove our most general result Theorem
3.3
on the LQG-IG local sets of Definition
3.1
. The main theorems stated in Section
1.3
are all straightforward consequences of Theorem
3.3
and its minor variant Theorem
3.19
.

First, we give the definition of an LQG-IG local set.
This is an analog of the definition of a local set of the GFF
[
SS13
, Section 3]
in the setting of LQG surfaces decorated by imaginary geometry fields.
Roughly speaking, a random closed set
K
is an LQG-IG local set of an IG-decorated LQG surface
\mathcal{S}
if, for “any” IG-decorated LQG subsurface
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{S}
chosen conditionally independently of
K
given
\mathcal{S}
, the event {
K
lies in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
} is conditionally independent of
\mathcal{S}
given
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, and further conditioned on this event, the decorated LQG surface
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
further decorated by
K
is conditionally independent of
\mathcal{S}
. To make sense of a “random LQG sub-surface”
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, we will discretize our setup at scale
\varepsilon
and define
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local sets.

Let
\mathbf{c}_{L}>25
,
n\geq 1
and
\mathbf{c}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n}\leq 1
satisfy
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}+\mathbf{c}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{c}_{n}=26
. Let
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
(Definition
1.6
), and independently let
(\mathbb{D},\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{IG}_{({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}
(Definition
1.7
). Let
\eta^{\prime}
be a counterclockwise space-filling
\SLE
with central charge
(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}})
measurable with respect to
\Psi_{1}\dots,\Psi_{n}
in the following way. Let
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{1}=26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{2}=\cdots=\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{n}=0
, and let
\chi_{i}=\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{i})
and
\widehat{\chi}_{i}=\chi(\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{i})
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Let
A
be an orthogonal matrix which maps
(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{n})
to
(\widehat{\chi}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\chi}_{n})
, and let
(\widehat{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\Psi}_{n})
be the image of
(\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
under
A
as in (
1.11
), so by Proposition
1.17
the law of
(\widehat{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\Psi}_{n})
is
\mathrm{IG}_{(\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\mathbf{c}}_{n})}
. Let
\eta^{\prime}
be the counterclockwise space-filling SLE curve coupled with
\widehat{\Psi}_{1}
as in Section
2
.

In Definition
3.1
, we will discretize the above setup
using a finite set of points
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{D}
independent of
\eta^{\prime}
, chosen using the LQG area measure
\mu_{\Phi}
. The curve
\eta^{\prime}
is split by the points of
\Lambda
into
|\Lambda|+1
curve segments which we call
\eta^{\prime}_{0},\dots,\eta^{\prime}_{|\Lambda|}
; each
\eta^{\prime}_{i}
is defined on a time interval
[0,t_{i}]
. For
i=0,\dots,|\Lambda|
, let
D_{i}=\eta_{i}^{\prime}([0,t_{i}])
be the region traced out by
\eta_{i}^{\prime}
, let
a_{i}=\eta^{\prime}_{i}(0)
and
d_{i}=\eta_{i}^{\prime}(t_{i})
be the start and end points of
\eta_{i}^{\prime}
respectively, and let
b_{i}
(resp.
c_{i}
) be the furthest point on the clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) arc
6
6

6

For
\kappa^{\prime}\geq 8
the boundary of
\partial D_{i}
is simple. For
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
, the clockwise arc of
\partial D_{i}
from
a_{i}
to
d_{i}
is a nonsimple curve, constructed by ordering the points of
\partial D_{i}
hit by the left side of
\eta^{\prime}_{i}
according to the last time they are hit.
of
\partial D_{i}
from
a_{i}
to
d_{i}
such that the boundary arc from
a_{i}
to
b_{i}
(resp.
c_{i}
) is a subset of
\overline{(\partial\mathbb{D}\cup\bigcup_{j\leq i}D_{i})}
.
See Figure
4
.

Figure 4:

Let
\kappa>4
satisfy
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}(\kappa)=26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
. In each figure the space-filling
\SLE_{\kappa}
loop
\eta^{\prime}
traces three
D_{i}
regions in the order yellow, green, blue. The points of
\Lambda
are shown in black. In particular
a_{i}
and
d_{i}
are black, whereas
b_{i}
and
c_{i}
are shown in color. The left boundaries of the
D_{i}
(clockwise boundary arcs from
a_{i}
to
d_{i}
) are red, while the right boundaries are black.

Left:
When
\kappa\geq 8
, each of the
D_{i}
is simply connected.
Right:
When
\kappa\in(4,8)
, each
D_{i}
has countably many components, and arises from a sequence of simply-connected regions ordered by the time they are traced by
\eta^{\prime}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
.
An
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set

K\subset\overline{\mathbb{D}}
is a random compact set coupled with
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime})
such that
K\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
is a.s. connected, and
the following holds. See Figure
5
(left).

Independently of
(\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},K,\eta^{\prime})
,
sample a Poisson point process
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{D}
with intensity measure
\varepsilon^{-1}\mu_{\Phi}
and define
D_{i},\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}
as above. Independently of everything else sample a nonnegative integer
T\geq 0
with
\mathbb{P}[T=t]=2^{-t-1}
. Let
I_{0}
be the set of indices
i
such that
D_{i}\cap\partial\mathbb{D}\neq\emptyset
, and for
j\geq 0
inductively define
I_{j+1}
from
I_{j}
by independently sampling a point
p_{j+1}\in(\partial\bigcup_{i\in I_{j}}D_{i})\backslash\partial\mathbb{D}
from the quantum length measure, then setting
I_{j+1}=I_{j}\cup\{i\>:\>p_{j+1}\in\partial D_{i}\}
. We stop the process either at time
T
or at the time
t
when
\bigcup_{i\in I_{t}}D_{i}=\overline{\mathbb{D}}
, whichever is earlier; call this time
\tau
.

Conditioned on
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\{(\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}):i\in I_{\tau}\},\Lambda\cap\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i})/{\sim}
, the event
\{K\subset\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime},\Lambda)
.
Moreover, further conditioning on
\{K\subset\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
, the decorated LQG surface
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,K)/{\sim}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime},\Lambda)
.

The procedure of Definition
3.1
can be viewed as a semi-continuous analog of
peeling
for random planar maps (see, e.g.,
[
Ang05
]
). Indeed, let
\mathcal{M}_{t}
be the decorated LQG sub-surface parametrized by
\bigcup_{i\in I_{t}}D_{t}
, then if
t<\tau
, we explore from a point on
\partial\mathcal{M}_{t}
chosen in a way only depending on
\mathcal{M}_{t}
to obtain
\mathcal{M}_{t+1}
.

In Definition
3.1
, we could equivalently replace
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,K)/{\sim}
with

(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\{(\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}):i\in I_{\tau}\},\Lambda\cap\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},K)/{\sim},

since the latter decorated LQG surface is determined by
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,K)/{\sim}
and

(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\{(\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}):i\in I_{\tau}\},\Lambda\cap\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i})/{\sim}.

Definition
3.1
is closely related to the definition of local sets of the GFF given in
[
SS13
, Lemma 3.9]
but more complicated since we consider LQG sub-surfaces rather than planar domains. Roughly speaking, replacing the deterministic open sets in their definition of GFF local sets with the randomly chosen LQG surface
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\{(\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}):i\in I_{\tau}\},\Lambda)/{\sim}
gives Definition
3.1
.

We emphasize that in the above definition, for the decorated LQG surface we condition on,
\{(\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}):i\in I_{\tau}\}
is an
unordered
set of tuples, and each tuple is not labelled by its index
i
.

Figure 5:

Combinatorially, the planar map structure depicted here cannot arise in our setup, but see Definition
4.7
for a description of the possible planar maps and Figures
8
and
9
(left) for examples.

Left.

Figure for Definition
3.1
. Using
\eta^{\prime}
and
\Lambda
, the disk
\mathbb{D}
is divided into regions
D_{i}
. A random domain
\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}
(gray) is explored independently of
K
(green). We say
K
is an
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set if, roughly speaking, the event
\{K\subset\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
is conditionally independent of the unexplored decorated LQG surfaces (white) given the explored decorated LQG surface (gray, not decorated by
K
), and further conditioning on
\{K\subset\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
, the explored decorated LQG surface further decorated by
K
is conditionally independent of the unexplored decorated LQG surfaces.

Right.

Figure for Proposition
3.5
.
The explored LQG surface
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
is depicted in gray. Assuming there is only one IG field, and the loops
L_{i}
(red) on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
are chosen in a way measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
, Proposition
3.5
says the encircled IG field-decorated LQG surfaces are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
.

Definition 3.2
.

A random compact set
K\subset\overline{\mathbb{D}}
coupled with
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime})
is called an
LQG-IG local set
if it is an
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Examples of LQG-IG local sets include flow lines of
\Psi_{1}
,
\SLE_{6}
, Brownian motion, and
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}
-metric balls run until stopping times intrinsic to the decorated LQG surface. See the proofs of Theorems
1.9
,
1.13
and
1.14
in Section
3.4
for details.

The following conditional independence result is the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.3
.

Suppose
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime},K)
are as in Definition
3.2
. Let
\mathcal{F}=\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\sigma((\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}),\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},K,-\mathbbm{i})/{\sim})
. Then the decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
parametrized by the connected components
U
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash K
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}
.

There is a variant of Theorem
3.3
for
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
(Theorem
3.19
) whose statement we defer to Section
3.4
to avoid notational confusion. Its proof is essentially the same as that of Theorem
3.3
.

Remark 3.4
.

Definition
3.1
gives a discretization of SLE-decorated LQG via a Poisson point process of intensity
\varepsilon^{-1}\mu_{\Phi}
.
A related discretization where
\eta^{\prime}
is segmented such that each
D_{i}
has fixed LQG area is used to motivate and analyze the
mated-CRT map

[
DMS21
,
GHS19
,
GMS21
]
. The Poissonian formulation simplifies our arguments due to its locality property: the restrictions of a Poisson point process to fixed subdomains are independent.

In Sections
3.1
–
3.3
we carry out the three steps described in the introduction to prove Theorem
3.3
.
In Section
3.1
we state Proposition
3.5
which is a special case of Theorem
3.3
where
n=1
and
K
is a particular set defined in terms of mating of trees; its proof is deferred to Section
4
.
In Section
3.2
we use the locality of
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}=0}
fields to extend to the
n\geq 1
setting where all but one IG field has central charge 0. Finally, in Section
3.3
we use Proposition
1.17
to generalize to the setting of arbitrary central charges, completing the proof of Theorem
3.3
. In Section
3.4
we prove Theorem
3.19
, and deduce the results stated in the introduction from Theorems
3.3
and
3.19
.

3.1
An
n=1
special case via mating-of-trees

In Definition
3.1
with
n=1
, we independently sample an LQG surface from
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
and an IG field from
\mathrm{IG}_{26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
, discretize this IG-decorated LQG surface in a Poissonian way, and discover a random decorated LQG sub-surface via a peeling process.
Proposition
3.5
below states that given this explored LQG sub-surface, its complementary LQG surfaces are conditionally independent, where “complementary LQG surface” is appropriately defined. See Figure
5
(right).

Proposition 3.5
.

Consider the setting of Definition
3.1
with
n=1
(so
\eta^{\prime}
is the counterclockwise space-filling SLE curve coupled with
\Psi_{1}
). Let
L_{1},\dots,L_{m}
be simple loops in the interior of
\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}
and for
k=1,\dots,m
let
O_{k}
be the bounded connected component of
\mathbb{C}\backslash L_{k}
. Suppose
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,L_{1},\dots,L_{m})/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}:=(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\{\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}):i\in I_{\tau}\},\Lambda)/{\sim}
, and the
O_{k}
are pairwise disjoint. Then conditioned on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
, the IG-decorated LQG surfaces
(O_{1},\Phi,\Psi)/{\sim},\dots,(O_{m},\Phi,\Psi)/{\sim}
are conditionally independent.

Remark 3.6
.

For
\gamma\in(0,\sqrt{2}]
, the proposition still holds if we simply define the
O_{i}
to be the complementary connected components of
\mathbb{D}\backslash M_{\tau}
.
For
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
, this simpler formulation runs into topological issues because the interior of each
D_{i}
has multiple components, hence
D_{i}
might intersect multiple connected components of
\mathbb{D}\backslash M_{\tau}
. While a suitable modification of this formulation is true, we prefer to avoid this issue altogether by using the loops
L_{i}
.

We defer the proof of Proposition
3.5
to Section
4
. The starting point is the mating-of-trees theorem for the LQG disk
[
DMS21
,
AG21
]
, which describes the independent coupling of
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
and
\mathrm{IG}_{26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
in terms of a pair of correlated one-dimensional Brownian motions. See Section
4.1
. A statement like Proposition
3.5
should then seem plausible due to the independent increments of Brownian motion; see Proposition
4.3
for a factorized description of the LQG surfaces
(D_{i},\Phi,\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})/{\sim}
coming from Brownian motion.

On the other hand, consider the planar map whose faces correspond to the
D_{i}
from Definition
3.1
; its precise definition is given in Section
4.3
(see Figure
9
(left)). This random planar map has the global condition that the faces must “chain together” to form a
single
path, corresponding to the order they are traced by the space-filling SLE loop. Further, adjacent faces (not necessarily consecutive) must intersect in a way which is compatible with their relative ordering in this path. See Definition
4.7
for details. As such, one could a priori expect complicated dependencies between disjoint regions of the map.
To deal with this difficulty, we show that conditioned on the random submap corresponding to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
in Proposition
3.5
, the set of possible complementary submaps factorizes as a product (Proposition
4.12
), i.e. the possible realizations of each submap do not depend on the other complementary submaps. We prove this in Section
4.5
via a topological/combinatorial argument involving
total curvature
, a.k.a.
winding
(Section
2.6
). In the context of imaginary geometry, winding is natural since it is related to the boundary conditions of flow and counterflow lines. We prove that the global conditions described above are equivalent to local conditions related to winding, and hence obtain the combinatorial factorization Proposition
4.12
.

Proposition
3.5
is proved by combining the probabilistic input of Proposition
4.3
with the combinatorial input of Proposition
4.12
.

A simpler version of the argument of Section
4.5
leads to a conditional independence statement for the “upper” and “lower” halves of a uniform meander given its associated winding function. We record this result and its proof in Appendix
B
, both to motivate the argument of Section
4.5
and because the result is of independent interest.

3.2
Extending to
(\mathbf{c}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n})=(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},0,\dots,0)
via locality of
\mathbf{c}=0
IG fields

In this section we build on Proposition
3.5
to prove Proposition
3.8
, a special case of Theorem
3.3
where all but one of the IG fields has central charge 0 and we approximate
K
by the union of the space-filling SLE segments
D_{i}
which it intersects rather than by
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K;{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})
. We first state the more general Proposition
3.7
whose proof we defer to Section
3.3
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. In the setting of Definition
3.1
, let
K
be an
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set coupled with
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime})
. Sample a Poisson point process
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{D}
with intensity measure
\varepsilon^{-1}\mu_{\Phi}
independently of
(\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime},K)
and define
D_{i},\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}
as in Definition
3.1
.
Then the decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
parametrized by the connected components
U
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash K
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
, where

\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}:=\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{\varepsilon},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\{(\eta^{\prime}_{i},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})\>:\>i\in I\},K)/{\sim}}}\right),

(3.1)

I=\{i\>:\>D_{i}\cap K\neq\emptyset\}
, and
K_{\varepsilon}=\bigcup_{i\in I}D_{i}
.

In this section we only prove the following special case of Proposition
3.7
.
The general case is proven in Section
3.3
using Proposition
1.17
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Proposition
3.7
holds when
(\mathbf{c}_{1},\mathbf{c}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n})=(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},0,\dots,0)
and
\eta^{\prime}
is the counterclockwise space-filling SLE coupled with
\Psi_{1}
.

Proposition
3.8
follows from Proposition
3.5
and a
locality
property for
\mathbf{c}=0
IG fields. In Section
3.2.1
we state the locality property for planar domains due to Dubédat
[
Dub09b
]
. In Section
3.2.2
we extend locality to the setting of IG surfaces. We use this locality property to prove Proposition
3.8
in Section
3.2.3
.

3.2.1
Locality in planar domains

In this section we state Theorem
3.10
on the locality of
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}=0}
in planar domains, due to Dubédat
[
Dub09b
]
. For the reader’s convenience we include the argument in Appendix
C
.

For
i,j\in\{1,2\}
let
D_{ij}
be a bounded simply-connected domain with smooth boundary. Suppose that for the
D_{ij}
, there exists a pair of common open boundary segments and a pair of common smooth disjoint cross-cuts
\eta_{\ell},\eta_{r}
joining them; call the region they bound
B\subset D_{ij}
, and let
x\in\partial B\cap D_{ij}
be a common boundary point. We call the connected component of
D_{ij}\backslash B
immediately clockwise of
x
the
left subdomain
of
D_{ij}
, and similarly define the
right subdomain
. For
i,j\in\{1,2\}
we suppose the left subdomains of
D_{i1}
and
D_{i2}
agree and the right subdomains of
D_{1j}
and
D_{2j}
agree. See Figure
6
.

Figure 6:
Setup for Theorem
3.10
.

For an IG surface
(D_{ij},\Psi,x)/{\sim_{{\mathbf{c}}}}
sampled from
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}
on the one pointed domain (Definition
1.7
), let
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}^{B,ij}
denote the law of the decorated IG surface
(B,\Psi,\eta_{\ell},\eta_{r},x)/{\sim_{\mathbf{c}}}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

The measures
\mathrm{IG}^{B,ij}_{\mathbf{c}}
are mutually absolutely continuous for all
i,j\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

Let
\theta_{0}\in[0,2\pi)
be the angle such that the counterclockwise tangent to
B
at
x
is parallel to
e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta_{0}}
, and let
\mathfrak{h}_{ij}
be the harmonic function on
D_{ij}
whose boundary value at
y\neq x
equals the total curvature of the counterclockwise boundary arc from
x
to
y
. By Lemma
2.7
, if
h_{ij}
is a zero boundary GFF on
D_{ij}
, then the law of
(D_{ij},h_{ij}+\chi({\mathbf{c}})(\mathfrak{h}_{ij}+\theta_{0}),x)/{\sim_{\mathbf{c}}}
is
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}
. By Lemma
2.4
, the values of
\mathfrak{h}_{ij}
on
\partial B\cap\partial D_{ij}
(and hence on a neighborhood of
\partial B\cap\partial D_{ij}
in
\partial D_{ij}
) agree for all
i,j
. Therefore the laws of
(h_{ij}+\chi({\mathbf{c}})(\mathfrak{h}_{ij}+\theta_{0}))|_{B}
are mutually absolutely continuous for all
i,j

[
MS16a
, Proposition 3.4]
.
∎

Theorem 3.10

(
[
Dub09b
]
)
.

\frac{d\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}=0}^{B,11}}{d\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}=0}^{B,21}}(\mathcal{S})=\frac{d\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}=0}^{B,12}}{d\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}=0}^{B,22}}(\mathcal{S})\qquad\text{ for }\mathrm{IG}^{B,ij}_{\mathbf{c}=0}\text{-a.e. IG surface }\mathcal{S}.

(3.2)

The left hand side of (
3.2
) describes the relative likelihood of observing
\mathcal{S}
when we vary the left subdomain.
Equation (
3.2
) states that this relative likelihood does not depend on the right subdomain. In this way Theorem
3.10
is a locality result for
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}=0}
.

3.2.2
Locality for
\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}=(0,\dots,0)
IG surfaces

In this section we adapt Theorem
3.10
to the setting of IG surfaces.

We work in the domain
(\mathbb{D},x)
where
x=-\mathbbm{i}
. See Figure
7
(left).
For
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, consider two disjoint cross-cuts
\eta_{\ell}^{ij},\eta_{r}^{ij}
which do not contain
x
. Suppose the connected component
B^{ij}
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash(\eta_{\ell}^{ij}\cup\eta_{r}^{ij})
having
x
on its boundary also has
\eta_{\ell}^{ij}
and
\eta_{r}^{ij}
on its boundary, and
x,\eta_{\ell}^{ij},\eta_{r}^{ij}
lie on
\partial B^{ij}
in clockwise order. Let
L^{ij}
be the connected component of
\mathbb{D}\backslash B^{ij}
having
\eta_{\ell}^{ij}
on its boundary, let
R^{ij}
be the other connected component, and let
V^{ij}
be a simply connected subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
such that
B^{ij}\subset V^{ij}
and
\partial V^{ij}\setminus\partial\mathbb{D}
lies at positive distance from
B^{ij}
.

Suppose for each
i=1,2
the curve-decorated domains
(L^{ij}\cup V^{ij},\eta_{\ell}^{ij},\eta_{r}^{ij})
for
j=1,2
are conformally equivalent, and suppose for each
j=1,2
that
(V^{ij}\cup R^{ij},\eta_{\ell}^{ij},\eta_{r}^{ij})
for
i=1,2
are conformally equivalent. One should think of this as saying that
modulo conformal equivalence
, the four curve-decorated domains arise from choosing among two possible left subdomains and two possible right subdomains. In particular, unlike in Theorem
3.10
, we do not assume
L^{1j}=L^{2j}
.

For
(\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
sampled from
\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}
on
(\mathbb{D},x)
, let
\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{ij}
denote the law of the curve-decorated
\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}
-IG surface
(B^{ij},\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta_{\ell}^{ij},\eta_{r}^{ij})/{\sim}
.

Figure 7:

Left.
Proposition
3.11
gives an identity for Radon-Nikodym derivatives of the turquoise
{\mathbf{c}}
-IG surface when
i
and
j
take values in
\{1,2\}
.
Middle.
In the proof of Proposition
3.11
, we define a domain
\widetilde{V}^{ij}
with smooth boundary. Four possible domains (boundaries shown in bold) can be obtained by gluing to
B^{ij}
a “left piece” and “right piece” chosen from the connected components of
\widetilde{V}^{ij}\backslash B^{ij}
and
\mathbb{D}\backslash B^{ij}
. We apply Theorem
3.10
to these four domains.
Right.
Setup for the proof of Proposition
3.12
.
The shaded region is
M_{\tau}=\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\subset\mathbb{D}
, and
L_{1},\dots,L_{m}
are the red loops. We choose a region
B
(turquoise) bounded by a pair of cross-cuts separating
L_{1}
from the other loops such that
x\in\partial B
. Let
L
and
R
be the two connected components of
\mathbb{D}\backslash B
(each colored gray and white), and let
V\subset M_{\tau}
be a simply connected neighborhood of
B
(between blue curves).

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}
be a vector with all entries equal to 0.
The four laws
\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{ij}
are mutually absolutely continuous, and

\frac{d\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{11}}{d\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{21}}(\mathcal{S})=\frac{d\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{12}}{d\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{22}}(\mathcal{S})\qquad\text{ for }\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{ij}\text{-a.e.\ IG surface }\mathcal{S}.

Proof.

From our conformal equivalence assumptions, it is immediate that
(V^{ij},\eta_{\ell}^{ij},\eta_{r}^{ij})
for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
are all conformally equivalent.
Pick a simply connected domain
\widetilde{V}^{11}
with smooth boundary such that
B^{11}\subset\widetilde{V}^{11}\subset V^{11}
, and
B^{11}
(resp.
\widetilde{V}^{11}
) lies at positive distance from
\partial\widetilde{V}^{11}\backslash\partial\mathbb{D}
(resp.
\partial V^{11}\backslash\partial\mathbb{D}
). Define
\widetilde{V}^{ij}
for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
using the above conformal equivalence. See Figure
7
(middle).

For this paragraph we fix the choice
i=j=1
. Let
L_{0}
(resp.
R_{0}
) be the connected component of
\widetilde{V}^{11}\backslash B^{11}
immediately clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) of
x
, and write
L_{1}=L^{11}
and
R_{1}=R^{11}
. For
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
let
D_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=\overline{L_{i^{\prime}}\cup B^{11}\cup R_{j^{\prime}}}
, see Figure
7
(middle). Let
n
be the number of entries of
\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}
.
Recall
\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{11}
is the law of the
{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}
-IG surface
(B^{11},\Psi_{1}|_{B^{11}},\dots,\Psi_{n}|_{B^{11}},\eta^{11}_{\ell},\eta^{11}_{r})/{\sim}
where
\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}
are independently sampled from
\mathrm{IG}_{{{\mathbf{c}}=0}}
on
(D_{11},x)
with additive constant fixed such that the boundary value infinitesimally counterclockwise of
x=-\mathbbm{i}
in
\partial\mathbb{D}
is
0
. Similarly, we define
\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{00}
,
{\mathrm{IG}}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{10}
and
\mathrm{IG}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{01}
by replacing
D_{11}
with
D_{00},D_{10}
and
D_{01}
respectively.
This give four measures, which are mutually absolutely continuous by the sentence above Theorem
3.10
. Applying Theorem
3.10

n
times gives

\frac{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{00}}{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{10}}\frac{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{11}}{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{01}}=1\qquad\text{ for }{\mathrm{IG}}^{00}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}\text{-a.e. IG surface}.

For each
i,j\in\{1,2\}
we repeat the preceding paragraph to define three measures
{\mathrm{IG}}_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}^{00},{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{i0},{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{0j}
. Some measures are multiply defined (for instance
{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{10}
is defined twice, for
i=1,j=1,2
), but these definitions are consistent by conformal equivalence of the domains. In total this gives nine mutually absolutely continuous measures satisfying

\frac{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{00}}{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{i0}}\frac{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{ij}}{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{0j}}=1\qquad\text{ for }{\mathrm{IG}}^{00}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}\text{-a.e. IG surface, for }i,j\in\{1,2\}.

(3.3)

Multiplying the left hand sides of (
3.3
) for
(i,j)\in\{(1,1),(2,2)\}
, and dividing by the left hand sides for
(i,j)\in\{(1,2),(2,1)\}
, we obtain
\frac{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{11}}{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{21}}\frac{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{22}}{d{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{12}}=1
for
{\mathrm{IG}}_{{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}^{ij}
-a.e. IG surface, as desired.
∎

3.2.3
LQG-IG local sets of
(\mathbf{c}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n})=(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},0,\dots,0)
IG-decorated LQG surfaces

In this section we prove Proposition
3.8
.
We work in the setting of Proposition
3.8
, so
(\mathbf{c}_{1},\mathbf{c}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n})=(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},0,\dots,0)
,
K
is an
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set coupled with
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime})
, and
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{D}
is a Poisson point process with intensity measure
\varepsilon^{-1}\mu_{\Phi}
independent of
(\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime},K)
. As before the curve
\eta^{\prime}
is segmented by the points
\Lambda
into curve segments
\eta_{0}^{\prime},\dots,\eta_{|\Lambda|}^{\prime}
, and each
\eta_{i}^{\prime}
traces out a domain
D_{i}
with boundary points
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}
.

Recall that in Definition
3.1
, we sample a random set of indices
I_{\tau}
conditionally independent of
K
given
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime},\Lambda)
. We first show an
n\geq 1
generalization of Proposition
3.5
via locality of
{\mathbf{c}}=0
IG fields.

Proposition 3.12
.

Assume
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
and
(\mathbf{c}_{1},\mathbf{c}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n})=(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},0,\dots,0)
.
In the setting of Definition
3.1
, let
L_{1},\dots,L_{m}
be disjoint loops in
M_{\tau}
which bound disjoint regions
O_{1},\dots,O_{m}
and let

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}=(M_{\tau},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\{\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}\>:\>i\in I_{\tau}\})/{\sim}
. Suppose
(M_{\tau},\Phi,L_{1},\dots,L_{m})/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
\sigma(\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau})
, i.e., the loops on
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
are chosen in a way depending only on
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
. Then almost surely, given
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
the IG-decorated LQG surfaces
(O_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
for
i=1,\dots,m
are conditionally independent.

Proof.

We begin with a proof outline. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}:=(M_{\tau},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\{(\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}):i\in I_{\tau}\})/{\sim}
be
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
with the
{\mathbf{c}}=0
fields forgotten. We split
\mathbb{D}
into three regions
L,B,R
with
B\subset M_{\tau}
such that
(M_{\tau},\Phi,B)/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
(see Figure
7
, right). By our
n=1
result (Proposition
3.5
) the IG-decorated LQG surfaces
(L\cup B,\Phi,\Psi_{1})/{\sim}
and
(R\cup B,\Phi,\Psi_{1})/{\sim}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
. Using the
{\mathbf{c}}=0
IG locality property (Proposition
3.11
), we can show this conditional independence still holds when we instead condition on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
further decorated by
\Psi_{2}|_{B},\dots,\Psi_{n}|_{B}
. The Markov property of the GFF then implies the conditional independence of
(L\cup B,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
and
(R\cup B,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
under this conditioning; an easy argument then shows this still holds when conditioning on
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
instead. Varying our choice of
B
gives the desired result.

We now turn to the proof proper.
Draw a pair of cross-cuts
\eta_{\ell},\eta_{r}
in
\mathbb{D}
which stay in
M_{\tau}
and do not hit
x:=-\mathbbm{i}
, such that the connected component
B
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash(\eta_{\ell}\cup\eta_{r})
having
x
on its boundary also has
\eta_{\ell}
and
\eta_{r}
on its boundary, and
x,\eta_{\ell},\eta_{r}
lie on
\partial B
in clockwise order. Let
L
be the connected component of
\mathbb{D}\backslash B
having
\eta_{\ell}
on its boundary, and let
R
be the other connected component. Choose a simply connected domain
V
such that
B\subset V\subset M_{\tau}
, and
B
(resp.
V
) lies at positive distance from
\partial V\backslash\partial\mathbb{D}
(resp.
\partial M_{\tau}\backslash\partial\mathbb{D}
). We do this in such a way that
(M_{\tau},\Phi,B,V)/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
, and
O_{1}\subset L
while
O_{2},\dots,O_{m}\subset R
. See Figure
7
(right).

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}=(L\cup B,\Phi,\Psi_{1})/{\sim}
and
\mathcal{S}_{r}=(R\cup B,\Phi,\Psi_{1})/{\sim}
.
Note that
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
and
(O_{1},\Phi,\Psi_{1})/{\sim}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{r}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
and
(O_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1})/{\sim}
for
i=2,\dots,m
. Thus, by Proposition
3.5
, conditioned on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
, the decorated LQG surfaces
\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}
are conditionally independent. Let

\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}=(M_{\tau},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\{(\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}):i\in I_{\tau}\},\Psi_{2}|_{B},\dots,\Psi_{n}|_{B})/{\sim}.

Using the locality of
\mathbf{c}=0
IG fields, we will prove that conditioned on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
the decorated LQG surfaces
\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}
are still conditionally independent.

Let
P_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}
be the conditional law of of
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
. Similarly let
P_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}}
be the conditional law of
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
given
(\mathcal{M}_{\tau},\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r})
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}}
) be the conditional law of
(\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r})
given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
). By the aforementioned conditional independence we have
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(d\mathcal{S}_{\ell},d\mathcal{S}_{r})=\mathcal{L}^{\ell}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(d\mathcal{S}_{\ell})\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(d\mathcal{S}_{r})
for some probability measures
\mathcal{L}^{\ell}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}},\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}
which are measurable functions of
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
. We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.13
.

Condition on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
and fix
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{1},\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{2},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{1},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{2}
(where the
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{i}
and
\mathcal{S}_{r}^{j}
denote possible realizations of
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{S}_{r}
). Then the probability measures
P_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{i},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{j}}
are mutually absolutely continuous for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
. Furthermore, for a.e.
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
we have
\frac{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{1},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{1}}}{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{2},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{1}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})=\frac{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{1},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{2}}}{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{2},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{2}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof we condition on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
.
For each
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, we have a copy of the setup described above, where we have fields
\Phi^{ij},\Psi_{1}^{ij},\dots,\Psi_{n}^{ij}
, a Poisson point process
\Lambda^{ij}
, and domains
B^{ij}\subset V^{ij}\subset M^{ij}
. Fixing
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{i}
and
\mathcal{S}_{r}^{j}
corresponds to conditioning on all of the above (modulo conformal coordinate change) except
\Psi_{2}^{ij},\dots,\Psi_{n}^{ij}
. Proposition
3.11
gives mutual absolute continuity of the conditional laws of the IG surface
\mathcal{B}=(B^{ij},\Psi_{2},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{ij}_{\ell},\eta^{ij}_{r})/{\sim}
for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, and further gives
an identity for Radon-Nikodym derivatives of these conditional laws. Since
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
determines and is determined by
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{M}_{\tau})
, we obtain the desired Lemma
3.13
.
∎

Condition on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
. By definition we have

P_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}=\iint P_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\ell}(d\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{r}(d\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime})

(3.4)

where
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}
denote possible realizations of
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{S}_{r}
. The mutual absolute continuity claim of Lemma
3.13
applied to (
3.4
) implies that a.s.
P_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}}
and
P_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}
are mutually absolutely continuous.
We can thus take Radon-Nikodym derivatives in (
3.4
) to get

\frac{dP_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}}{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})=\iint\frac{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}}}{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})\frac{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}}}{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\ell}(d\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\prime})\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{r}(d\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}).

(3.5)

Lemma
3.13
implies that
\frac{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}}}{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})
does not depend on
\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}
, i.e., it equals
f_{\ell}(\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})
for some function
f_{\ell}
. Likewise
\frac{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}}}{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})=f_{r}(\mathcal{S}_{r},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})
for some function
f_{r}
. Thus (
3.5
) gives

\frac{dP_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}}{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})=\int f_{\ell}(\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})\mathcal{L}^{\ell}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(d\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\prime})\int f_{r}(\mathcal{S}_{r},\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})\mathcal{L}^{r}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(d\mathcal{S}_{r}^{\prime}),

so
\frac{dP_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}}}{dP_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}}=F_{\ell}(\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})F_{r}(\mathcal{S}_{r},\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})
for some functions
F_{\ell},F_{r}
.

Recall that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}}
denotes the conditional law of
(\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r})
given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
. By considering two ways of sampling from the joint law of
(\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*},\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r})
given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}}(d\mathcal{S}_{\ell},d\mathcal{S}_{r})P_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(d\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})

\displaystyle=P_{\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{S}_{r}}(d\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\ell}(d\mathcal{S}_{\ell})\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{r}(d\mathcal{S}_{r})

\displaystyle=F_{\ell}(\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\ell}(d\mathcal{S}_{\ell})F_{r}(\mathcal{S}_{r},\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{r}(d\mathcal{S}_{r})P_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}(d\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}).

Thus, for a.e.
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
the measure
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}}
factorizes as
F_{\ell}(\mathcal{S}_{\ell},\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{\ell}(d\mathcal{S}_{\ell})F_{r}(\mathcal{S}_{r},\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*})\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\tau}}^{r}(d\mathcal{S}_{r})
, or in other words, a.s. conditioned on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
the decorated LQG surfaces
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{S}_{r}
are conditionally independent.

Now, let
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\ell}=(L\cup B,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\eta_{\ell},\Psi_{2},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
be
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
decorated by the additional
{\mathbf{c}}=0
IG fields, and likewise define
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{r}
. By the above conditional independence and the domain Markov property of the GFF,
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\ell}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{r}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
. Next, let
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*,\ell}
be
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
decorated by the fields
\Psi_{i}|_{(L\cup B)\cap M_{\tau}}
rather than
\Psi_{i}|_{B}
for
i=2,\dots,n
. It is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\ell}
, so
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\ell},\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{r}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*,\ell}
. Finally,
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
is
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*,\ell}
decorated by the fields
\Psi_{i}|_{M_{\tau}}
rather than
\Psi_{i}|_{(L\cup B)\cap M_{\tau}}
for
i=2,\dots,n
, hence is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{*,\ell}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{r}
. We conclude
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\ell},\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{r}
are conditionally independent given
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
.

Thus, given
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
, the decorated LQG surface
(O_{1},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
is conditionally independent of
(O_{2},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim},\dots,(O_{m},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
. Repeating the argument for the other
m-1
indices, we obtain the mutual independence of the
(O_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
given
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.8
.

Let
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
be a finite collection of connected components of
\mathbb{D}\backslash K
chosen such that
(K_{\varepsilon},\Phi,\partial U_{1},\dots,\partial U_{m})/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
, and let
L_{1},\dots,L_{m}
be disjoint simple loops in
K_{\varepsilon}
such that
L_{i}
is homotopic
7
7

7

More precisely, if
f:U_{i}\to\mathbb{D}
is a conformal map then
f(L_{i})
is homotopic to
\partial\mathbb{D}
in
f(U_{i}\cap K_{\varepsilon})
.
to
\partial U_{i}
in
U_{i}\cap K_{\varepsilon}
, and
(K_{\varepsilon},\Phi,L_{1},\dots,L_{m})/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
. Note that even if
\overline{U}_{i}\cap\overline{U}_{j}\neq\emptyset
for some
i\neq j
(e.g., when
K
has “pinch points”), by construction
K_{\varepsilon}
contains a neighborhood of
K
, so the loops
L_{1},\dots,L_{m}
can always be chosen

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
be defined as in Proposition
3.12
. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=(K_{\varepsilon},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\{\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}\>:\>i\in I\})/{\sim}
, so that
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}=\sigma(\widehat{\mathcal{M}},(K_{\varepsilon},\Phi,K)/{\sim})
. By Definition
3.1
, conditioned on
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
, the event
\{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}=\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime},\Lambda)
, and further conditioned on
\{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}=\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\}
, the decorated LQG surface
(K_{\varepsilon},\Phi,K)/{\sim}
(and hence
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
) is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime},\Lambda)
. Our loops are chosen in a way which is determined by
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
, so by Proposition
3.12
, conditioned on
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
,
\{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}=\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
, the decorated LQG surfaces bounded by the
L_{i}
are conditionally independent. Consequently, with the same conditioning, the
(U_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
are conditionally independent.

Notice that on
\{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}=\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\}
, the decorated LQG surface
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
. Therefore the
(U_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
and
\{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}=\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\}
. But conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
, the event
\{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}=\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Lambda,\Psi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
, since the event corresponds, in the inductive definition of
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
, to always selecting the point
p_{j}\in\partial(\bigcup_{i\in I_{j}}D_{i})
to lie in
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
at every step
j
and stopping when the whole
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
is explored. Thus, conditioned just on
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
, the
m
complementary LQG surfaces
(U_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
are conditionally independent. Since
m
is arbitrary, this gives the result.
∎

3.3
General
(\mathbf{c}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{n})
by rotation: proof of Theorem
3.3

We now use Proposition
1.17
to deduce Proposition
3.7
from Proposition
3.8
, after which Theorem
3.3
is straightforward.

Proof of Proposition
3.7
.

Let
\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}=({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})
and
\hat{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}=(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},0,\dots,0)
. Recall from the discussion at the beginning of Section
3
the vector of IG fields
(\widehat{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\Psi}_{n})
with central charges
(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}},0,\dots,0)
and the
n\times n
orthogonal matrix
A
taking
(\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
to
(\widehat{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\hat{\Psi}_{n})
.
By Proposition
1.17
, for any
D
the IG-decorated LQG surfaces
(D,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}
and
(D,\Phi,\widehat{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\Psi}_{n})/{\sim_{\widehat{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}}
determine each other (by applying
A
or
A^{-1}
). Thus, applying Proposition
3.8
to
(\Phi,\widehat{\Psi}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\Psi}_{n},\eta^{\prime},K)
, the decorated LQG surfaces
(U_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim_{\underline{{\mathbf{c}}}}}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
, and define

\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}=\sigma((\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}),\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},K)/{\sim}),

so
\mathcal{F}=\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
. It suffices to show that the decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
parametrized by the connected components
U
of
\mathbb{D}\setminus K
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
. Indeed, conditional independence is preserved under decreasing limits of
\sigma
-algebras by the backward martingale convergence theorem.

Sample a Poisson point process
\Lambda^{*}\subset\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
with intensity measure
\mu_{\Phi}\times\mathrm{Leb}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
which, given
\Phi
, is conditionally independent of everything else. For
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
define
\Lambda_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}:=\{z\>:\>(z,t)\in\Lambda^{*}\text{ and }t<(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\}
, so that
\Lambda_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
is a Poisson point process with intensity measure
(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\mu_{\Phi}
. Then, segment
\eta^{\prime}
using
\Lambda_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
and define
D_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime})
,
\eta_{i}^{\prime}(\varepsilon^{\prime})
,
a_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime})
,
b_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime})
,
c_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime})
,
d_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime})
for
i=0,\dots,|\Lambda_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}|
as in Definition
3.1
. Let

I(\varepsilon^{\prime}):=\{i\>:\>K\cap D_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime})\neq\emptyset\}\quad\text{and}\quad K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{i\in I(\varepsilon^{\prime})}D_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime}).

For
S\subset\overline{\mathbb{D}}
, write
\Lambda^{*}|_{S}:=\{(z,t)\in\Lambda^{*}\>:\>z\in S\}
. Define

\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{MOT}}:=\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\{(\eta^{\prime}_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime}),a_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime}),b_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime}),c_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime}),d_{i}(\varepsilon^{\prime}))\>:\>i\in I(\varepsilon^{\prime})\},K,\Lambda^{*}|_{K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}})/{\sim}}}\right).

This is the same
\sigma
-algebra as defined in Proposition
3.7
except that we further keep track of
\Lambda^{*}
. The same argument still applies so Proposition
3.7
holds in this setting.

For
\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime}<\varepsilon^{\prime}
, the set
K_{\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\subset K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
is measurable with respect to
K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
and
\Psi_{1}|_{K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}},\dots,\Psi_{n}|_{K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}
. Indeed, the segments of
\eta^{\prime}
contained in
K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
are locally determined by
\widehat{\Psi}_{1}|_{K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}

[
GMS19
, Lemma 2.4]
and hence by the
\Psi_{i}|_{K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}
. Since
\eta^{\prime}
is defined via flow lines, which are intrinsic to the IG surface, we conclude that the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
is decreasing.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{0+}^{\mathrm{MOT}}=\bigcap_{\varepsilon^{\prime}>0}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
. As
K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\downarrow K
as
\varepsilon^{\prime}\to 0
, and
{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}
induces the Euclidean topology
[
HM22
, Proposition 1.7]
, we see that a.s.
K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})
for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\prime}
. Since
\eta^{\prime}
is locally determined by
\widehat{\Psi}_{1}
and hence by
\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n}
, for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\prime}
the set
K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
is determined by
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})
and the restrictions of
\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda^{*}
to this domain.
As before, it follows that

\mathcal{F}_{0+}^{\mathrm{MOT}}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}
where

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}:=\sigma((\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}),\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},K,\Lambda^{*}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})})/{\sim}).

For each connected component
U
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash K
let
U_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(\partial U;{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})\cap U
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
be the collection of all decorated LQG surfaces
(U_{\varepsilon},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda^{*}|_{U_{\varepsilon}})/{\sim}
as
U
varies. We know
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
and
\sigma(\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon})
for all
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
because
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})\subset K_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\cup\bigcup_{U}U_{\varepsilon}
. Hence
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{0+}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
and
\sigma(\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon})
. On the other hand,
\mathcal{F}_{0+}^{\mathrm{MOT}}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}
, and
\sigma(\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon})\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}
. We conclude
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}=\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{0+}^{\mathrm{MOT}},\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon})
.

By Proposition
3.7
and the backward martingale convergence theorem, the collection of decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda^{*}|_{U})/{\sim}
indexed by the connected components
U
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash K
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}_{0+}^{\mathrm{MOT}}
. Since each
(U_{\varepsilon},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda^{*}|_{U_{\varepsilon}})/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda^{*}|_{U})/{\sim}
by the locality and conformal covariance of the LQG metric (see Section
2.4
), the conditional independence still holds if we further condition on
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
. We conclude that the decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda^{*}|_{U})/{\sim}
are conditionally independent given
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}=\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{0+}^{\mathrm{MOT}},\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon})
. Since the restrictions of a Poisson point process to disjoint domains are independent Poisson point processes in these domains, the decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
are conditionally independent from
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\varepsilon}
given
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
, hence are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
. This completes the proof.
∎

In Lemma
3.14
below we give a
\sigma
-algebra equivalent to
\mathcal{F}
in Theorem
3.3
which does not use the LQG metric. We will not need to use Lemma
3.14
but state it to give context. Recall that for a measure space
(\Sigma,\mathcal{G},\mu)
, the
completion
of
\mathcal{G}
is
\sigma(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{N}_{\mu})
where
\mathcal{N}_{\mu}
is the collection of all subsets of
\mu
-null sets in
\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 3.14
.

In the setting of Theorem
3.3
, sample a Poisson point process
\Lambda^{*}\subset\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
with intensity measure
\mu_{\Phi}\times\mathrm{Leb}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
which, given
\Phi
, is conditionally independent of everything else. For
\varepsilon>0
let
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}=\{z\in\mathbb{D}:(z,t)\in\Lambda^{*}\text{ and }t\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\}
. The curve
\eta^{\prime}
is split by the points of
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\backslash K
into a collection of curve segments; let
\{D_{i}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\}
be the traces of these curve segments. Define

\begin{gathered}I^{\prime}(\varepsilon):=\{i\>:\>K\cap D_{i}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\neq\emptyset\},\qquad K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}=\bigcup_{i\in I^{\prime}(\varepsilon)}D_{i}^{\prime}(\varepsilon),\\
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\sigma((K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},K,\{(z,t)\in\Lambda^{*}\>:\>z\in K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\backslash K\})/{\sim}),\qquad\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}.\end{gathered}

Then
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
agree up to null sets, i.e., they have the same completion.

Proof.

By the arguments in the proof of Theorem
3.3
immediately above,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is equal to

\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}:=\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\sigma((\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}),\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},K,\{(z,t)\in\Lambda^{*}\>:\>z\in\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}})\backslash K\})/{\sim}).

By definition
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
, and by Kolmogorov’s zero-one law, conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
, every event in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
has conditional probability either
0
or
1
. Therefore
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
have the same completion, as needed.
∎

3.4
Proofs of remaining theorems

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

We explain the
\kappa<4
case by constructing
\eta
as a flow line of an
\mathrm{IG}_{{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}}
field; the
\kappa>4
and
\kappa=4
cases are the same except
\eta
is a counterflow line or level line of the IG field, respectively.

Let
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
and independently let
(\mathbb{D},\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Psi,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{IG}_{({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n},{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}})}
. For concreteness we use the embedding given right below Definition
1.7
.

Suppose
\rho_{L}+\rho_{R}=\kappa-6
. Note that if
f:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}
is a conformal map with
f(0)=-\mathbbm{i}
, then
\Psi\circ f-\chi\arg f^{\prime}
has the law of a zero boundary GFF plus the harmonic function
\mathfrak{h}
such that
\mathfrak{h}(x)=2\pi\chi
for
x\in(-\infty,0)
and
\mathfrak{h}(x)=0
for
x\in(0,\infty)
, where
\chi=\chi({\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}})
as defined in (
1.4
).
Thus, the flow line of angle
\theta=\frac{\pi}{\chi\sqrt{\kappa}}(1+\rho_{R})
is an
\SLE_{\kappa}(\rho_{L};\rho_{R})
curve from
-\mathbbm{i}
to
\mathbbm{i}
; see Section
2.5
for details. Let
K
be this angle
\theta
flow line of
\Psi
. Since
K
is a local set of
\Psi
, in the setting of Definition
3.1
, conditioned on
\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}
the event
\{K\subset\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
is measurable with respect to
\Psi|_{\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}}
, and on this event
K
is measurable with respect to
\Psi|_{\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}}
. Consequently, the event
\{K\subset\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
is measurable with respect to
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Psi)/{\sim}
, and on this event
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Psi,K)/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Psi)/{\sim}
. This follows from the fact that flow lines are intrinsic to IG surfaces, plus absolute continuity considerations; see the discussion after the statement of
[
MS16a
, Proposition 3.4]
for details.
Therefore,
K
is an
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set for all
\varepsilon>0
, and hence an LQG-IG local set.

By Theorem
3.3
, given
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\sigma((\mathcal{B}_{0+}(K\cup\partial D;\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}),\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Psi)/{\sim})
defined as in (
1.10
) (i.e.,
\mathcal{F}
where we additionally keep track of
\Psi
in an infinitesimal neighborhood of
K\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
), the complementary IG-decorated LQG surfaces are conditionally independent. In the imaginary geometry coupling of
K
and
\Psi
, the conditional law of
\Psi
given
K
is that of the the zero boundary GFF plus a harmonic function with boundary values measurable with respect to
K
(see Section
2
). The information carried by
\Psi
in an infinitesimal neighborhood of
K\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
is thus just the boundary values of the harmonic function, and so is measurable with respect to
K
. We conclude
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}
, finishing the proof.

Now instead assume
\rho_{L}+\rho_{R}=-2
. Let
K
be an angle
\theta
flow line of
\Psi
in
\mathbb{D}
from
\mathbbm{i}
to
-\mathbbm{i}
; varying
\theta
gives
\SLE_{\kappa}(\rho_{L};\rho_{R})
for any
\rho_{L},\rho_{R}>-2
with sum
-2
. Proceeding as before completes the argument.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.11
.

Suppose
\kappa\in(4,8)
.
Let
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
and independently let
(\mathbb{D},\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Psi,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{IG}_{{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n},{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}}
.

As in the proof of Theorem
1.9
, the counterflow line of
\Psi
started at
-\mathbbm{i}
targeted at
\mathbbm{i}
with an appropriately chosen angle
\theta
is an
\SLE_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
curve.
This counterflow line is a local set of
\Psi
. In fact, by choosing a countable dense set of target points, one can realize a
branching
\SLE_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
process
as a local set of
\Psi
, and thus obtain the CLE
κ
gasket as a local set of
\Psi

[
MS17
, Section 1.2.3]
. Now the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
1.9
directly above applies.

If
\kappa=4
, the same argument holds since
\mathrm{CLE}_{4}
can be realized as the level loops of a zero boundary GFF, and in this coupling the CLE
4
gasket is a local set of this GFF
[
MS
]
(see
[
ASW19
, Proposition 1]
for a published construction).
∎

Remark 3.15
.

When
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
, if we assume the
\mathrm{CLE}_{\kappa}
gasket can be constructed via flow and counterflow lines of a sample from
\mathrm{IG}_{{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{SLE}}}
, then Theorem
1.11
holds for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
by the same proof. We expect that the desired imaginary geometry construction of CLE follows from the arguments of
[
MSW17
]
. Indeed
[
MSW17
, Theorem 7.8]
iteratively constructs
\mathrm{CLE}_{\kappa}
via the so-called
boundary CLE
, which arises from branching flow and counterflow lines for
\Psi

[
MSW17
, Table 1]
.

We now turn to Theorem
1.16
. It suffices to show that if
(K_{t})_{t\leq T}
is an LQG-IG peeling process (Definition
1.15
), then
K_{T}
is an LQG-IG local set.

Lemma 3.16
.

If
(K_{t})_{t\leq T}
is an LQG-IG peeling process for
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
and
\eta^{\prime}
is coupled with
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
as in Definition
3.2
, then
K_{T}
is an LQG-IG local set for
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime})
.

Proof.

As in Definition
3.1
, let
\varepsilon>0
, and let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{D}
be a Poisson point process with intensity measure
\varepsilon^{-1}\mu_{\Phi}
conditionally independent of everything else given
\Phi
, and define
D_{i},\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}
as explained above Definition
3.1
. Recall that
I_{\tau}
is the set of indices
i
of the regions
D_{i}
discovered by the random inductive peeling process of Definition
3.1
. To lighten notation define

M_{\tau}=\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}.

We first check that
\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{T};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}
is an
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set. Let
t_{0}=0
and inductively define the stopping times
t_{j+1}=T\wedge\inf\{s\geq t_{j}\>:\>K_{s}\cap\partial\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t_{j}};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})\cap\mathbb{D}\neq\emptyset\}
for the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\delta}
defined in Definition
1.15
.
For each
j\geq 0
let Claim
j
be the following claim: “Conditioned on the decorated LQG surface

(M_{\tau},\Phi,\Lambda,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\{(\eta^{\prime}_{i},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})\>:\>i\in I_{\tau}\},\Lambda\cap M_{\tau})/{\sim},

(3.6)

the event
\{\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t_{j}};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}\subset M_{\tau}\}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda)
. Moreover, further conditioning on
\{\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t_{j}};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}\subset M_{\tau}\}
, the decorated LQG surface
(M_{\tau},\Phi,(K_{s})_{s\leq{t_{j}}})/{\sim}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda)
.” We will inductively prove that Claim
j
holds for all
j
.

By definition
K_{t_{0}}=\partial\mathbb{D}
. By the locality of the LQG metric and the fact that it is invariant under LQG coordinate change (Section
2.4
) the event
\{\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t_{0}};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}\subset M_{\tau}\}
is measurable with respect to (
3.6
). Conditioning on this event and on (
3.6
), by the definition of LQG-IG peeling process, the decorated LQG surface
(M_{\tau},\Phi,(K_{s})_{s\leq t_{1}})/{\sim}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda)
. Thus Claim
0
holds. Now, assuming Claim
j
, we will show Claim
j+1
. Combining Claim
j
with the fact that
(K_{t})_{t\leq T}
is an LQG-IG peeling process yields the following: Conditioned on (
3.6
),
\{\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t_{j}};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}\subset M_{\tau}\}
and
(M_{\tau},\Phi,(K_{s})_{s\leq t_{j}})/{\sim}
, the decorated LQG surface
(M_{\tau},\Phi,(K_{s})_{s\leq t_{j+1}})/{\sim}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda)
. By the locality and conformal covariance of the LQG metric, with the above conditioning, the event
\{\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t_{j+1}};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}\subset M_{\tau}\}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda)
, and further conditioning on this event,
(M_{\tau},\Phi,(K_{s})_{s\leq t_{j+1}})/{\sim}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda)
. We have thus obtained a version of Claim
j+1
where we not only condition on (
3.6
), but additionally on
\{\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t_{j}};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}\subset M_{\tau}\}
and
(M_{\tau},\Phi,(K_{s})_{s\leq t_{j}})/{\sim}
. Since
\{\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t_{j}};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}\subset M_{\tau}\}\subset\{\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t_{j+1}};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}\subset M_{\tau}\}
and
(M_{\tau},\Phi,(K_{s})_{s\leq t_{j+1}})/{\sim}
determines
(M_{\tau},\Phi,(K_{s})_{s\leq t_{j}})/{\sim}
, by Claim
j
, the various independence statements still hold if we do not condition on the additional terms above. Therefore Claim
j+1
holds.

We claim that
t_{j}=T
for all sufficiently large
j
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction this is false. Arbitrarily pick a point
p_{j}\in K_{t_{j}}\backslash\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{t_{j-1}};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})
for each
j>0
. By definition
\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}(p_{i},p_{j})>\delta
for every
i<j
, contradicting the fact that
(\overline{\mathbb{D}},\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})
has the Euclidean topology and thus is compact. Thus
t_{j}=T
for all large
j
. By Claim
j
, we conclude
\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{T};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}
is an
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG set.

Now, we need to justify that
K_{T}
itself is an
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set.
Let
I^{\delta}=\{i\>:\>D_{i}\cap\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{T};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}\neq\emptyset\}
and let
I=\{i\>:\>D_{i}\cap K_{T}\neq\emptyset\}
.
Given (
3.6
), the event
\{I^{\delta}=I_{\tau}\}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Lambda,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
. Indeed,
\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{T};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}
is
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local, and
\{I^{\delta}=I_{\tau}\}
is measurable with respect to (
3.6
) and
(\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{T};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})},\Phi,(K_{t})_{s\leq T})/{\sim}
. We conclude that
1_{I=I_{\tau}}=\lim_{\delta\to 0}1_{I^{\delta}=I_{\tau}}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Lambda,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
given (
3.6
), fulfilling the first condition of Definition
3.1
.
Next, since
\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{T};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}
is
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local, given (
3.6
) and
\{I^{\delta}=I_{\tau}\}
the decorated LQG surface
(M_{\tau},\Phi,\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{T};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})})/{\sim}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Lambda,\Psi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(K_{T};\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi})}\downarrow K_{T}
and
1_{I=I_{\tau}}=\lim_{\delta\to 0}1_{I^{\delta}=I_{\tau}}
, we obtain the second condition of Definition
3.1
.
Thus
K_{T}
is an
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set for all
\varepsilon>0
, and hence is an LQG-IG local set.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.16
.

This follows from Theorem
3.3
and Lemma
3.16
.
∎

We next address the unit boundary length LQG disk with a marked interior point, whose law is denoted
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
(Definition
1.6
). The main result is Theorem
3.19
for the LQG-IG local sets defined below, from which all the other theorems follow.

Definition 3.17
.

Consider the setting of Definition
3.1
except that
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,0,-\mathbbm{i})
is an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
instead.
An
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set

K\subset\overline{\mathbb{D}}
is a random compact set coupled with
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime})
such that
K\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
is a.s. connected, almost surely
0\in K
, and
the following holds.

Independently of
(\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},K,\eta^{\prime})
, sample a Poisson point process
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{D}
with intensity measure
\varepsilon^{-1}\mu_{\Phi}
and define
D_{i},\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}
as in Definition
3.1
.
Independently of everything else sample a nonnegative integer
T\geq 0
with
\mathbb{P}[T=t]=2^{-t-1}
, let
I_{0}
be the set of indices
i
such that
D_{i}(\varepsilon)\cap\partial\mathbb{D}\neq\emptyset
, and for
j\geq 0
inductively define
I_{j+1}
from
I_{j}
by independently sampling a point
p_{j+1}\in(\partial\bigcup_{i\in I_{j}}D_{i})\backslash\partial\mathbb{D}
from the LQG length measure, then setting
I_{j+1}=I_{j}\cup\{i\>:\>p_{j+1}\in\partial D_{i}\}
. We stop the process either at time
T
or at the time
t
when
I_{t}=\{0,\dots,N+1\}
, whichever is earlier; call this time
\tau
.

Conditioned on
\{0\in\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
and on
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\{\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}):i\in I_{\tau}\},0,\Lambda\cap\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i})/{\sim}
, the event
\{K\subset\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\Lambda)
.
Moreover, further conditioning on
\{K\subset\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
, the decorated LQG surface
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,K)/{\sim}
is conditionally independent of
(\Phi,\Psi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime},\Lambda)
.

K
is called a
LQG-IG local set
if it is an
\varepsilon
-LQG-IG local set for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Definition
3.17
for
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
is the same as Definitions
3.1
and
3.2
for
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
, except that we require that
K
contains the marked bulk point
0
, and in our random exploration of the disk we condition on
0
lying in the explored region.
The following lemma is an analog of Proposition
3.5
for
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
.

Lemma 3.18
.

Consider the setting of Definition
3.1
with
n=1
. Weight by
\mu_{\Phi}(\mathbb{D})
, sample a point
z\in\mathbb{D}
from
\mu_{\Phi}/\mu_{\Phi}(\mathbb{D})
conditionally independently of everything except
\Phi
, and condition on
\{z\in\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
.
Let
L_{1},\dots,L_{m}
be simple loops in the interior of
\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}
and let
O_{k}
be the bounded connected component of
\mathbb{C}\backslash L_{k}
. Suppose
(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,L_{1},\dots,L_{m})/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\bullet}:=(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i},\Phi,\Psi_{1},\{\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}):i\in I_{\tau}\},\Lambda,z)/{\sim}
, and the
O_{k}
are pairwise disjoint. Then conditioned on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\bullet}
, the IG-decorated LQG surfaces
(O_{1},\Phi,\Psi)/{\sim},\dots,(O_{m},\Phi,\Psi)/{\sim}
are conditionally independent.

Proof.

For a finite measure
M
let
M^{\#}=M/|M|
be the corresponding probability measure.
In the setting of Definition
3.1
, weighting by
\mu_{\Phi}(\mathbb{D})
, sampling
z\sim\mu_{\Phi}^{\#}
and restricting to
\{z\in\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\}
is equivalent to weighting by
\mu_{\Phi}(\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i})
then sampling
z\sim(\mu_{\Phi}|_{\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}})^{\#}
. Thus, the present setup can be obtained starting with the setup of Proposition
3.5
, weighting
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
by its LQG area and sampling a point in
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
from the probability measure proportional to its area measure to get
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\bullet}
. This weighting and sampling does not affect the conditional law of the
(U_{i},\Phi,\Psi)/{\sim}
given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
, so Proposition
3.5
gives the desired conditional independence.
∎

Now we state and prove Theorem
3.19
, which is the
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
variant of Theorem
3.3
.

Theorem 3.19
.

Suppose
(\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},\eta^{\prime},K)
are as in Definition
3.17
. Let
\mathcal{F}=\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\sigma((\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(K\cup\partial\mathbb{D};{\mathfrak{d}_{\Phi}}),\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n},K,0)/{\sim})
. Then the decorated LQG surfaces
(U,\Phi,\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})/{\sim}
parametrized by the connected components
U
of
\mathbb{D}\backslash K
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
3.18
in place of Proposition
3.5
, we can prove the
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}^{\bullet}
analogs of Proposition
3.8
, Proposition
3.7
and Theorem
3.3
by the same arguments. The last of these analogs is the desired result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.12
.

By the same argument as in Theorem
1.9
we see that
K
is an LQG-IG local set so Theorem
3.19
applies.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.13
.

Brownian motion is conformally invariant, and local in the sense that for any domains
D_{1}\subset D_{2}
, Brownian motion in
D_{1}
started at
z\in D_{1}
and run until it exits
D_{1}
agrees in law with Brownian motion in
D_{2}
started at
z
and run until it exits
D_{1}
. Consequently
K
is an LQG-IG local set, so Theorem
3.19
gives the result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.14
.

The LQG metric ball
K
is an LQG-IG local set by the locality property of the LQG metric. Thus the claim follows from Theorem
3.19
.
∎

4
Independence of unexplored regions of the discretized LQG disk

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
3.5
which we used earlier to prove Theorem
3.3
. To that end we first rephrase it in a more convenient way, as Theorem
4.1
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
, let
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{UQD}_{{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
, and let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{D}
be a Poisson point process with intensity measure
\varepsilon^{-1}\mu_{\Phi}
. Independently of
(\Phi,\Lambda)
, let
(\mathbb{D},\Psi,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{IG}_{26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}}
and let
\eta^{\prime}
be the counterclockwise space-filling
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
measurable with respect to
\Psi
. Here, the LQG parameter
\gamma\in(0,2)
and SLE parameter
\kappa^{\prime}>4
satisfy
\kappa^{\prime}=\frac{16}{\gamma^{2}}
since
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}+(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}})=26
. Condition on
|\Lambda|>0
and write
N=|\Lambda|-1
.
The curve
\eta^{\prime}
is split by the points
\Lambda
to give domains
D_{i}
decorated by curve segments
\eta_{i}^{\prime}:[0,t_{i}]\to\overline{\mathbb{D}}
and boundary points
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq N+1
, as in Definition
3.1
. Recall

a_{i}=\eta^{\prime}_{i}(0)
and
d_{i}=\eta_{i}^{\prime}(t_{i})
, while
b_{i}
(resp.
c_{i}
) is the furthest point on the clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) arc of
\partial D_{i}
from
a_{i}
to
d_{i}
such that the boundary arc from
a_{i}
to
b_{i}
(resp.
c_{i}
) is a subset of
\overline{(\partial\mathbb{D}\cup\bigcup_{j\leq i}D_{i})}
.
See Figure
4
.

Independently of
(\Phi,\Psi,\eta^{\prime},\Lambda)
, sample a nonnegative integer
T\geq 0
with
\mathbb{P}[T=t]=2^{-t-1}
, let
I_{0}
be the set of indices
i
