such that
D_{i}\cap\partial\mathbb{D}\neq\emptyset
, and for
j\geq 0
inductively define
I_{j+1}
from
I_{j}
by independently sampling a point
p_{j+1}\in(\partial\bigcup_{i\in I_{j}}D_{i})\backslash\partial\mathbb{D}
from the LQG length measure, then setting
I_{j+1}=I_{j}\cup\{i\>:\>p_{j+1}\in\partial D_{i}\}
. We stop the process either at time
T
or at the time
t
when
\bigcup_{i\in I_{t}}D_{i}=\overline{\mathbb{D}}
, whichever is earlier; call this time
\tau
. Let

M_{\tau}:=\bigcup_{i\in I_{\tau}}D_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{M}_{\tau}=(M_{\tau},\Phi,\Psi,\{(\eta^{\prime}_{i},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})\>:\>i\in I_{\tau}\})/{\sim}.

(4.1)

See Figure
5
(right).

Theorem 4.1
.

In the setting immediately above, let
L_{1},\dots,L_{m}
be simple loops in the interior of
M_{\tau}
and let
O_{k}
be the bounded connected component of
\mathbb{C}\backslash L_{k}
. Suppose
(M_{\tau},\Phi,L_{1},\dots,L_{m})/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
, and the
O_{k}
are pairwise disjoint. Then conditioned on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
, the IG-decorated LQG surfaces
(O_{1},\Phi,\Psi)/{\sim},\dots,(O_{m},\Phi,\Psi)/{\sim}
are conditionally independent.

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

When
|\Lambda|>0

the claim is equivalent to Theorem
4.1
. When
|\Lambda|=0
the claim is vacuously true.
∎

Our proof of Theorem
4.1
involves non-probability measures. In the setting of probability theory, independent random variables
X
and
Y
have a joint law that factorizes as a product measure
\mu_{X}\times\mu_{Y}
. In the non-probability setting, the notion of independence is no longer well defined, but factorization still makes sense. For instance, Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
factorizes as a product of Lebesgue measures on
\mathbb{R}
. Our argument involves showing that certain measures, densities, and combinatorial structures factorize.

In Section
4.1
we explain the mating-of-trees theorem which identifies space-filling
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
-decorated
\gamma
-LQG disks with correlated 2D Brownian motion excursions. In Section
4.2
we use the Markov property of Brownian motion to decompose the decorated
\gamma
-LQG disk into “cells” which are conditionally independent given their boundary lengths. We also obtain a factorized form for the joint density of the boundary lengths. In Section
4.3
we define decorated planar maps which we call “quilts” whose faces correspond to cells and whose edges are labelled by the corresponding LQG lengths, and we obtain a factorization for the law of the edge lengths for a given planar map. In Section
4.4
we prove that conditioned on the existence of a specified subquilt, the complementary connected subquilts are conditionally independent. The inputs are the factorization from the previous section, a factorization of uniform measures, and a combinatorial factorization of planar maps whose proof is deferred to Section
4.5
. This last result is a generalization of Proposition
B.1
on the independence of arc diagrams of a uniform meander given its winding function. Finally in Section
4.6
we prove Theorem
4.1
.

4.1
Mating-of-trees for the LQG disk

In this section, we review the construction of Brownian excursions in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
, then recall in Proposition
4.2
the mating-of-trees theorem which identifies an SLE-decorated LQG disk with a Brownian excursion in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
.

We first recall Brownian motion in the cone. Let
\mathbbm{a}^{2}=2/\sin(\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})
be the mating-of-trees variance computed in
[
ARS21
, Theorem 1.3]
.
Consider Brownian motion
(L_{t},R_{t})
with
\Var(L_{t})=\Var(R_{t})=\mathbbm{a}^{2}t
and
\Cov(L_{t},R_{t})=-\cos(\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})\mathbbm{a}^{2}t
. Let
\mu(t;z)
be the law of Brownian motion started at
z
and run for time
t
, and let
\mu(t;z,w)
be the disintegration of
\mu(t;z)
over the endpoint of the Brownian motion, i.e.
\mu(t;z)=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu(t;z,w)\,dw
and the measure
\mu(t;z,w)
is supported on trajectories ending at
w
. We emphasize that
|\mu(t;z)|=1
(that is,
\mu(t;z)
is a probability measure) but
|\mu(t;z,w)|
is typically not 1 (rather,
|\mu(t;z,\cdot)|
is the density of a two-dimensional Gaussian random variable with mean
z
and covariance matrix
t\operatorname{Id}
). The Markov property of Brownian motion implies that for any
t_{1},t_{2}>0
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
we have
\mu(t_{1}+t_{2};z_{1},z_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu(t_{1};z_{1},w)\mu(t_{2};w,z_{2})\,dw
where the equality means that when we sample a pair of paths from the right hand side, concatenating them gives a sample from the left hand side.

We now recall the construction of non-probability measures which correspond to Brownian motion started at a vertex of a cone and restricted to staying in the cone.
Let
E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}
and
E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}
be the events that a Brownian motion trajectory stays in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
respectively.
[
Shi85
]
allows us to define the Brownian excursion in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and Brownian excursion in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
by

\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(t;0)=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\delta^{-1}(1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\mu(t;\delta)),\qquad\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t;0)=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\delta^{-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}}(1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}}\mu(t;\delta e^{i\pi/4})).

Here the limits are in the sense of weak convergence on
C([0,t],\mathbb{C})
equipped with the uniform topology.
Indeed, for the first limit, the existence of
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\delta^{-1}|1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\mu(t;\delta)|
is stated in
[
Shi85
, (4.1)]
, and the conditional law of
\mu(t;\delta)
given
E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}
has a limit as
\delta\to 0
by
[
Shi85
, Theorem 2]
. The second limit is likewise justified.
Define by disintegration the measures
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(t;0,z)
and
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t;0,z)
on the space of paths from
0
to
z
.

[
Shi85
, Theorem 2]
gives the following Markov properties:

\begin{gathered}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(t_{1}+t_{2};0,z)=1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(t_{1};0,w)\mu(t_{2};w,z)\,dw,\\
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t_{1}+t_{2};0,z)=1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t_{1};0,w)\mu(t_{2};w,z)\,dw.\end{gathered}

(4.2)

Here, on the right hand sides, the restriction to
E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}
or
E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}
applies to the path from
0
to
z
obtained by
concatenating
the pair of paths.
Note that the non-probability measures in these identities induce weightings. For instance, if we sample
(L_{t},R_{t})
from
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t_{1}+t_{2};0,z)
, then the law of
(L_{t},R_{t})|_{[0,t_{1}]}
is
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t_{1};0,w)\,dw
weighted by
\mu(t_{2};L_{t_{1}}+\mathbbm{i}R_{t_{1}},z)[E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}]
.

Finally, we define the Brownian excursion in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
from
\mathbbm{i}
to
0
via

\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t;\mathbbm{i},0):=1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{t}{2};\mathbbm{i},z}}\right)\times\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{t}{2};z,0}}\right)\,dz,

where we define
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(t/2;\mathbbm{i},z)
from
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(t/2;0,z-\mathbbm{i})
by translation, and define
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t/2;z,0)
from
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t/2;0,z)
by time-reversal. By its definition, the measure
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t;\mathbbm{i},0)
inherits an analogous Markov property from (
4.2
).

We can now state the mating-of-trees theorem for the LQG disk, see Figure
8
(left).
Recall that for a finite measure
M
the probability measure
M^{\#}
is defined to be
M/|M|
. The following mating-of-trees theorem was first shown by
[
DMS21
]
for the restricted range
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
, then proved in full by
[
AG21
]
.

Proposition 4.2

(
[
AG21
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,2)
and
\kappa^{\prime}=\frac{16}{\gamma^{2}}
.
Let
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-\mathbbm{i})
be an embedding of a
\gamma
-LQG disk conditioned to have unit boundary length, and let
\eta^{\prime}
be an independent counterclockwise space-filling
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
in
(\mathbb{D},-\mathbbm{i})
. Parametrize
\eta^{\prime}
by
\mu_{\Phi}
-area, and let
L_{t}
(resp.
R_{t}
) be the LQG length of the counterclockwise (resp. clockwise) boundary arc of
\mathbb{D}\backslash\eta^{\prime}([0,t])
from
-\mathbbm{i}
to
\eta^{\prime}(t)
. Then the law of the process
t\mapsto(L_{t},R_{t})
is
(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t;\mathbbm{i},0)\,dt)^{\#}
. See Figure
8
(left).

Figure 8:

Left:
A
\gamma
-LQG disk decorated by an independent counter-clockwise space-filling
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
curve
\eta^{\prime}
where
\kappa^{\prime}=\frac{16}{\gamma^{2}}
. Parametrizing
\eta^{\prime}
by LQG area,
L_{t}
and
R_{t}
are the left and right LQG boundary lengths of the region not yet explored by
\eta^{\prime}
at time
t
.
Middle:
A Poisson point process with intensity given by the LQG area measure. Here it has two points, cutting
\eta^{\prime}
into three segments, which in order of traversal are red, green and blue. We mark out some LQG lengths for each region. Note that when
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
these segments are not simply connected, see Figure
4
(right).
Right:
The two points cut the Brownian motion into three paths. The lengths
(\ell_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{-},r_{0}^{+})
,
(\ell_{1}^{-},\ell_{1}^{+},r_{1}^{-},r_{1}^{+})
and
(\ell_{2}^{-},r_{2}^{-})
correspond to the lengths marked by arrows.

4.2
Decomposition into cells and factorization

In the setting of Theorem
4.1
, for
\gamma\in(0,\sqrt{2}]
let
\mathcal{C}_{i}:=(D_{i},\Phi,\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})/{\sim}
be the
i
th (simply connected) curve-decorated LQG surface explored by the curve; a similar definition is given below for
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
.
The goal of this section is to prove that the side lengths of
\mathcal{C}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{N+1}
have a joint probability density function that factors, and given these side lengths the
\mathcal{C}_{i}
are conditionally independent (Proposition
4.3
). In brief, this is a consequence of Proposition
4.2
and the Markov property of Brownian motion.

For
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is more complicated for topological reasons: the curve
\eta_{i}^{\prime}
fills out a countable chain of simply-connected domains, each parametrizing a curve-decorated LQG surface. See Figure
4
(right). Let
\mathcal{C}_{i}
be the decorated generalized LQG surface given by the chain of curve-decorated LQG surfaces, further decorated by boundary points
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}
. Note that
(D_{i},\Phi,\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})/{\sim}
is obtained from
\mathcal{C}_{i}
by identifying pairs of boundary points of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

The decorated LQG surfaces
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{N}
each have four marked points,
\mathcal{C}_{0}
has three marked points (since
a_{0}=b_{0}
), and
\mathcal{C}_{N+1}
has two marked points (since
b_{N+1}=c_{N+1}=d_{N+1}
). Given
\mathcal{C}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{N+1}
, we can recover
(\mathbb{D},\Phi,-i,\eta^{\prime},\Lambda_{\varepsilon})/{\sim}
by conformal welding. See for instance the discussion on conformal welding in
[
DMS21
, Section 3.5]
.

Let
(\ell_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{-})
be the boundary arc lengths of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
ordered clockwise from
a_{0}
, for
1\leq i\leq N
let
(\ell_{i}^{-},\ell_{i}^{+},r_{i}^{+},r_{i}^{-})
be the boundary arc lengths of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
ordered clockwise from
a_{i}
, and let
(\ell_{N+1}^{-},r_{N+1}^{-})
be the boundary arc lengths of
\mathcal{C}_{N+1}
ordered clockwise from
a_{N+1}
. See Figure
8
(middle).
These lengths can be expressed explicitly in terms of the corresponding Brownian motion increments, see (
4.9
) below.

Since the Brownian motion remains in the cone, restricting to
\{N=n\}
, we have the constraints

\displaystyle\ell_{0}^{+}+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(\ell_{j}^{+}-\ell_{j}^{-})-\ell_{i}^{-}>0\>\>\text{ for }1\leq i\leq n,\>\>\quad 1+\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}(r_{j}^{+}-r_{j}^{-})-r_{i}^{-}>0\>\>\text{ for }0\leq i\leq n,

(4.3)

\displaystyle\ell_{n+1}^{-}=\ell_{0}^{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\ell_{i}^{+}-\ell_{i}^{-}),\qquad\qquad r_{n+1}^{-}=1+\sum_{i=0}^{n}(r_{i}^{+}-r_{i}^{-}).

(4.4)

Let
S_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4})^{n}
be the set of lengths
((\ell_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{-},r_{0}^{+})
,
(\ell_{i}^{-},\ell_{i}^{+},r_{i}^{-},r_{i}^{+})_{i\leq n})
satisfying (
4.3
).
Let
\mathrm{Leb}_{S_{n}}
be Lebesgue measure on
S_{n}
.
The goal of this section is to prove the following factorized description of the joint law of these lengths, and the conditional independence of the
\mathcal{C}_{i}
given these lengths.

Proposition 4.3
.

There exists a constant
Z_{\varepsilon}>0
and functions
P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{init}},P^{\varepsilon},P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{end}}
such that the law of
((\ell_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{-},r_{0}^{+})
,
(\ell_{i}^{-},\ell_{i}^{+},r_{i}^{-},r_{i}^{+})_{1\leq i\leq N})
is
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{n}
, where, defining
\ell_{n+1}^{-}
and
r_{n+1}^{-}
via (
4.4
),

\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}_{n}:=Z_{\varepsilon}^{-1}P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{init}}(\ell_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{-},r_{0}^{+})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}P^{\varepsilon}(\ell_{i}^{-},\ell_{i}^{+},r_{i}^{-},r_{i}^{+})\times P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{end}}(\ell_{n+1}^{-},r_{n+1}^{-})\,\mathrm{Leb}_{S_{n}}.

(4.5)

Conditioned on
\{N=n\}
and on these
4n+3
lengths, the LQG surfaces
\mathcal{C}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
are conditionally independent, and the conditional law of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
depends only on
(\ell_{i}^{-},\ell_{i}^{+},r_{i}^{-},r_{i}^{+})
(where we write
\ell_{0}^{-}=\ell_{n+1}^{+}=r_{n+1}^{+}=0
).

In our proof, we will obtain explicit descriptions of
P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{init}},P^{\varepsilon}
and
P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{end}}
in terms of Brownian motion, but these are not important for later arguments.
The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

First, the Markov property of Brownian motion gives the following. Let
n\geq 0
and
t_{0},\dots,t_{n+1}>0
, then

\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}t_{i};\mathbbm{i},0}}\right)=1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n+1}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(t_{0};\mathbbm{i},z_{1})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mu(t_{i};z_{i},z_{i+1})}}\right)\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t_{n+1};z_{n+1},0)\,\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}dz_{i}.

Notice that the measures
\mu(t_{i};z_{i},z_{i+1})
and
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(t_{0};\mathbbm{i},z_{1})
are not supported on the space of paths in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
, but the indicator
1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}}
enforces that we only consider concatenated paths that stay in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
. We now rephrase in terms of the increments of the Brownian motion. Setting
w_{0}=z_{1}-\mathbbm{i}
and
w_{i}=z_{i+1}-z_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
,

\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}t_{i};\mathbbm{i},0}}\right)=1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(t_{0};0,w_{0})\,dw_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu(t_{i};0,w_{i})\,dw_{i}}}\right)\,\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\biggl(t_{n+1};\sum_{j=0}^{n}w_{j}+\mathbbm{i},0\biggr).

(4.6)

That is, if we translate and concatenate the
(n+2)
paths of a sample from the right hand side, we get a single path from
\mathbbm{i}
to
0
whose law is
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}t_{i};\mathbbm{i},0)
.

Equation (
4.6
) explains how to decompose a Brownian excursion of duration
t=\sum_{i=0}^{n+1}t_{i}
into
n+2
trajectories of durations
t_{0},\dots,t_{n+1}
. Next, we will choose
t
randomly, then partition
[0,t]
using a Poisson point process.
Let
\mathrm{Exp}_{\lambda}(dx)=1_{x>0}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}
be the law of the exponential random variable with intensity
\lambda
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Sample
t\sim\mathrm{Leb}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
, let
\Lambda
be a Poisson point process on
[0,t]
with intensity
\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{Leb}_{[0,t]}
, and let
t_{0},\dots,t_{m}
be the successive lengths of the intervals comprising
[0,t]\backslash\Lambda
. Then the law of
(t_{0},\dots,t_{m})
is
\varepsilon\sum_{m\geq 0}\prod_{i=0}^{m}\mathrm{Exp}_{1/\varepsilon}(dt_{i})
.

Proof.

Let
P
denote the law of a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with intensity
\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{Leb}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
.
Palm’s theorem for Poisson point processes
[
Kal17
, Page 5]
implies the following two procedures give the same law of pairs
(\Lambda,t)
where
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
is countable and
t\in\Lambda
:

•

Sample
(t,\Lambda_{0})\sim(\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{Leb}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}})\times P
, and let
\Lambda=\Lambda_{0}\cup\{t\}
.

•

Sample
(M,\Lambda)\sim\mathrm{Count}_{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\times P
, and let
t
be the
M
th smallest element of
\Lambda
. Here,
\mathrm{Count}_{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
denotes the counting measure on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}=\{1,2,\dots\}
.

Note that if
\Lambda\sim P
, the law of the lengths of successive intervals comprising
\mathbb{R}_{+}\backslash\Lambda
is
(\mathrm{Exp}_{1/\varepsilon})^{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
. Thus, multiplying both laws by
\varepsilon
and looking at the laws of the intervals of
[0,t]\backslash\Lambda
gives the result.
∎

Write
\mu^{\varepsilon}(z,w):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu(t;z,w)\mathrm{Exp}_{1/\varepsilon}(dt)
and similarly define
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}^{\varepsilon}
.
Using Lemma
4.4
then (
4.6
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t;\mathbbm{i},0)\,dt=\varepsilon\sum_{m\geq 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m+1}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}t_{i};\mathbbm{i},0}}\right)\,\prod_{i=0}^{m}\mathrm{Exp}_{1/\varepsilon}(dt_{i})

(4.7)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbbm{i},0)+\varepsilon\sum_{n\geq 0}1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}^{\varepsilon}(0,w_{0})\,dw_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu^{\varepsilon}(0,w_{i})\,dw_{i}}}\right)\,\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}^{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}w_{j}+\mathbbm{i},0}}\right).

Equation (
4.7
) says that if we take the total time duration
t
to be “sampled” from Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, then the Brownian excursion measure
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}(t;\mathbbm{i},0)
can be written as a countable sum of measures indexed by the total number of points
m\geq 0
in the Poisson point process. For each of these measures for
m\geq 1
(
n=m-1\geq 0
), the Brownian excursion can be decomposed as the union of
n+2
sub-paths. We can sample these
n+2
sub-paths as Brownian bridges with exponential durations, restricted to the event that they stay in the cone.

Proposition 4.5
.

In the setting of Theorem
4.1
,
let
L_{t}
(resp.
R_{t}
) be the LQG length of the counterclockwise (resp. clockwise) boundary arc of
\mathbb{D}\backslash\eta^{\prime}([0,t])
from
-\mathbbm{i}
to
\eta^{\prime}(t)
. Writing
T_{i}=\sum_{j<i}t_{j}
, for
0\leq i\leq N
define
(L_{t}^{i},R_{t}^{i})=(L_{T_{i}+t}-L_{T_{i}},R_{T_{i}+t}-R_{T_{i}})
for
t\in[0,t_{i}]
, and define
(L_{t}^{N+1},R_{t}^{N+1})=(L_{T_{N+1}+t}-L_{T_{N+1}+t_{N+1}},R_{T_{N+1}+t}-R_{T_{N+1}+t_{N+1}})
for
t\in[0,t_{N+1}]
.
Then the joint law of
(L_{t}^{0},R_{t}^{0}),\dots,(L_{t}^{N+1},R_{t}^{N+1})
equals, for some constant
{Z_{\varepsilon}}
,

{Z_{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\sum_{n\geq 0}1_{E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}^{\varepsilon}(0,w_{0})\,dw_{0}}}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu^{\varepsilon}(0,w_{i})dw_{i}}}\right)\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}^{\varepsilon}\biggl(\sum_{j=0}^{n}w_{j}+\mathbbm{i},0\biggr).

(4.8)

Proof.

We use Proposition
4.2
. Let
t=\mu_{h}(\mathbb{D})
. Since
\eta^{\prime}
is parametrized by LQG area, it is a measure-preserving transform from
([0,t],\mathrm{Leb}_{[0,t]})
to
(\mathbb{D},\mu_{h})
, so if
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{D}
is a Poisson point process with intensity
\varepsilon^{-1}\mu_{h}
, then
\eta^{-1}(\Lambda)
is a Poisson point process on
[0,t]
with intensity
\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{Leb}_{[0,t]}
. Restricting to the event that there are at least two cells corresponds to discarding the term
\varepsilon\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}^{\varepsilon}(i,0)
in (
4.7
). This gives the result with
{Z_{\varepsilon}}
the normalization constant making (
4.8
) a probability measure.
∎

We now define the functions
P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{init}},P^{\varepsilon},P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{end}}
, then Proposition
4.3
is essentially an immediate consequence of Proposition
4.5
.
For a Brownian excursion
(L_{t},R_{t})
sampled from
\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu^{\varepsilon}(0,w)\,dw
whose (random) duration we call
T
, let
P^{\varepsilon}
be the density function for

(-\inf_{t\in[0,T]}L_{t},L_{T}-\inf_{t\in[0,T]}L_{t},-\inf_{t\in[0,T]}R_{t},R_{T}-\inf_{t\in[0,T]}R_{t})
.
For
(L_{t},R_{t})
sampled from
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}^{\varepsilon}(0,w)\,dw
with random duration
T
, let
P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{init}}
be the density function for
(L_{T},-\inf_{[0,t]}R_{t},R_{T}-\inf_{[0,t]}R_{t})
.
Let
P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{end}}(\ell^{-},r^{-})=|\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}^{\varepsilon}(\ell^{-}+\mathbbm{i}r^{-},0)|
for
\ell^{-},r^{-}>0
.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Recall the processes
(L_{t}^{0},R_{t}^{0}),\dots,(L_{t}^{N+1},R_{t}^{N+1})
defined in Proposition
4.5
. Restrict to the event
\{N=n\}
.
We can describe the lengths
\ell_{i}^{\pm},r_{i}^{\pm}
in terms of these processes:

\begin{gathered}\ell_{0}^{+}=L^{0}_{t_{0}},\quad r_{0}^{-}=-\inf_{t\leq t_{0}}R^{0}_{t},\quad r_{0}^{+}=R_{t_{0}}^{0}+r_{0}^{-},\quad\ell_{n+1}^{-}=L_{0}^{n+1},\quad r_{n+1}^{-}=R_{0}^{n+1},\\
\ell_{i}^{-}=-\inf_{t\leq t_{i}}L^{i}_{t},\quad\ell_{i}^{+}=L^{i}_{t_{i}}+\ell_{i}^{-},\quad r_{i}^{-}=-\inf_{t\leq t_{i}}R^{i}_{t},\quad r_{i}^{+}=R^{i}_{t_{i}}+r_{i}^{-}\qquad\text{ for }i=1,\dots,n.\end{gathered}

(4.9)

Now, since the Brownian motion event
E_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}
corresponds to the lengths lying in
S_{n}
, by Proposition
4.5
the law of the lengths
(\ell_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{-},r_{0}^{+})
,
(\ell_{i}^{-},\ell_{i}^{+},r_{i}^{-},r_{i}^{+})_{i\leq n}
is (
4.5
).

Next, we study the conditional law of the
\mathcal{C}_{i}
given the lengths. Let
\ell^{\pm},r^{\pm}>0
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{init}}^{\ell^{+},r^{-},r^{+}}
be the law of
(L_{t},R_{t})_{[0,T]}
sampled from
\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}^{\varepsilon}(0,w)\,dw
and conditioned on
\{\ell^{+}=L_{T},\>r^{-}=-\inf_{t\leq T}R_{t},\>r^{+}=R_{T}+r^{-}\}
. Let
\mathcal{P}^{\ell^{-},\ell^{+},r^{-},r^{+}}
be the law of
(L_{t},R_{t})_{[0,T]}
sampled from
\int_{\mathbb{C}}\mu^{\varepsilon}(0,w)\,dw
and conditioned on
\{\ell^{-}=-\inf_{t\leq T}L_{t},\>\ell^{+}=L_{T}+\ell^{-},\>r^{-}=-\inf_{t\leq T}R_{t},\>r^{+}=R_{T}+r^{-}\}
. Let
\mathcal{P}^{\ell^{-},r^{-}}_{\mathrm{end}}
be the probability measure proportional to
\mu_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}^{\varepsilon}(\ell^{-}+\mathbbm{i}r^{-},0)
.

By Proposition
4.5
,
conditioned on
N=n
and on the lengths
(\ell_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{-},r_{0}^{+})
,
(\ell_{i}^{-},\ell_{i}^{+},r_{i}^{-},r_{i}^{+})_{1\leq i\leq n}
,
(\ell_{n+1}^{-},r_{n+1}^{-})
, the processes
(L_{t}^{0},R_{t}^{0}),\dots,(L_{t}^{n+1},R_{t}^{n+1})
have the conditional law
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{init}}^{\ell_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{-},r_{0}^{+}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}^{\ell_{n}^{-},\ell_{n}^{+},r_{n}^{-},r_{n}^{+}}\times\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{end}}^{\ell_{n+1}^{-},r_{n+1}^{-}}
. By, e.g.,
[
AG21
, Lemma 2.17]
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is measurable with respect to
(L_{t}^{i},R_{t}^{i})
for each
i=0,\dots,n+1
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.3
The planar map structure of the discretized LQG disk

In this section, we introduce
templates
and
quilts
to describe the decorated planar map structure of the discretized LQG disk, see Figure
9
. The main result of this section is a factorization result for quilts (Lemma
4.9
), which arises from our factorization for sequences of cells (Proposition
4.3
).

Consider planar maps with the sphere topology, with a possibly empty subset of faces marked as holes; in what follows we will only use the term “face” to refer to non-hole faces. A
template

T
is such a planar map such that on each face we mark one or more vertices, one of which is distinguished as the root vertex for the face. Note that if
v
is a marked vertex for one face, it need not be a marked vertex for other faces.
We call a face
F
of
T
a
k
-gon
if it has
k
marked vertices, and we call a collection of edges between consecutive marked vertices of
F
a
side
of
F
. We denote the edge set of the planar map
T
by
E_{T}
.
A
quilt
is obtained from a template by assigning a positive length to each edge of
E_{T}
; the set of all quilts with template
T
can can be identified with
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{T}}
.

Figure 9:

Left:
Picture of a template with one 1-gon (shaded), one 2-gon, one 3-gon and
n=21
4-gons. Each face has a root vertex (black) and possibly other marked vertices (pink). For
T\in\mathcal{T}
we can order the polygons (red curve passing through
F_{0},F_{1},\dots,F_{n},F_{n+1}
).
Middle:
The template is a planar map with vertices shown in black. Its edge set is called
E_{T}
.
A quilt can be obtained from the template by assigning a positive length to each edge. If
T\in\mathcal{T}
we can color the left (resp. right) boundary arcs of each face red (resp. blue) to get a tree and a cycle-rooted forest.
Right:
Figure for Lemma
4.9
. Order
E_{T}^{\ell}
(red edges) by the first time an edge is traversed in a counter-clockwise contour around the tree. Each black arrow corresponds to a left side of a face of
T
. Each arrow traverses one or more edges, the earliest of which is bolded. The key to proving Lemma
4.9
is the observation that each edge is bolded in exactly one arrow.

In the setting of Theorem
4.1
, the curve-decorated LQG disk is split into decorated LQG surfaces
\mathcal{C}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{N+1}
(defined in Section
4.2
) parametrized by the regions
D_{i}
traced out by the space-filling SLE between times when it hits the Poisson point process.
We introduce templates and quilts to describe the planar map underlying this discretization, see Figure
8
(middle). We will construct a template with faces
F_{0},\dots,F_{n+1}
corresponding to the LQG surfaces
\mathcal{C}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
respectively, and a final exterior face
F_{\mathrm{ext}}
. The marked points of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
correspond to the marked vertices of
F_{i}
, so the faces are a 2-gon, a 3-gon, and
n
4-gons. For a 3-gon, we call the vertex clockwise of the root vertex the terminal vertex, and for a 2-gon or 4-gon we call the vertex opposite the root vertex the terminal vertex.
The root and terminal vertices of
F_{i}
correspond to the starting and ending points of the SLE curve in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

When
\gamma\in(0,\sqrt{2}]
, this quilt is obtained as follows. Define the faces
(F_{\mathrm{ext}},F_{0},\dots,F_{N+1})=(\hat{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathbb{D},D_{0},\dots,D_{N+1})
and the vertex set
\bigcup_{i=0}^{N+1}\{a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}\}
, where the points
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}\in\partial D_{i}
are as in Theorem
4.1
. The edges are the connected components of
(\partial\mathbb{D}\cup\bigcup_{i=0}^{N+1}\partial D_{i})\backslash(\bigcup_{i=0}^{N+1}\{a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}\})
, and each is labeled with its LQG length. Each
F_{i}
is given the marked vertices
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}\in\partial D_{i}
as defined in Theorem
4.1
, the root vertex of
F_{i}
is
a_{i}
, and
F_{\mathrm{ext}}
is a 1-gon with root vertex
a_{0}
. See Figure
9
.

We now give an equivalent iterative definition which applies for all
\gamma\in(0,2)
. Let
a_{0}=b_{0}=-\mathbbm{i}
,
c_{0}=1
and
d_{0}=0
. Draw radii from
d_{0}
to
b_{0}
and
c_{0}
to cut
\mathbb{D}
into a quarter-circle
F_{0}
and a three-quarter-circle
U_{1}
. Consider the quilt with three faces: the 1-gon
F_{\mathrm{ext}}=\hat{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\mathbb{D}
with root vertex
a_{0}
, the 3-gon
F_{0}
with root vertex
a_{0}
and remaining marked vertices
c_{0},d_{0}
, and the 2-gon
U_{1}
with root vertex
d_{0}
and other marked vertex
-\mathbbm{i}
. Assign lengths to the edges such that the side lengths of
\partial F_{0}
correspond to the side lengths of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, and the sum of lengths along
\partial\mathbb{D}
is 1.
For
j=1,\dots,N
, we iteratively do the following. Let
\ell_{j}^{-},\ell_{j}^{+},r_{j}^{-},r_{j}^{+}
be the boundary lengths of
\mathcal{C}_{j}
as defined in Section
4.2
. Let
a_{j}=d_{j-1}
. Add a vertex
b_{j}
on the clockwise arc of
\partial U_{j}
from
a_{j}
to
-\mathbbm{i}
such that, if the previous vertex on this arc is
v
and the next vertex is
v^{\prime}
, the sum of lengths from
a_{j}
to
v
is less than
\ell_{j}^{-}
and the sum of lengths from
a_{j}
to
v^{\prime}
is greater than
\ell_{j}^{-}
. Split the edge between
v
and
v^{\prime}
at
b_{j}
, with lengths assigned such that the sum of lengths from
a_{j}
to
b_{j}
is
\ell_{j}^{-}
.
Likewise add a vertex
c_{j}
on the counterclockwise arc from
a_{j}
to
-\mathbbm{i}
such that the sum of lengths from
a_{i}
to
c_{i}
is
r_{j}^{-}
. Finally, mark a bulk point
d_{j}\in U_{j}
, add edges from
d_{j}
to
b_{j},c_{j}
, and give them lengths
\ell_{j}^{+}
and
r_{j}^{+}
respectively. This splits
U_{j}
into two faces; let
U_{j+1}
be the face having
-\mathbbm{i}
on its boundary and
F_{j}
the other face. Let
F_{j}
have root
a_{j}
and remaining marked vertices
b_{j},c_{j},d_{j}
, and let
U_{j+1}
have root vertex
d_{j}
and other marked vertex
-\mathbbm{i}
. After carrying this procedure out for
j=1,\dots,N
, we are left with a quilt with faces
F_{\mathrm{ext}},F_{0},\dots,F_{N},U_{N+1}
; rename
U_{N+1}
to
F_{N+1}
to complete the definition.

Remark 4.6

(Relationship with mated-CRT map)
.

An equivalent way to define the random quilt arising in the setting of Theorem
4.1
is as follows. Our setting is closely related to that of the mated-CRT map (see
[
GHS20
]
), except we discretize via a Poisson point process rather than at equal intervals (see Remark
3.4
). We can construct a variant of the mated-CRT map from the discretization in exactly the same way; in this map, each
D_{i}
corresponds to a vertex. Take the dual of this mated-CRT map variant, and for each edge between a pair of consecutive faces (i.e. faces traced successively by the space-filling curve), split it by adding a degree 2 vertex. Marked vertices and lengths can be assigned to obtain the quilt.

We now describe the set of templates
\mathcal{T}
that can arise from the discretized LQG disk by the above correspondence. See Figure
9
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be the collection of templates
T
with the following desciption:
T
has no holes and the face set of
T
can be called
F_{\mathrm{ext}},F_{\mathrm{0}},F_{1},\dots,F_{n},F_{n+1}
for some
n\geq 0
. The face
F_{\mathrm{ext}}
is a 1-gon,
F_{0}
is a 3-gon, the faces
F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
are 4-gons, and
F_{n+1}
is a 2-gon. Moreover,

(a)

The root vertices of
F_{\mathrm{ext}}
and
F_{0}
agree, and the side of
F_{0}
counterclockwise of the root vertex lies on
\partial F_{\mathrm{ext}}
.

(b)

For
i=0,1,\dots,n
the terminal vertex of
F_{i}
is the root vertex of
F_{i+1}
, and the terminal vertex of
F_{n+1}
is the root vertex of
F_{0}
. (This corresponds to the endpoint of the SLE curve in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
agreeing with the starting point of the curve in
\mathcal{C}_{i+1}
.)

(c)

For
i=1,\dots,n+1
the root vertex of
F_{i}
has degree 2, and all other marked vertices of
F_{i}
have degree 3. (This corresponds to the non-consecutive
\mathcal{C}_{i}
intersecting generically almost surely, see e.g.
[
GMS19
, Figure 1]
.)

(d)

For
i=1,\dots,n
, the two sides of
F_{i}
adjacent to its root vertex both lie on
\partial(F_{\mathrm{ext}}\cup\bigcup_{j=0}^{i-1}F_{j})
, while the other two sides of
F_{i}
only intersect
\partial(F_{\mathrm{ext}}\cup\bigcup_{j=0}^{i-1}F_{j})
at endpoints. (This corresponds to the definition of the marked points of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
.)

For
T\in\mathcal{T}
let
\mathcal{Q}_{T}
be the set of all quilts with template
T
.

By the correspondences described in Definition
4.7
between (
b
)-(
d
) and the properties of
(\mathcal{C}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{N+1})
,
there is a.s. a unique template
T\in\mathcal{T}
describing the discretized LQG disk via the correspondence stated right above Definition
4.7
. Thus, there is a.s. a unique quilt
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{T}
such that the side lengths of
F_{i}
agree with those of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq N+1
. Let
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
denote the law of the random quilt
Q
arising from the discretization of the LQG disk, so
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
is a probability measure on
\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{Q}_{T}
. (This is a slight abuse of notation since Proposition
4.3
defines
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
to be the law of the side lengths of
\mathcal{C}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{N+1}
, but these lengths encode the same information as
Q
.)

For each
T\in\mathcal{T}
we denote the restriction of
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
to
\mathcal{Q}_{T}
by
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{\mathcal{Q}_{T}}
; this is a finite non-probability measure. We will also need the following notation.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
\mathrm{Unif}_{T}
be the infinite measure on
\mathcal{Q}_{T}
given by Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{T}}
.

We give a factorization for quilts with template
T
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Fix a template
T\in\mathcal{T}
with
n+3
faces
F_{\mathrm{ext}},F_{0},\dots,F_{n+1}
. For a quilt
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{T}
, let
C_{i}
be the tuple of side lengths of face
F_{i}
, and define
w(Q):=Z_{\varepsilon}^{-1}P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{init}}(C_{0})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}P^{\varepsilon}(C_{i})\times P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{end}}(C_{n+1})
with
Z_{\varepsilon},P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{init}},P^{\varepsilon},P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{end}}
as in (
4.5
). Then

\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{\mathcal{Q}_{T}}(dQ)=w(Q)\mathrm{Unif}_{T}(dQ).

Proof.

Recall
S_{n}
from (
4.5
) which parametrizes the space of quilts from
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
with
n+3
faces via the lengths
(\ell_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{-},r_{0}^{+}),(\ell_{i}^{-},\ell_{i}^{+},r_{i}^{-},r_{i}^{+})_{1\leq i\leq n}
. Let
S_{T}\subset S_{n}
be the set of lengths such that the resulting quilt has template
T
. Recall that
E_{T}
is the edge set of
T
and that a quilt with template
T
is identified with its collection of edge lengths
(x_{e})_{e\in E_{T}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{T}}
. Consider the affine bijection
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{T}}\to S_{T}
sending
(x_{e})_{e\in E_{T}}\mapsto(\ell_{0}^{+},r_{0}^{-},r_{0}^{+}),(\ell_{i}^{-},\ell_{i}^{+},r_{i}^{-},r_{i}^{+})_{i\leq n}
. We will give a combinatorial argument that shows the Jacobian matrix of
f
has unit determinant, so
f^{-1}_{*}\mathrm{Leb}_{S_{T}}=\mathrm{Unif}_{T}
; combining with (
4.5
) then gives the desired result.

For
1\leq i\leq n
, for the 4-gon
F_{i}
let
L_{i}^{-}
be the side clockwise of the root vertex and
L_{i}^{+}
the side clockwise of
L_{i}^{-}
.
(Under the correspondence described in Definition
4.7
,
L_{i}^{-}
and
L_{i}^{+}
correspond to the lower left and upper left marked boundary arcs of the
i
th space-filling SLE segment, respectively.)
Let
L_{0}^{+}
be the side of
F_{0}
clockwise of its root vertex. For
(i,\pm)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\{+,-\}
and
(i,\pm)=(0,+)
, let
E_{i}^{\pm}\subset E_{T}
be the set of edges that lie on the side
L_{i}^{\pm}
. Let
E_{T}^{\ell}=\bigcup_{i,\pm}E_{i}^{\pm}
. See Figure
9
(right).
By definition
\ell_{i}^{\pm}=\sum_{e\in E_{i}^{\pm}}x_{e}
; this gives the affine bijection between
(\ell_{0}^{+},\ell_{1}^{-},\ell_{1}^{+},\dots,\ell_{n}^{-},\ell_{n}^{+})
and
(x_{e})_{e\in E_{T}^{\ell}}
.
We note for later use that this bijection implies
|E_{T}^{\ell}|=2n+1
.

The edges
E_{T}^{\ell}
form a tree rooted at the root vertex
v_{0}
of
F_{0}
. (This tree corresponds to the tree of flow lines forming the left boundaries of the mated-CRT map cells.)
Consider the counterclockwise contour around this tree starting at
v_{0}
, i.e., the depth-first exploration starting at
v_{0}
. This contour traverses each edge of
E_{T}^{\ell}
twice, once in each of the two possible directions. Furthermore, this contour passes through the faces in increasing order, see Figure
9
(left) and (middle). This follows from property (
b
) of Definition
4.7
.
Thus, it traverses the sides
L_{0}^{+},L_{1}^{-},L_{1}^{+},\dots,L_{n}^{+}
in order (tracing the edges on each
+
/
-
side in the direction of increasing/decreasing distance from
v_{0}
in the tree), then traces the left side of
F_{n+1}
to return to
v_{0}
, see Figure
9
(right). We assign an ordering
\prec
to
E_{T}^{\ell}
by the time an edge is
first
visited by the contour.

We claim that for each
e\in E_{T}^{\ell}
, there is some
(i,\pm)
such that
e\in E_{i}^{\pm}
and
e
is the
\prec
-earliest edge in
E_{i}^{\pm}
, see Figure
9
(right).
Let
j
be the index such that
e\in E_{j}^{+}
. If
e
is the
\prec
-earliest edge of
E_{j}^{+}
(i.e. the edge of
L_{j}^{+}
closest to
v_{0}
) we are done. Otherwise, let
v
be the endpoint of
e
closer to
v_{0}
. By (
c
), every internal vertex of the tree has degree 3, and, in our counterclockwise traversal, after tracing
L_{j}^{+}
we have only visited two of the edges adjacent to
v
. Let
k>j
be the index such that the side
L_{k}^{+}
is traversed starting from
v
. Then
v
is the endpoint of
L_{k}^{-}
, so
e
is the
\prec
-earliest edge of
E_{k}^{-}
.
Thus the claim holds. Further, since
|E_{T}^{\ell}|=2n+1=|\{L_{0}^{+},L_{1}^{-},\dots,L_{n}^{+}\}|
, each
e
is the
\prec
-earliest edge of exactly one
L_{i}^{\pm}
.

Consequently, if we order
E_{T}^{\ell}
by
\prec
and order the sides
L_{i}^{\pm}
by the
\prec
-earliest edge they contain, the zero-one matrix sending
(x_{e})_{e\in E_{T}^{\ell}}
to
(\ell_{0}^{+},\ell_{1}^{-},\ell_{1}^{+},\dots,\ell_{n}^{-},\ell_{n}^{+})
is upper triangular with ones on the diagonal, and so has determinant
1
.
A similar argument shows the zero-one matrix
sending
(x_{e})_{e\in E_{T}\backslash E_{T}^{\ell}}
to
(r_{0}^{-},r_{0}^{+},r_{1}^{-},r_{1}^{+},\dots,r_{n}^{-},r_{n}^{+})
has unit determinant. Thus
f
has unit determinant as needed. ∎

4.4
Independence of complementary subquilts

Recall
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
is the probability measure on quilts arising from discretized LQG disks (see Lemma
4.9
). The goal of this section is to show Proposition
4.10
: informally, a quilt sampled from
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
conditioned on having a specific subquilt has conditionally independent complementary subquilts. The proof uses our earlier factorization for quilts with a specified template (Lemma
4.9
), a combinatorial factorization for the set of templates having a fixed subtemplate (Proposition
4.12
) whose proof we defer to Section
4.5
, and a factorization of Lebesgue measures (Lemma
4.13
).

Let
T^{*}
be a template in
\mathcal{T}
with a connected subset of faces marked. The marked faces give rise to another template as follows: say two faces are adjacent if their boundaries share an edge, replace each connected component of the non-marked faces with a hole, then delete every vertex except marked vertices of the faces and vertices adjacent to multiple faces. We call this the marked
subtemplate
of
T^{*}
. Each edge of the marked subtemplate corresponds to the union of one or more edges of the quilt (since deleting vertices amounts to merging edges).

Given a quilt
Q\in\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{Q}_{T}
, if we mark a connected subset of its faces, the marked
subquilt
is the quilt whose template is the marked subtemplate and whose lengths are inherited from
Q
. We say that a quilt
M
is a subquilt of
Q
if one can mark a subset of faces of
Q
so that the marked subquilt agrees with
M
. See Figure
10
(a),(b).

Fix a connected template
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
with
b\geq 1
holes labelled
1,\dots,b
, whose faces are all 4-gons except for a 1-gon, 2-gon and 3-gon. Suppose further that
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
can be realized as a marked subtemplate of a template in
\mathcal{T}
, see Figure
10
(a)-(c), so in particular its holes share no common boundary edges. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}
be the set of quilts having template
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
and whose 1-gon has length 1. The goal of this section is the following.

Proposition 4.10
.

Recall the probability measure
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
on quilts arising from discretized LQG disks in Lemma
4.9
.
For fixed
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
, the following holds for all
M\in\mathcal{Q}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}
. Sample a quilt
Q
from
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
conditioned on
\{Q\text{ has }M\text{ as a subquilt}\}
, and let
Q_{i}
be the subquilt of
Q
obtained by marking the faces of
Q
in the
i
th hole of
M
. Then
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{b}
are conditionally independent.

Implicitly, the proposition statement uses the fact that one can make sense of conditioning on the measure zero event of having a specific subquilt; this will be shown in the proof.

Equip
\mathcal{Q}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}
with the metric
d(M^{1},M^{2})=\sum_{e}|x_{e}^{1}-x_{e}^{2}|
, where the summation is taken over edges of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
and
x_{e}^{i}
is the length of
e
in the quilt
M^{i}
. Let
B_{\delta}(M^{0})
denote the radius
\delta
ball with respect to this metric.

Proposition 4.11
.

For fixed
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
as above, there is a family of finite measures
\{m^{\varepsilon}_{M}\}_{M\in\mathcal{Q}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}}
on
\bigcup_{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{Q}_{T}
such that the following holds.

•

The measure
m_{M}^{\varepsilon}
is supported on the subset of quilts having
M
as a subquilt. Moreover, for all
M^{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}
and
\delta>0
sufficiently small in terms of
M^{0}
, letting
F_{\delta,M^{0}}
be the event that a quilt has a subquilt lying in
B_{\delta}(M^{0})
,

\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{F_{\delta,M^{0}}}=\int_{B_{\delta}(M^{0})}m^{\varepsilon}_{M}\mathrm{Unif}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}(dM).

(4.10)

•

For a sample from
m_{M}^{\varepsilon}/|m_{M}^{\varepsilon}|
, the
b
complementary subquilts of
M
are independent.

In (
4.10
) we want
\delta
sufficiently small to avoid the situation that
Q
has multiple subquilts lying in
B_{\delta}(M^{0})
. If instead
\delta\in(0,\infty]
is arbitrary, the left hand side of (
4.10
) needs to be weighted by the number of subquilts lying in
B_{\delta}(M^{0})
.

The desired Proposition
4.10
is an immediate consequence.

Proof of Proposition
4.10
.

The existence of the conditional law of
Q
given
\{Q\text{ has }M\text{ as a subquilt}\}
follows from the first claim of Proposition
4.11
, and the conditional independence of the
Q_{i}
follows from the second claim.
∎

We now turn to the proof of Proposition
4.11
. The key ingredients are the factorization of quilts of a given template (Lemma
4.9
), a template factorization result (Proposition
4.12
), and a Lebesgue measure factorization statement (Lemma
4.13
).

Let
\mathcal{T}^{*}
be the collection of all
T^{*}
having
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
as its marked subtemplate. That is,
\mathcal{T}^{*}
contains all templates of
\mathcal{T}
obtained by filling in the holes of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
and marking the faces corresponding to
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
. Recall the holes of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
are labelled
1,\dots,b
. For
T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}

and
i\leq b
, let
T_{i}^{*}
be the marked template with
b-1
holes obtained from
T^{*}
by replacing the
j
th connected component of the unmarked faces with a hole for
j\neq i
, see Figure
10
(c),(d). Let
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{i}
be the collection of all possible
T_{i}^{*}
as
T^{*}
varies.

Proposition 4.12
.

The map
\mathcal{T}^{*}\to\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathcal{T}^{*}_{i}
sending
T^{*}\mapsto(T^{*}_{1},\dots,T^{*}_{b})
is a bijection.

Proposition
4.12
is an independence statement about templates: no matter how we fill the holes indexed by
\{1,\dots,b\}\backslash\{i\}
, the choices for how to fill the
i
th hole are the same, namely
\mathcal{T}_{i}^{*}
. See Figure
10
(b),(c). We defer the proof of Proposition
4.12
to Section
4.5
.

Figure 10:

(a)
A template
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
. The white regions are holes of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
.
(b), (c)
Two different
T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
(vertices of polygons not shown). The marked gray region correspond to
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
, and the arrows depict the orderings on faces of
T^{*}
. Notice that the blue arrows are traversed in a different order in the two pictures. Perhaps surprisingly, regardless of how one hole is filled, the choices for filling the other hole are the same (Proposition
4.12
).
(d)
For the marked template
T^{*}
depicted in (c), the marked template
T_{i}^{*}
is obtained by replacing all but the
i
th connected component of non-marked faces (bolded) with holes. In the figure
T_{i}^{*}
is the template with
b-1
holes (white, not bolded). In Lemma
4.13

J_{T_{i}^{*}}\subset E_{T_{i}^{*}}
is the set of bolded edges, and the subquilt
Q_{i}
(bolded) can be identified with a tuple of edge lengths (in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}
).

Recall that
\mathrm{Unif}_{T^{*}}
is the measure on marked quilts having template
T^{*}
given by Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{T^{*}}}
. For
Q
sampled from
\mathrm{Unif}_{T^{*}}
, let
\widetilde{\mathrm{Unif}}_{T^{*}}
be the law of
(M,Q_{1},\dots,Q_{b})
where
M
and
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{b}
are the marked subquilt and complementary quilts of
Q
. For
T_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}_{i}^{*}
, let
J_{T_{i}^{*}}\subset E_{T_{i}^{*}}
be the edges on the boundaries of the unmarked faces, see Figure
10
(d). For any marked quilt
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{T^{*}}
, the subquilt
Q_{i}
is specified by the lengths of the edges in
J_{T_{i}^{*}}
, so we may identify
Q_{i}
with an element of
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}
.

We now give a factorization for uniform measures on templates, which follows from the product structure of Lebesgue measure on Euclidean space, and the fact that the holes of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
do not share any common boundary edges. It is essentially just a linear change of variables.

Lemma 4.13
.

There is a collection
\{\mathrm{Unif}^{M}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}\}
of measures on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}
for
M\in\mathcal{Q}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}
,
1\leq i\leq b
and
T_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}_{i}^{*}
such that

\widetilde{\mathrm{Unif}}_{T^{*}}(dM,dQ_{1},\dots,dQ_{b})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathrm{Unif}^{M}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}(dQ_{i})}}\right)\mathrm{Unif}_{E_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}}(dM)\qquad\text{ for all }T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*},

where
T_{1}^{*},\dots,T_{b}^{*}
are defined via
T^{*}
as in Proposition
4.12
.

Proof.

Consider the disjoint union
E_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}=E_{0}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{b}E_{i}
where
E_{i}
is the set of edges lying on the boundary of the
i
th hole of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
and
E_{0}
the set of edges not lying on the boundary of any hole. By the identification
\mathcal{Q}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}\leftrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}}
we can identify
M
in
\mathcal{Q}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}
with a tuple
(M_{0},M_{1},\dots,M_{b})
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{0}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{i}}
. Likewise identify a quilt in
\mathcal{Q}_{T^{*}}
with a tuple
(M_{0},Q_{1},\dots,Q_{b})
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{0}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathbb{R}_{+}^{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}
.
Let
\mathrm{Unif}_{E_{i}}
(resp.
\mathrm{Unif}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}
) denote Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{i}}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}
), then

\mathrm{Unif}_{T^{*}}(dM_{0},dQ_{1},\dots,dQ_{b})=\mathrm{Unif}_{E_{0}}(dM_{0})\times\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathrm{Unif}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}(dQ_{i}).

(4.11)

Since
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
is the marked subtemplate of
T^{*}
, each edge
e\in E_{i}
is a concatenation of one or more edges
e^{\prime}\in J_{T_{i}^{*}}
.
Define
M_{i}
as a function of
Q_{i}
by assigning each edge
e\in E_{i}
the sum of lengths of the corresponding
e^{\prime}\in J_{T_{i}^{*}}
. Push forward the
measures in (
4.11
) under the map
(M_{0},Q_{1},\dots,Q_{b})\mapsto(M_{0},Q_{1},M_{1},\dots,Q_{b},M_{b})
and denote the new measures with tildes:

\widetilde{\mathrm{Unif}}_{T^{*}}(dM,dQ_{1},\dots,dQ_{b})=\mathrm{Unif}_{E_{0}}(dM_{0})\times\prod_{i=1}^{b}\widetilde{\mathrm{Unif}}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}(dQ_{i},dM_{i}).

(4.12)

Finally, we can disintegrate each
\widetilde{\mathrm{Unif}}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}
to obtain a family of measures
8
8

8

The general theory of disintegrations only specifies this family up to measure zero sets of
M_{i}
, but we are just applying a linear change of variables to a Lebesgue measure, so
\mathrm{Unif}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}^{M_{i}}
is well defined for every
M_{i}
.

\{\mathrm{Unif}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}^{M_{i}}\}
for
M_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E_{i}}
such that
\widetilde{\mathrm{Unif}}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}(dQ_{i},dM_{i})=\mathrm{Unif}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}^{M_{i}}(dQ_{i})\mathrm{Unif}_{E_{i}}(dM_{i})
. Since
M_{i}
is a function of
M
we may define
\mathrm{Unif}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}^{M}=\mathrm{Unif}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}^{M_{i}}
, then (
4.12
) equals
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathrm{Unif}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}^{M}(dQ_{i})}}\right)\mathrm{Unif}_{E_{0}}(dM_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathrm{Unif}_{E_{i}}(dM_{i})
. The
dM_{0}
and
dM_{i}
terms factorize to give
\mathrm{Unif}_{E_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}}(dM)
, as desired.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.11
.

We will derive an expression for the law of
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{F_{\delta,M^{0}}}
, then write down a choice of
m_{M}^{\varepsilon}
for which (
4.10
) holds, and which satisfies the remaining conditions of finiteness and independent subquilts.

Fix
M^{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}
. By definition, the quilts
Q\in\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{Q}_{T}
satisfying
F_{\delta,M^{0}}
are those which have a subquilt in
B_{\delta}(M^{0})
; this subquilt is a.s. unique when
\delta>0
is sufficiently small since edge lengths are a.s. distinct.
Thus, viewing a sample of
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{F_{\delta,M^{0}}}
as being a
marked
quilt where the subquilt in
B_{\delta}(M^{0})
is marked, we have

\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{F_{\delta,M^{0}}}=\sum_{T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}}\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{\mathcal{Q}_{T^{*}}\cap F_{\delta,M^{0}}}.

Identify a marked quilt
Q
with the tuple
(M,Q_{1},\dots,Q_{b})
where
M
is the marked subquilt and
Q_{i}
are the complementary subquilts of
M
. Write
F_{\delta,M^{0}}^{*}
for the event that the marked subquilt lies in
B_{\delta}(M^{0})
.
Lemma
4.9
gives
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{\mathcal{Q}_{T^{*}}\cap F_{\delta,M^{0}}}=1_{F_{\delta,M^{0}}^{*}}w(Q)\mathrm{Unif}_{T^{*}}(dQ)
where
w(Q)=Z_{\varepsilon}^{-1}P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{init}}(C_{0})\times\prod_{i=1}^{n}P^{\varepsilon}(C_{i})\times P^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{end}}(C_{n+1})
and
C_{0},\dots,C_{n+1}
are the tuples of side lengths of the faces of
Q
. Since
M,Q_{1},\dots,Q_{b}
partition the faces of
Q
, we can group the terms to get
w(Q)=Z_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\widetilde{w}(M)\prod_{i=1}^{b}\widetilde{w}(Q_{i})
.
Applying Lemma
4.13
thus gives

\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{\mathcal{Q}_{T^{*}}\cap F_{\delta,M^{0}}}(dQ)=1_{F_{\delta,M^{0}}^{*}}w(Q)\mathrm{Unif}_{T^{*}}(dQ)=1_{F_{\delta,M_{0}}^{*}}Z_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{b}\widetilde{w}(Q_{i})\mathrm{Unif}^{M}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}(dQ_{i})}}\right)\widetilde{w}(M)\mathrm{Unif}_{E_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}}(dM).

By Proposition
4.12
,

\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{F_{\delta,M^{0}}}=\sum_{T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}}\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|_{\mathcal{Q}_{T^{*}}\cap F_{\delta,M^{0}}}(dQ)=1_{F_{\delta,M^{0}}^{*}}Z_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{T_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}_{i}^{*}}\widetilde{w}(Q_{i})\mathrm{Unif}^{M}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}(dQ_{i})}}\right)}}\right)\widetilde{w}(M)\mathrm{Unif}_{E_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}}(dM).

Define for each
M\in\mathcal{Q}_{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}
the measure

m^{\varepsilon}_{M}:=Z_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\widetilde{w}(M)\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{T_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}_{i}^{*}}\widetilde{w}(Q_{i})\mathrm{Unif}^{M}_{J_{T_{i}^{*}}}(dQ_{i})}}\right),

(4.13)

and view
m^{\varepsilon}_{M}
as a measure on the set of quilts having
M
as a subquilt. The above gives (
4.10
). Finiteness of the measures follows from (
4.10
) since
|\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}|=1<\infty
, and independence of the complementary subquilts of a sample from
m^{\varepsilon}_{M}/|m^{\varepsilon}_{M}|
follows from the product structure of (
4.13
).
∎

4.5
Combinatorial independence of complementary subtemplates

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
4.12
. The arguments are topological in nature and essentially depend on Hopf’s Umlaufsatz: the total curvature/winding in a simple loop is
\pm 2\pi
(Lemma
2.3
). See Section
2.6
for details on total curvature/winding. The reader may want to read Appendix
B
before reading this section to motivate the arguments involved.

Throughout this section we work in the setting of Proposition
4.12
and use the notation of that proposition.
It is notationally convenient for us to slightly modify
\mathcal{T}^{*}
and
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
by replacing the 1-gon face
F_{\mathrm{ext}}
with a hole
H_{\mathrm{ext}}
.

The proof makes repeated use of the following observation. We say two faces of a template are
adjacent
if their boundaries intersect on an edge.

Lemma 4.14
.

Suppose
T\in\mathcal{T}
has external hole
H_{\mathrm{ext}}
and faces
F_{0},\dots,F_{n+1}
. For each 4-gon
F_{i}
, let
\partial_{-}F_{i}
be the union of the two sides adjacent to the root vertex of
F_{i}
. Let
\partial_{-}F_{0}
be the side counterclockwise of the root vertex of
F_{0}
, and let
\partial_{-}F_{n+1}=\partial F_{n+1}
. Let
\partial_{+}F_{i}=\partial F_{i}\backslash\partial_{-}F_{i}
for all
i
.

If
F_{i}
and
F_{j}
are adjacent, then
i<j
if and only if
\partial_{+}F_{i}
intersects
\partial_{-}F_{j}
on an edge.

Proof.

This is immediate from (
d
) in Definition
4.7
.
∎

Recall the holes of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
are called
H_{\mathrm{ext}},H_{1},\dots,H_{b}
.
We embed
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
in the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
such that
H_{\mathrm{ext}}
contains
\infty
. Let
x\in\partial H_{\mathrm{ext}}
be the root vertex of the 3-gon. For
i=1,\dots,b
let
V_{i}\subset\partial H_{i}
be the set of all points on the
i
th boundary loop which are a root vertex or a terminal vertex of some face.

In each face
F
of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
we draw a smooth curve
\eta_{F}
from its root vertex to its terminal vertex; we call
\eta_{F}
the curve of
F
. We do this in a way such that if the curves of two faces meet at an endpoint, they can be smoothly concatenated. This gives us an embedded directed graph
G
in
\hat{\mathbb{C}}\backslash(H_{\mathrm{ext}}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{b}H_{i})
whose vertex set is
\{x\}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{b}V_{i}
and whose edges are the maximal concatenations of the curves, see Figure
11
(left) for an embedding of
G
for the
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
depicted in Figure
10
(a).

Suppose
T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
, so
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
is the marked subtemplate of
T^{*}
. Embed
T^{*}
in
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
such that
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
has the same embedding as before; this corresponds to drawing faces to fill the holes
H_{1},\dots,H_{b}
. We draw curves in each added face from its root vertex to its terminal vertex, and so link up the previous smooth curves to get a single simple smooth curve from
x
to
x
. This curve visits the faces of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
in the order induced by the face ordering of
T^{*}
. Recall the notion of
total curvature
from Section
2.6
. Let
\theta(x)\in[0,2\pi)
be the angle such that the initial tangent to the curve at
x
is parallel to
e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta(x)}
, and for each point
p
on the curve, let
\theta(p)
equal
\theta(x)
plus the total curvature of the curve from
x
to
p
.
We label each
v\in\bigcup_{i}V_{i}
with the value
\theta(v)
.
See Figure
11
(right).

We will show in Lemma
4.16
that the winding on
\bigcup V_{i}
does not depend on
T^{*}
. We first need the following lemma.
For each face
F
, let
\partial_{\ell}F\subset\partial F
(resp.
\partial_{r}F\subset\partial F
) be the clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) boundary arc of
F
from its root vertex to its terminal vertex.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
F
and
F^{\prime}
be faces of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
, and let the root and terminal vertices of
F
(resp.
F^{\prime}
) be
v_{-}
and
v_{+}
(resp.
v_{-}^{\prime}
and
v_{+}^{\prime}
).
Suppose
F
and
F^{\prime}
are adjacent (have a common boundary edge),
F
preceeds
F^{\prime}
in the ordering of faces, and
F
and
F^{\prime}
are not consecutive faces (
v_{+}\neq v_{-}^{\prime}
). Suppose
\partial_{r}F
intersects
\partial_{r}F^{\prime}
along an edge and let
\eta_{FF^{\prime}}
be the clockwise boundary arc of
\partial(F\cup F^{\prime})
from
v_{+}
to
v_{-}^{\prime}
. Then the curve obtained by concatenating
\eta_{F}
,
\eta_{FF^{\prime}}
and
\eta_{F^{\prime}}
has total curvature
\theta(v_{+}^{\prime})-\theta(v_{-})
, in the sense of Definition
2.5
. See Figure
12
(left).

The statement also holds if we replace
\partial_{r}
with
\partial_{\ell}
and clockwise with counterclockwise.

Proof.

We address the first claim, the other case follows the same argument.
Let
\eta_{1}
(resp.
\eta_{2}
) be the concatenations of the curves of all faces of
T^{*}
preceding
F
(resp. between
F
and
F^{\prime}
). Then
\eta_{1}
is a curve from
x
to
v_{-}
, and
\eta_{2}
is a curve from
v_{+}
to
v_{-}^{\prime}
. See Figure
12
(left).
The connected components of
\mathbb{C}\backslash\overline{(\eta_{1}\cup F\cup F^{\prime}\cup H_{\mathrm{ext}})}
are simply connected. Let
D
be the component containing
\eta_{2}
, then
\overline{D}
also contains
\eta_{FF^{\prime}}
. Since
\overline{D}
is simply connected,
\eta_{2}
and
\eta_{FF^{\prime}}
are homotopic in
\mathbb{C}\backslash(\eta_{F}\cup\eta_{F^{\prime}})
, so by Definition
2.5
the total curvature of the concatenation of
\eta_{F},\eta_{FF^{\prime}}
and
\eta_{F^{\prime}}
agrees with that of the concatenation of
\eta_{F},\eta_{2},\eta_{F^{\prime}}
, which by definition is
\theta(v_{+}^{\prime})-\theta(v_{-})
.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

The function
\theta(v)
on
\bigcup_{i}V_{i}
defined above depends on the embedding of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
and the curves of the faces of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
, but does not depend on the choice of
T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
or the embedding of
T^{*}
.

Proof.

We claim that if
F
and
F^{\prime}
are adjacent faces of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
(i.e. have a common boundary edge), then the values of
\theta
on the curve of
F
determine its values on the curve of
F^{\prime}
, and vice versa. Each
T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
induces an ordering on the faces of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
, and Lemma
4.14
implies the relative order of
F
and
F^{\prime}
does not depend on
T^{*}
(since
F
and
F^{\prime}
are adjacent); without loss of generality
F
precedes
F^{\prime}
. If the curve passes directly from
F
to
F^{\prime}
, then the claim is immediate. Otherwise, by Lemma
4.15

\theta(v_{+}^{\prime})-\theta(v_{-})
equals the total curvature of a curve that does not depend on
T^{*}
, so the claim also holds in this case.

The set of faces of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
is connected under the adjacency relation, and the values of
\theta
on the curve segment in the 3-gon are known by definition, so repeatedly using the claim gives the result.
∎

Figure 11:

Left:
We represent
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
in Figure
10
(a) by a graph embedded in the plane. Each edge is a smooth curve (blue arrow) corresponding to a sequence of faces of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
(blue arrows in Figure
10
(b), (c)).
Right:
We add red edges according to the template
T^{*}
shown in Figure
10
(c). We define
\theta(v)
to be the total curvature of the concatenated curve from
x
to
v
. This function
\theta
(labelled values) turns out not to depend on the choice of
T^{*}
.

In our embedded directed graph
G
, the degree of each vertex in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{b}V_{i}
is one, each curve lies in
\hat{\mathbb{C}}\backslash(H_{\mathrm{ext}}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{b}H_{i})
, and the in-degree and out-degree of
x
is one.
Let
A_{i}
be the finite collection of ways of adding directed edges between vertices in
V_{i}
such that the edges lie in
H_{i}
, the resulting graph is still planar, the indegree and outdegree of each vertex in
V_{i}
is one, and the following compatibility relation holds: any added edge
e
from
v
to
w
, drawn as a simple smooth curve in
H_{i}
which joins smoothly with the edges of
G
at
v
and
w
, has total curvature equal to
\theta(w)-\theta(v)
. This compatibility condition makes sense since
\theta(u),\theta(v)
are well defined (Lemma
4.16
), and every embedding of
e
as a simple smooth curve in
H_{i}
that joins smoothly with edges of
G
has the same total curvature (Lemma
2.4
). See Figure
11
(right) where for each hole
H_{i}
the collection of red arrows represents an element of
A_{i}
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Suppose
T_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}_{i}^{*}
. In each unmarked face of
T_{i}^{*}
draw a curve from its root vertex to its terminal vertex. Concatenating these gives a collection of curves from
V_{i}
to
V_{i}
; view each curve as an edge from
V_{i}
to
V_{i}
and let
\alpha_{i}
be the set of these edges. Then
\alpha_{i}\in A_{i}
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
4.16
since
T_{i}^{*}
is a subtemplate of some marked template in
\mathcal{T}^{*}
.
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

For any
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
, adding the edges of each of the
\alpha_{i}
to
G
gives a Hamiltonian cycle, i.e. a cycle that visits every vertex.

Proof.

In the new graph every vertex has indegree and outdegree one. Thus the edges form a collection of cycles. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there is a cycle not containing
x
. Since for any edge from
w
to
v
the total curvature is
\theta(v)-\theta(w)
, the total curvature of the cycle is 0, contradicting the fact that simple smooth closed curves (loops) have total curvature
\pm 2\pi
(Hopf’s Umlaufsatz).
∎

By definition, each
T_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}_{i}^{*}
is a subtemplate of some
T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
; this
T^{*}
defines an ordering on the faces of
T_{i}^{*}
. Lemma
4.14
says the relative ordering of adjacent faces of
T_{i}^{*}
is determined by
T_{i}^{*}
, so this relative ordering does not depend on the choice of
(\alpha_{j})_{j\neq i}
arising from the choice of
T^{*}
. More strongly, as we see next,
every

(\alpha_{j})_{j\neq i}\in\prod_{j\neq i}A_{j}
gives the same partial ordering on adjacent faces, not just those coming from some
T^{*}
. (We expect, but do not prove, that every
(\alpha_{j})_{j\leq b}\in\prod_{j\leq b}A_{j}
arises from some choice of
T^{*}
.)

Lemma 4.19
.

Fix
i\leq b
and
T_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}_{i}^{*}
. Suppose
\alpha_{j}\in A_{j}
for each
j\neq i
. Draw a curve in each face of
T_{i}^{*}
from the root vertex to the terminal vertex, and draw curves in each
H_{j}
according to
\alpha_{j}
, to get a simple curve from
x
to
x
visiting each face exactly once (Lemma
4.18
). For the induced ordering on faces of
T_{i}^{*}
, the relative order of adjacent faces does not depend on
(\alpha_{j})_{j\neq i}
.

Proof.

Let
F,F^{\prime}
be adjacent faces of
T^{*}_{i}
, and let
v_{-},v_{+}
(resp.
v_{-}^{\prime},v_{+}^{\prime}
) be the root and terminal vertices of
F
(resp.
F^{\prime}
). If
\{v_{-},v_{+}\}\cap\{v_{-}^{\prime},v_{+}^{\prime}\}\neq\emptyset
, then one face’s terminal vertex is the other’s root vertex, and the former face precedes the latter regardless of the choice of
(\alpha_{j})_{j\neq i}
.

Otherwise, suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
\{\alpha_{j}^{FF^{\prime}}\}_{j\neq i}
for which
F
precedes
F^{\prime}
, and
\{\alpha_{j}^{F^{\prime}F}\}_{j\neq i}
for which
F^{\prime}
precedes
F
. Assume
\partial_{r}F
intersects
\partial_{r}F^{\prime}
on an edge; the other case is similar.
Let
\eta_{FF^{\prime}}
be the clockwise boundary arc of
\partial(F\cup F^{\prime})
from
v_{+}
to
v_{-}^{\prime}
, and let
\eta_{F^{\prime}F}
be the clockwise boundary arc of
\partial(F\cup F^{\prime})
from
v_{+}^{\prime}
to
v_{-}
, see Figure
12
(middle).
By precisely the same argument of Lemma
4.15
with the curve from
T^{*}
replaced by the curve arising from
\{\alpha_{j}^{FF^{\prime}}\}_{j\neq i}
, the total curvature of the concatenation of
\eta_{F}
,
\eta_{FF^{\prime}}
and
\eta_{F^{\prime}}
is
\theta(v_{+}^{\prime})-\theta(v_{-})
. By instead using the curve arising from
\{\alpha_{j}^{F^{\prime}F}\}_{j\neq i}
, the total curvature of the concatenation of
\eta_{F^{\prime}},\eta_{F^{\prime}F},\eta_{F}
is
\theta(v_{+})-\theta(v_{-}^{\prime})
. Let
\eta
be a simple loop obtained by concatenating
\eta_{F},\eta_{FF^{\prime}},\eta_{F^{\prime}},\eta_{F^{\prime}F}
and having its starting and ending point be a point on the interior of
\eta_{F}
, so its total curvature is well-defined by Definition
2.5
. By combining the previous two total curvature values, and the fact that the total curvatures of
\eta_{F}
and
\eta_{F^{\prime}}
are
\theta(v_{+})-\theta(v_{-})
and
\theta(v_{+}^{\prime})-\theta(v_{-}^{\prime})
, we conclude that the total curvature of
\eta
is
0
. On the other hand,
\eta
is a simple loop so it should have total curvature
\pm 2\pi
(Lemma
2.3
), a contradiction.
∎

We now turn to the main proof.

Figure 12:

Left:
Figure for Lemma
4.15
. Suppose
F
preceeds
F^{\prime}
but are not consecutive faces, and suppose
\partial_{r}F
intersects
\partial_{r}F^{\prime}
.
Then
\theta(v_{+}^{\prime})-\theta(v_{-})
is the total curvature of the concatenation of
\eta_{F}
,
\eta_{FF^{\prime}}
and
\eta_{F^{\prime}}
.
In this figure, the curves of
F
and
F^{\prime}
are line segments, and the winding in
\eta_{F^{\prime}}
is
\pi
less that that in
\eta_{F}
.

Middle:
Figure for Lemma
4.19
. If
F
preceeds
F^{\prime}
for some
\{\alpha_{j}^{FF^{\prime}}\}_{j\neq i}
but
F^{\prime}
preceeds
F
for some
\{\alpha_{j}^{F^{\prime}F}\}_{j\neq i}
, then the
the loop obtained by concatenating
\eta_{F},\eta_{FF^{\prime}},\eta_{F^{\prime}},\eta_{F^{\prime}F}
and rooting at a point in the interior of
\eta_{F}
has total curvature 0 (in the sense of Definition
2.5
). This contradicts the fact that this loop has total curvature
\pm 2\pi
.
Right:
Figure for Lemma
4.20
for
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
, where the interior of
D_{i}
(green, left) is conformally mapped into a subset of
\mathbb{H}
such that the right boundary of
D_{i}
is sent to
[0,1]
.

Proof of Proposition
4.12
.

Clearly the map is injective. We will show it is surjective.
Suppose
(T_{1}^{*},\dots,T_{b}^{*})\in\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathcal{T}_{i}^{*}
. Let
T^{*}
be the marked template obtained by filling in the
i
th hole of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
in the same way as in
T_{i}^{*}
. We will show that
T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
. Since
T^{*}
has
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
as its marked subtemplate, we need to show the four conditions (
a
)-(
d
) defining
\mathcal{T}
in Definition
4.7
hold for
T^{*}
. (
a
) is automatic since
T^{*}
has
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
as a subtemplate.
By Lemmas
4.17
and
4.18
the faces of
T^{*}
can be ordered as
F_{0},F_{1},\dots,F_{n+1}
so that (
b
) holds. Next (
c
) holds for each
T_{i}^{*}
since
T_{i}^{*}
is a subtemplate for some element of
\mathcal{T}^{*}
, so (
c
) also holds for
T^{*}
. Finally we address (
d
). First suppose
F
is an unmarked face of
T^{*}
, i.e.
F
lies in the
i
th hole of
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
for some
i
. We know
T_{i}^{*}
is a subtemplate of some
\hat{T}^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
, and
\hat{T}^{*}
satisfies (
d
) for the face
F
. By Lemma
4.19
, the relative order of
F
and any adjacent face is the same in
T^{*}
and
\hat{T}^{*}
, so
T^{*}
also satisfies (
d
) for the face
F
. A similar argument applies if
F
is a marked face of
T^{*}
, so (
d
) holds. Thus,
T
satisfies all four conditions so
T\in\mathcal{T}
, and so
T^{*}\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
.
∎

4.6
Proof of Theorem
4.1

In order to prove Theorem
4.1
, we will show that the IG field is measurable with respect to the LQG surface decorated by space-filling SLE curve segments. We do this first for each
D_{i}
(Lemma
4.20
), then for the whole explored region (Lemma
4.21
).

Lemma 4.20
.

There is a measurable function
F
such that the following holds.
In the setting described just above Theorem
4.1
, a.s.
(D_{i},\Phi,\Psi,(\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}))/{\sim}=F((D_{i},\Phi,\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})/{\sim})
for each
i=0,\dots,N+1
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\Psi}
be the distribution
\Psi
viewed modulo additive constant in
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. We will show that
\widehat{\Psi}
is measurable with respect to
(D_{i},\Phi,\eta_{i}^{\prime},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})
. Since the arguments we will use do not depend on the choice of embedding of the decorated LQG surface, this implies the lemma.

By
[
MS17
, Theorem 1.10]
, for each connected component
U
of
\mathrm{int}(D_{i})
(the interior of
D_{i}
), the field
\Psi|_{U}
is determined up to additive constant in
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. This completes the proof for
\gamma\in(0,\sqrt{2}]
since
\mathrm{int}(D)
is connected in that regime. For
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
, it remains to show that fixing one of these additive constants fixes all the remaining constants. Basically, the reason for this is that
\partial D_{i}
is the union of four flow lines of
\Psi
, and the values of
\Psi
along a flow line are well-defined up to adding a single integer multiple of
2\pi\chi
.

Recall that
D_{i}
was defined to be the region traced by the space-filling curve
\eta^{\prime}
between hitting two points. Therefore,
\partial D_{i}
comprises segments of flow lines of
\Psi
with specified angles. For each connected component
U
of
D_{i}
let
\partial^{p}U
denote the prime end boundary of
D_{i}
, let
\partial^{p}_{r}U
be the right boundary arc of
U
with orientation chosen such that its starting and ending points agree with those of
\eta
in
U
, and let
\partial_{r}^{p}D_{i}
be the concatenation of the
\partial^{p}_{r}U
ordered by the time
U
is filled out by
\eta
.

Let
f:\mathrm{int}(D_{i})\to\mathbb{H}
be a map such that
f:\mathrm{int}(D_{i})\to f(\mathrm{int}(D_{i}))
is conformal, and whose extension to
\partial^{p}_{r}D_{i}
is a homeomorphism from
\partial^{p}_{r}D_{i}
to
[0,1]\subset\mathbb{H}
. See Figure
12
(right). Let
\widetilde{\Psi}
be the distribution on
F(\mathrm{int}(D_{i}))
given by
\widetilde{\Psi}=\Psi\circ f^{-1}-\chi(26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}})\arg((f^{-1})^{\prime})
, where
\mathrm{arg}((f^{-1})^{\prime})
is as defined in (
2.3
).
By the definition of boundary conditions of interior flow lines
[
MS17
, Theorem 1.1]
, and the consistency of (
2.3
) with the usual definition of
\arg((f^{-1})^{\prime})
for simply connected domains (Proposition
2.6
),
\widetilde{\Psi}
is constant on
[0,f(c_{i}))
and on
(f(c_{i}),1]
. Furthermore, the point
c_{i}
lies on the boundary of one of the connected components of the interior of
D_{i}
. Consequently, fixing the additive constant of one of the
\Psi|_{U}
fixes the additive constants of
\Psi|_{U^{\prime}}
for all
U^{\prime}
, as needed.
∎

Lemma 4.21
.

In the setting of Theorem
4.1
let
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}=(M_{\tau},\Phi,\{(\eta^{\prime}_{i},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})\>:\>i\in I_{\tau}\})/{\sim}
be the decorated LQG surface obtained from
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
by forgetting the IG field
\Psi
.
Then

\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\Psi}
be
\Psi
viewed modulo additive constant in
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
.
We will show that
\widehat{\Psi}|_{M_{\tau}}
is measurable with respect to
(M_{\tau},\Phi,\{(\eta^{\prime}_{i},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})\>:\>i\in I_{\tau}\})
, i.e., we prove that the desired result holds when LQG surfaces are replaced by their embeddings in
M_{\tau}
. None of the arguments we will use depend on the choice of embedding, so this implies the desired result.

Condition on
(M_{\tau},\Phi,\{(\eta^{\prime}_{i},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})\>:\>i\in I_{\tau}\})
.
By Lemma
4.20
,
\Psi|_{D_{i}}
is determined modulo additive constant in
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
for each
i
. Next, if
D_{i}
intersects
D_{i^{\prime}}
on an interval
\eta
, then since
\eta
is a segment of a flow line, the additive constant for
\Psi|_{D_{i}}
fixes the additive constant for
\Psi|_{D_{i^{\prime}}}
by flow line boundary conditions
[
MS17
, Theorem 1.1]
. Finally, for any
i,i^{\prime}\in I_{\tau}
it is possible to find a sequence
i_{1},\dots,i_{j}
with
i_{0}=i
and
i_{j}=i^{\prime}
such that
D_{i_{k}}
and
D_{i_{k+1}}
intersect on an interval for all
k
. We conclude that under this conditioning
\widehat{\Psi}|_{M_{\tau}}
is deterministic, i.e.,
\widehat{\Psi}|_{M_{\tau}}
is measurable with respect to
(M_{\tau},\Phi,\{(\eta^{\prime}_{i},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i})\>:\>i\in I_{\tau}\})
.
∎

Finally, we show that for all quilts having a certain subtemplate, the peeling procedure of Theorem
4.1
chooses this subtemplate with probability not depending on the quilt.

Lemma 4.22
.

Fix
M\in\mathcal{Q}_{{T_{\mathrm{sub}}}}
for some
T_{\mathrm{sub}}
as in Section
4.4
, and let
Q
be any quilt having
M
as a subtemplate.
Carry out the peeling procedure on
Q
analogous to that of Theorem
4.1
. That is, sample
T\geq 0
with
\mathbb{P}[T=t]=2^{-t-1}
, explore all faces adjacent to the external face at step 0, at each subsequent step explore the faces adjacent to a boundary point chosen uniformly at random according to length, and stop after step
T
or when all faces are explored, whichever is earlier. Then the probability the explored subtemplate is
M
does not depend on the choice of
Q
.

Proof.

Given
Q
, the event
\{\text{explored subtemplate is }M\}
occurs when
T
equals the number of non-boundary-touching faces of
M
, and during each of the
T
peeling steps we pick a point that lies in
M
. The probability of this event depends only on
M
since
M
determines all the lengths involved in the probability computation for the
T
peeling steps.
∎

We can now prove Theorem
4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We can sample the
\varepsilon
-discretized LQG disk in two steps. First, we sample a quilt
Q\in\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{Q}_{T}
from
\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
. Then conditioned on
Q
, for each face
F
we sample an independent LQG cell
\mathcal{C}_{F}
conditioned on having boundary lengths given by the side lengths of
F
, and conformally weld the
\mathcal{C}_{F}
according to
Q
. By Proposition
4.3
this gives an
\varepsilon
-discretized LQG disk.

The peeling process described can already be applied after the first step of the above sampling procedure, namely to the quilt
Q
(see Lemma
4.22
). Mark the faces of
Q
discovered during the peeling process, and let
M
be the marked subquilt. By Bayes’ theorem, we may flip this procedure around: sample
M
from its marginal law, then sample
Q\sim\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}
conditioned on having
M
as a subquilt and weighted by the probability of marking
M
given
Q
. By Lemma
4.22
this probability does not depend on
Q
, so there is in fact no weighting. Thus, by Proposition
4.10
, conditioned on
M
the complementary subquilts
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{b}
of
M
are conditionally independent.
Conditioned on
M,Q_{1},\dots,Q_{b}
, sample independent collections of LQG cells
(\mathcal{C}_{F})_{F\in M},(\mathcal{C}_{F})_{F\in Q_{1}},\dots,(\mathcal{C}_{F})_{F\in Q_{b}}
.

Suppose
\gamma\in(0,\sqrt{2}]
, and for
1\leq i\leq b
let
\mathcal{D}_{i}
be the decorated LQG surface arising from conformally welding
(\mathcal{C}_{F})_{F\in Q_{j}}
according to
Q_{j}
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{b}
are conditionally independent given
\sigma(M,(\mathcal{C}_{F})_{F\in M})
.
Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
be the conformal welding of
(\mathcal{C}_{F})_{F\in M}
according to
M
, and
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}
the decorated LQG surface
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
after forgetting the IG field. Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}=\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}
, and using Lemma
4.21
, we have
\sigma(M,(\mathcal{C}_{F})_{F\in M})=\sigma(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau})=\sigma(\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\prime})=\sigma(\mathcal{M}_{\tau})
. Thus
\mathcal{D}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{b}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
. Finally, the loops
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{m}
partition
\{\mathcal{D}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{b}\}
, and each
(O_{k},\Phi,\Psi)/{\sim}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
and the
k
th subset of the partition (Lemma
4.21
), so
(O_{1},\Phi,\Psi)/{\sim},\dots,(O_{m},\Phi,\Psi)/{\sim}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
.

When
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
, we do not necessarily have
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}=\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}
. Each
\mathcal{C}_{F}
has cut points, so even though the planar maps
Q_{i}
each have the disk topology, the LQG surfaces
\mathcal{D}_{i}
may have cut points. Thus
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}
arises from
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
by identifying pairs of boundary points of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
according to the cut points of
\mathcal{D}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{b}
. Since the identifications on the
i
th boundary component of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
are a function of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{b}
are conditionally independent given
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}
, we conclude
\mathcal{D}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{D}_{b}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}^{\prime}
, and thus conditionally independent given
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
(Lemma
4.21
). The rest of the argument is identical to the
\gamma\in(0,\sqrt{2}]
case.
∎

5
Open problems

The results of this paper are only proven for LQG in the subcritical phase, i.e., when
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}>25
or equivalently
\gamma\in(0,2)
.
The reason for this is that our proofs rely on the mating of trees theorem
[
DMS21
,
AG21
]
in order to get a decomposition of the LQG disk into small pieces with nice independence properties. An analog of the mating of trees theorem in the critical case
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}=25
,
\gamma=2
is proven in
[
AHPS21
]
, but the analog of space-filling SLE is not a continuous curve, so this theorem does not give a decomposition of the LQG disk into
small
independent pieces.

Problem 5.1
.

Extend the theorems from Section
1.3
to the case of critical LQG (
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}=25
,
\gamma=2
).

One possible approach to Problem
5.1
is to take a limit of the objects involved in our theorem statements as
\gamma\to 2^{-}
.

The recent paper
[
AG23
]
defines an analog of the LQG disk in the supercritical phase
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}\in(1,25)
. The paper also constructs a coupling of the supercritical LQG disk with CLE
4
, which has similar properties to the relationships between LQG with
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}\geq 25
and independent SLE with central charge
{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathrm{M}}=26-{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}
.
It is natural to ask whether there are also “mismatched” coupling statements for LQG with
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}\geq 25
.

Problem 5.2
.

Extend the theorems from Section
1.3
to the case of supercritical LQG (
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}\in(1,25)
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\gamma|=2
).

An especially interesting special case of Problem
5.2
is when we have a supercritical LQG surface of central charge
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}=26-k\in\{2,\dots,24\}
decorated by
k
independent Gaussian free fields (i.e.,
\mathbf{c}_{1}=\dots=\mathbf{c}_{k}=1
).
The reason is that, from the work of Polyakov
[
Pol81
]
, this combination of objects is expected to be related to bosonic string theory in
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
. Moreover, this same combination of objects is also potentially connected to Yang-Mills theory (see, e.g.,
[
CPS23
]
). See Section
1.4
for further discussion.

In the matched case
\kappa\in\{\gamma^{2},16/\gamma^{2}\}
, there are relationships between SLE and LQG for a wide variety of different LQG surfaces (see, e.g.,
[
DMS21
,
MS19
,
AG21
,
AHS21
,
ASY22
]
). For simplicity, in this paper we focused exclusively on the case of the LQG disk, but we expect that analogs of our results are true for other types of LQG surfaces.

Problem 5.3
.

Prove analogs of the theorems from Section
1.3
