for other LQG surfaces besides the LQG disk, such as LQG disks with boundary insertions, LQG triangles, LQG spheres, LQG wedges, LQG cones, and non-simply connected LQG surfaces.

It is of interest to consider variants of the results of this paper where the imaginary geometry fields are replaced by other types of “matter fields” (random generalized functions satisfying a conformal covariance rule).
Some examples of possible matter fields to consider are conformal loop ensemble nesting fields
[
MWW15
]
, Ising spin fields
[
CGN15
]
,
winding fields
and
layering fields
associated with Brownian loop soups
[
CGK16
,
vdBCL18
,
LJ10
,
CGPR21
]
, and the conformally invariant fields associated with Brownian loop soups constructed in
[
JLQ23
]
.

Problem 5.4
.

Are there analogs of the conditional independence results of this paper where the auxiliary imaginary geometry fields are replaced by fields of the types mentioned just above?

Our main results give exact conditional independence statements for LQG surfaces cut by various types of sets, but, in contrast to the matched case, we do not have an exact description of either the law of the information we are conditioning on or the conditional laws of the complementary LQG surfaces given this information.

Problem 5.5
.

Assume that we are in the setting of Theorem
1.9
.
Is there an explicit random process which generates the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
of (
1.10
)?
Can the law of such a random process can be described explicitly?
What about the analogous questions in the settings of the other main results from Section
1.3
?

Problem 5.6
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.9
, is there an explicit description of the conditional laws of the IG-decorated LQG surfaces parametrized by the complementary connected components of the SLE curve
K
given the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
?
What about in the settings of the other main results from Section
1.3
?

Here is a possible approach for Problem
5.5
.
For simplicity, assume that
K
is the image of an SLE
{}_{\kappa}(\rho_{L};\rho_{R})
curve
\eta
with
\rho_{L}\geq\kappa/2-4
, so that
\eta
does not hit the clockwise arc of
\partial\mathbbm{D}
from its starting point to its ending point
[
Dub09a
, Lemma 15]
.
Let
\nu_{\Phi}
be the
\gamma
-LQG length measure on
\eta
, which should be defined as a Gaussian multiplicative chaos measure with respect to the Euclidean Minkowski content measure on
\eta

[
LR15
]
.
Assume that
\eta
is parametrized so that
\nu_{\Phi}(\eta[0,t])=t
for each
t\in[0,T]
, where
T=\nu_{\Phi}(\eta)
.

Let
f
be a conformal map from the connected component of
\mathbbm{D}\setminus\eta
which lies to the left of
\eta
to the upper half-plane
\mathbbm{H}
, taking the starting and ending points of
\eta
to 0 and
\infty
, respectively. For
j=1,\dots,n
, define

F_{j}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Psi_{j}-\chi(\mathbf{c}_{j})\operatorname{arg}f^{\prime}}}\right)(\eta(s))\,ds,\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

(5.1)

where we recall that
(\Psi_{1},\dots,\Psi_{n})
are the auxiliary imaginary geometry fields of central charges
({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{c}}_{n})
.
We conjecture that the process
(F_{1},\dots,F_{n})
generates the
\sigma
-algebra (
1.10
). It would be very interesting to find an explicit description of the law of
(F_{1},\dots,F_{n})
.

One of the most important relationships between SLE and LQG in the matched case is the
mating of trees
theorem
[
DMS21
, Theorem 1.9]
. This theorem says the following: suppose we have a
\gamma
-LQG cone together with an independent space-filling SLE
{}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
curve
\eta^{\prime}
for
\kappa^{\prime}=16/\gamma^{2}
. Parametrize
\eta^{\prime}
so that it traverses one unit of
\gamma
-LQG mass in one unit of time.
Let
L_{t}
(resp.
R_{t}
) denote the net change in the
\gamma
-LQG length of the left (resp. right) outer boundary of
\eta^{\prime}
relative to time 0. Then the process
(L,R)
is a two-dimensional Brownian motion with correlation
\operatorname{Corr}(L_{t},R_{t})=-\cos(\pi\gamma^{2}/4)
(note that the correlation is zero if
\kappa^{\prime}=8
). This theorem is proven building on the quantum zipper theorem in
[
She16
]
. Our results can be viewed as a mismatched analog of the quantum zipper theorem. It is therefore natural to ask of there is also a mismatched mating of trees theorem.

Problem 5.7
.

Suppose we have an appropriate
\gamma
-LQG surface together with a space-filling SLE
{}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
curve for
\kappa^{\prime}\not=16/\gamma^{2}>4
, sampled independently from the LQG surface and then parametrized by LQG mass.
Define the (mismatched) left/right
\gamma
-LQG boundary length process
(L,R)
as above.
Is there a variant of our main results which says that the joint conditional law of
L
and
R
given certain auxiliary information along the curve
\eta^{\prime}
is particularly simple? Are
L
and
R
conditionally independent given this information for certain special values of
\gamma
and
\kappa^{\prime}
?

In a similar vein to (
5.1
), a possible choice of auxiliary information to consider in Problem
5.7
is the following. Let
\Psi_{1}
be the whole-plane GFF coupled with
\eta^{\prime}
in the sense of imaginary geometry and let
\Psi_{2}
be another imaginary geometry field (independent from everything else) such that the central charges associated with
\eta^{\prime}
, the
\gamma
-LQG surface, and
\Psi_{2}
sum to 26. Let
a,b\in[-1,1]
with
a^{2}+b^{2}=1
be chosen so that
a\Psi_{1}+b\Psi_{2}
is a field of central charge 1 (i.e., with
\chi=0
). Then, consider the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the function

t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a\Psi_{1}+b\Psi_{2}}}\right)(\eta(s))\,ds.

(5.2)

In the special case when our LQG surface is an LQG sphere with
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}=30
,
\kappa^{\prime}=8
, and
\Psi_{2}
is an imaginary geometry field of central charge
-2
, we expect that
L
and
R
are conditionally independent given the
\sigma
-algebra generated by (
5.2
). Indeed, such a conditional independence statement would be a continuum analog of the conditional independence statement for meanders given in Proposition
B.1
; c.f.
[
BGS22
, Conjecture 1.3]
.

Next, let
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
be independent imaginary geometry fields on
\mathbbm{D}
of central charges
{\mathbf{c}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{c}}_{2}
, respectively. By Proposition
1.17
, if
a,b\in[-1,1]
with
a^{2}+b^{2}=1
, then
\widehat{\Psi}:=a\Psi_{1}+b\Psi_{2}
is another imaginary geometry field with a possibly different central charge. It is natural to wonder how the flow lines of
\Psi_{1}
,
\Psi_{2}
, and
\widehat{\Psi}
are related.

Problem 5.8
.

Is there a direct way to construct the flow lines of
\widehat{\Psi}
from the flow lines of
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
?

Here is a possible approach to Problem
5.8
. Let
q>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. Grow a flow line of
\Psi_{1}
(parametrized by Minkowski content, say) up to time
\varepsilon
. Then, grow a flow line of
\Psi_{2}
started from the tip of this first flow line, stopped at time
q\varepsilon
. Continue alternating
\varepsilon
-increments of flow lines of
\Psi_{1}
and
q\varepsilon
-increments of flow lines of
\Psi_{2}
in this manner to construct a curve
\eta_{\varepsilon}
. It is reasonable to guess that if we choose
q=q(a,b)
appropriately, then
\eta_{\varepsilon}
should converge to a flow line of
\widehat{\Psi}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

One motivation for Problem
5.8
comes from lattice models. To explain this, we follow the exposition in
[
Dub09b
, Section 1]
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
be two independent copies of the dimer model on
\varepsilon\mathbbm{Z}^{2}\cap\mathbbm{D}
. By the Temperlyan bijection,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
gives rise to a height function
\psi_{1}
on the dual graph of
\varepsilon\mathbbm{Z}^{2}\cap\mathbbm{D}
, and similarly for
\mathcal{D}_{2}
. These same height functions can also be obtained as height functions of two independent uniform spanning trees
T_{1},T_{2}
on
\varepsilon\mathbbm{Z}^{2}\cap\mathbbm{D}
. The height functions
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
converge as
\varepsilon\to 0
to two independent copies of the
\kappa=2
(
{\mathbf{c}}=-2
) imaginary geometry field on the disk
[
Ken01
]
. Moreover, the peano curves associated with
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
converge to two independent SLE
8
s
[
LSW04
]
which should be counterflow lines of these two imaginary geometry fields.

Now, consider the double dimer model
\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}
. It is believed (but not proven) that the set of boundaries of connected components of
\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}
converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
to CLE
4
. As explained in
[
Dub09b
, Section 1.2]
, the height function associated with
\mathcal{D}_{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}
is the difference
\widehat{\psi}=\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}
. This height function should converge as
\varepsilon\to 0
to a GFF on
\mathbbm{D}
whose level lines are the CLE
4
loops. Thus, the continuum limit of the double dimer model gives us two
{\mathbf{c}}=-2
imaginary geometry fields on
\mathbbm{D}
whose difference is a GFF.

Problem 5.9
.

Are there any other collections of lattice models where one naturally sees linear combinations of fields which converge to imaginary geometry fields?
Can the solution of Problem
5.8
be useful in analyzing these discrete models?

Appendix A
Planar maps decorated by multiple statistical physics models

Theorem
1.9
is the continuum analog of certain Markovian properties for random planar maps with boundary decorated by two or more statistical physics models. The general principle is as follows. Suppose that one of our statistical physics models gives rise to an “interface”
\eta
, viewed as a curve on the planar map
M
(or on a closely related planar, such as the dual map).
We can view the connected components of
M\setminus\eta
as submaps of
M
. Then in many cases these submaps are conditionally independent given the appropriate information about the restrictions to
\eta
of the
other
statistical physics models (besides the one used to generate
\eta
). In a similar vein, our other main results can be seen as discrete analogs of Markov properties for, e.g., random walk or graph distance balls on random planar maps decorated by one or more statistical physics models.

For concreteness, we now fully explain this principle in one special case: planar maps decorated by two instances of the Gaussian free field and one spanning tree. This case is particularly nice since the underlying random planar map is exactly uniform. In general, the marginal law of the underlying random planar map may not admit a simple description.

For a graph
G
, write
\mathcal{V}(G)
and
\mathcal{E}(G)
for its sets of vertices and edges, respectively.
For
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, let
\mathcal{M}_{n}
be the set of triangulations with the disk topology with simple boundary of perimeter
n
(i.e., triangulations with a marked exterior face whose boundary is a simple cycle of length
n
). For concreteness, we allow multiple edges but not self-loops. We note that everything in this section also works with other types of planar maps, e.g., triangulations with no multiple edges, quadrangulations, or planar maps with mixed face degrees.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\bullet}
be the set of 4-tuples
(M,T,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
where
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
,
T
is a spanning tree on
M
with wired boundary condition, and
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}:\mathcal{V}(M)\to\mathbbm{R}
are functions on the vertex set of
M
which vanish at each boundary vertex.

To define a probability measure on
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\bullet}
, let
\beta>0
be a universal constant to be chosen later. For
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
write
\lambda^{M}
for Lebesgue measure on the space of functions
\phi:\mathcal{V}(M)\to\mathbbm{R}
which vanish on the boundary.
Also write
\mu
for counting measure on pairs
(M,T)
where
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
T
is a spanning tree on
M
with wired boundary condition.
We define a probability measure on
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\bullet}
by

\frac{1}{Z}e^{-\beta\#\mathcal{E}(M)}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\sum_{i\in\{1,2\}}\sum_{\{x,y\}\in\mathcal{E}(M)}|\phi_{i}(x)-\phi_{i}(y)|^{2}}}\right)\,d\lambda^{M}(\phi_{1})\,d\lambda^{M}(\phi_{2})\,d\mu(M,T)

(A.1)

where
Z
is a normalizing constant.

Under this probability measure, the conditional law of
(T,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
given
M
is that of an independent triple consisting of a uniform spanning tree on
M
with wired boundary conditions and two discrete zero-boundary Gaussian free fields (GFFs) on
M
.
Moreover, the marginal law of
M
is given by the counting measure on
\mathcal{M}_{n}
weighted by (a quantity proportional to)

\displaystyle e^{-\beta\#\mathcal{E}(M)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\sum_{\{x,y\}\in\mathcal{E}(M)}|\phi(x)-\phi(y)|^{2}}}\right)\,d\lambda^{M}(\phi)}}\right]^{2}\times\#\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\text{spanning trees of $M$}}}\right\}.

(A.2)

By a standard computation for the discrete GFF (see, e.g.,
[
Dub09b
, Section 4.1]
), the integral with respect to
\lambda^{M}
in (
A.2
) is equal to an exponential function of
\#\mathcal{V}(M)
times
(\det\Delta_{M})^{-1/2}
, where
\Delta_{M}
is the discrete Laplacian determinant on
M
(restricted to functions which vanish on the boundary). Furthermore, by the Kirchoff matrix-tree theorem, the number of spanning trees of
M
is equal to
\det\Delta_{M}
.
Hence, by canceling two factors of
\det\Delta_{M}
, we get that the quantity (
A.2
) depends only on
\#\mathcal{V}(M)
. Therefore, the marginal law of
M
under (
A.1
) is given by the counting measure on
\mathcal{M}_{n}
weighted by an exponential function of
\#\mathcal{V}(M)
. Since we are looking at triangulations, the Euler characteristic formula shows that this is the same as the counting measure on
\mathcal{M}_{n}
weighted by an exponential function of
\#\mathcal{E}(M)
.

If we choose the constant
\beta
appropriately, then under the above marginal law of
M
the law of
\#\mathcal{E}(M)
has a polynomial tail (see, e.g.,
[
Cur16
, Section 3.2]
for a more general statement). Henceforth fix this choice of
\beta
.
For this
\beta
, the triangulation
M
converges in law
n\to\infty
in the Gromov-Hausdorff sense to the so-called
free area Brownian disk

[
BM17
,
GM19
,
AHS20
]
, which is in turn equivalent (as a metric measure space) to the unit boundary length
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG disk
[
MS21
]
.

Due to the convergence of the discrete GFF to the continuum GFF and the convergence of the peano curve of the uniform spanning tree on a fixed lattice to SLE
8

[
LSW04
]
, the above convergence leads naturally to the following conjecture.

Conjecture A.1
.

Let
(M,T,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\bullet}
be sampled from (
A.1
) and let
P
be the path in
M
which traverses
T
in contour (depth-first) order starting and ending at a marked boundary vertex. The 4-tuple
(M,P,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
converges in law under an appropriate scaling limit as
n\to\infty
to a unit boundary length
\sqrt{8/3}
-LQG disk together with an SLE
8
curve starting and ending at the same marked boundary point and two independent zero-boundary GFFs.

Possible topologies of convergence in Conjecture
A.1
include generalizations of the Gromov-Hausdorff topology for metric spaces decorated by a curve and a pair of generalized functions (see, e.g.,
[
GM17
,
Khe18
]
) or convergence under an appropriate embedding of
M
into the disk (e.g., the Tutte embedding
[
GMS21
]
or the Cardy embedding
[
HS23
]
).

Let us now describe a Markov property for
(M,T,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
. See Figure
13
for an illustration. Let
M^{*}
be the dual map of
M
and let
T^{*}
be the dual spanning tree of
T
, which consists of all edges of
M^{*}
which do not cross edges of
T
. Let
k\leq n-2
and let
e_{1},e_{2}\in\partial M
be two edges with the property that the two boundary arcs separating them have lengths
k
and
n-k-2
, respectively. There is a unique path
\eta
in
T^{*}
from the vertex of
M^{*}
corresponding to the face of
M
incident to
e_{1}
, to the vertex of
M^{*}
corresponding to the face of
M
incident to
e_{2}
.
The path
\eta
forms the outer boundary of the contour path
P
of Conjecture
A.1
if we start
P
at the leftmost vertex of
e_{1}
and stop
P
when it first hits a vertex of
e_{2}
. By, e.g.,
[
AG21
, Lemma 2.6]
, Conjecture
A.1
suggests that the scaling limit of
\eta
should be a chordal SLE
{}_{2}(-1,-1)
curve between two boundary points of the disk (provided we choose
k=k(n)
so that
k(n)/n
converges to a number in
(0,1)
).

Let
H
be the sub-map of
M
consisting the faces of
M
traversed by
\eta
and the vertices and edges of
M
on the boundaries of these faces.
We view
H
as a planar map with boundary equipped with two marked edges, namely
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
.
We count each edge of
H
which is not crossed by
\eta
as a boundary edge (even if this edge is not part of the external face of
H
). See Figure
13
.

Let
M_{L}
(resp.
M_{R}
) be the submap of
M
consisting of the vertices, edges, and faces of
M
which lie strictly to the left (resp. right) of
\eta
(not including
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
, or the edges and faces which are crossed by
\eta
).
Each of
M_{L}
and
M_{R}
is a triangulation with (not necessarily simple) boundary.
We view
M_{L}
(resp.
M_{R}
) as being equipped with two marked vertices, namely the endpoints of
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
.
Let
T_{L}
(resp.
T_{R}
) be the subgraph of
T
induced by the set of edges of
T
which lie on
M_{L}
(resp.
M_{R}
).

The following is a discrete analog of Theorem
1.9
.

Figure 13:

Left:
A triangulation with boundary
M
(red, gray, and green edges) decorated by a spanning tree
T
with wired boundary condition (red). The path
\eta
is the branch of the dual tree between the marked boundary edges
e_{1},e_{2}
, and
H
(light green) is the submap of
M
induced by the set of faces traversed by
\eta
.

Right:
The decorated planar maps
(M_{L},T_{L})
and
(M_{R},T_{R})
lying to the left and right of
H
(light blue and light yellow) are connected but do not necessarily have simple boundary. Identifying one boundary arc of each of these maps with the boundary arcs of
H
to the left and right of
\eta
, respectively, gives us back
(M,T)
.
If we sample the tree / discrete GFF decorated triangulation
(M,T,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
from the probability measure (
A.1
), then Proposition
A.2
tells us that
(M_{L},T_{L},\phi_{1}|_{M_{L}},\phi_{2}|_{M_{L}})
and
(M_{R},T_{R},\phi_{1}|_{M_{R}},\phi_{2}|_{M_{R}})
are conditionally independent given
(H,\phi_{1}|_{H},\phi_{2}|_{H})
.

Proposition A.2
.

The left and right decorated random planar maps
(M_{L},T_{L},\phi_{1}|_{M_{L}},\phi_{2}|_{M_{L}})
and
(M_{R},T_{R},\phi_{1}|_{M_{R}},\phi_{2}|_{M_{R}})
are conditionally independent given the middle decorated planar map
(H,\phi_{1}|_{H},\phi_{2}|_{H})
.

Proof.

We claim that the probability measure (
A.1
) admits a factorization of the form

d\nu_{H,L}(M_{L},T_{L},\phi_{1}|_{M_{L}},\phi_{2}|_{M_{L}})\,d\nu_{H,R}(M_{L},T_{L},\phi_{1}|_{M_{L}},\phi_{2}|_{M_{L}})\,d\nu_{0}(H,\phi_{1}|_{H},\phi_{2}|_{H})

(A.3)

where
\nu_{0}
is the law of
(H,\phi_{1}|_{H},\phi_{2}|_{H})
and
\nu_{H,L}
and
\nu_{H,R}
are probability measures that depend only on
H
. This immediately implies the desired conditional independence statement. We will now check (
A.3
) via elementary manipulations of (
A.2
).

Let us first note that by construction,

\#\mathcal{E}(M)=\#\mathcal{E}(H\setminus\partial H)+\#\mathcal{E}(M_{L})+\#\mathcal{E}(M_{R}).

(A.4)

Next, note that the only edges of
M
which join a vertex of
M_{L}
and a vertex of
M_{R}
are the marked boundary edges
e_{1},e_{2}
. Since
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
vanish on
\partial M
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
we have

\displaystyle\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\sum_{\{x,y\}\in\mathcal{E}(M)}|\phi_{i}(x)-\phi_{i}(y)|^{2}}}\right)=\prod_{A\in\{M_{L},M_{R},H\}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\sum_{\{x,y\}\in\mathcal{E}(A)}|\phi_{i}(x)-\phi_{i}(y)|^{2}}}\right)

(A.5)

Finally, we deal with the counting measure term in (
A.1
).
We first claim that if we see the decorated planar maps
(M_{L},T_{L})
,
(M_{R},T_{R})
, and
H
, then there is a unique way of gluing them together to recover
(M,T)
. Indeed, exactly one of the two boundary arcs of
M_{L}
separating its two marked points includes an edge which is not in
T_{L}
. We identify the edges of this boundary arc in chronological order with the right boundary arc of
H
between its two marked edges, and similarly for
M_{R}
.
This allows us to recover
M
.
By definition,
\eta
crosses no edges of
T
, so
T_{L}\cup T_{R}=T\setminus\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
.

One easily sees that the set of possibilities for
(M_{L},T_{L})
depends only on
H
(not on
(M_{R},T_{R})
) and similarly with
L
and
R
interchanged. This allows us to decompose the counting measure
\mu
on pairs
(M,T)
as a product measure

d\mu(M,T)=d\mu_{H,L}(M_{L},T_{L})\,d\mu_{H,R}(M_{R},T_{R})\,d\mu_{0}(H)

(A.6)

where
\mu_{0}
is the law of
H
and
\mu_{H,L}
and
\mu_{H,R}
are counting measures on
H
-dependent sets of tree-decorated maps. Plugging (
A.4
), (
A.5
), and (
A.6
) into (
A.1
) gives (
A.3
).
∎

Finally, we make some general comments on the central charges associated to statistical physics models.

Remark A.3
.

If
M
is a planar map and
S
is a statistical mechanics model on
M
, then the central charge associated with
S
is the number
{\mathbf{c}}
such that when
M
is large, the partition function of
S
is “close” (in a sense that we deliberately leave vague) to an exponential function of
\#\mathcal{E}(M)
or
\#\mathcal{V}(M)
times
(\det\Delta_{M})^{-{\mathbf{c}}/2}
, where
\Delta_{M}
is the discrete Laplacian.
By the discussion immediately after (
A.2
), spanning trees have central charge
-2
and the GFF has central charge
1
. As another example, random walks have central charge
0
.
Indeed, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a finite collection of planar maps with boundary decorated by a bulk marked point, and consider all pairs
(M,P)
where
M\in\mathfrak{M}
and
P=(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
is a path in
M
started at the marked point and terminated when it hits the boundary. Each pair
(M,P)
is given a weight
\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}{d_{i}^{-1}}
where
d_{i}
is the degree of
v_{i}
. If we sample
(M,P)
proportionately to its weight, then the marginal law of
M
is the uniform measure on
\mathfrak{M}
; this lack of weighting means random walks have central charge 0. Brownian motion, as the scaling limit of random walk, should also be viewed as having central charge 0.

Appendix B
Conditional independence for uniform meanders

In this appendix we prove a conditional independence property for uniform meanders (Proposition
B.1
below). This property is the inspiration for the arguments in Section
4.5
and can also be viewed as a discrete analog of certain variants of our main results.

An
(open) meander
of size
m
is a self-avoiding loop from
\infty
to
\infty
in
\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
which intersects
\mathbb{R}
at points
1,2,\dots,2m-1
, crossing it transversally at each of those points, and viewed modulo homeomorphisms
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
with
f(\mathbb{H})=\mathbb{H}
and
f(i)=i
for
i\in\{1,\dots,2m-1\}
.
Meanders have been studied at least since the work of Poincaré
[
Poi12
]
, and are connected to many different topics in math and physics, but are notoriously difficult to analyze mathematically. See
[
Zvo21
,
La 03
]
for expository works on meanders.
A meander of size
m
can be viewed as a planar map decorated by two Hamiltonian paths (corresponding to the loop and the real line). It is conjectured in
[
BGS22
]
, building on
[
DFGG00
]
, that uniform random meanders of size
m
converge in the scaling limit to the LQG sphere with central charge
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}=30
together with two independent SLE
8
curves.

A meander can be encoded by a pair of
arc diagrams
(non-crossing perfect matchings) on
\{1,\dots,2m-1\}\cup\{\infty\}
, one drawn above and the other drawn below
\mathbb{R}
. Orient the meander curve so it starts at
\infty
in the lower half-plane and ends at
\infty
in the upper half-plane. Then each arc inherits an orientation from the meander.

We can define a
winding function

\theta:\{1,\dots,2m-1\}\to\mathbb{R}
as follows. With the above orientation of the curve, let it intersect
\mathbb{R}
at points
v_{1},\dots,v_{2m-1}
in order (so
v_{1},\dots,v_{2m-1}
is a permutation of
1,\dots,2m-1
). Let
\theta(v_{1})=0
, and inductively, we set
\theta(v_{i+1})=\theta(v_{i})+\pi
if the arc from
v_{i}
to
v_{i+1}
is traversed in the counterclockwise direction and otherwise set
\theta(v_{i+1})=\theta(v_{i})-\pi
. See Figure
14
.
Note that if we draw the arcs as semicircles and rays as in the figure, then
\theta(v)+\frac{\pi}{2}
is the restriction to
\{1,\dots,2m-1\}
of a winding function of the curve (defined in Section
2.6
).

Figure 14:
The four meanders with winding function given by
(\theta(1),\dots,\theta(7))=(0,\pi,0,-\pi,0,\pi,0)
.
In the leftmost diagram, we have
(v_{1},\dots,v_{7})=(3,2,1,4,7,6,5)
.
In the proof of Proposition
B.1
, we have
A_{\theta}^{+}=\{\text{red, blue}\}
and
A_{\theta}^{-}=\{\text{purple, pink}\}
, and the set of all meanders with winding function
\theta
corresponds to
A_{\theta}^{+}\times A_{\theta}^{-}
.

We have the following conditional independence statement for meanders, whose proof is a simpler version of the argument of Section
4.5
.

Proposition B.1
.

For
m\geq 1
, sample a meander uniformly at random from the set of all meanders of size
m
. Conditioned on its winding function
\theta:\{1,\dots,2m-1\}\to\mathbb{R}
, the (oriented) arc diagrams above and below
\mathbb{R}
are conditionally independent.

Proof.

Fix
\theta
, and let
A^{+}_{\theta}
(resp.
A^{-}_{\theta}
) be the set of all arc diagrams on
\{1,\dots,2m-1\}\cup\{\infty\}
drawn above
\mathbb{R}
(resp. below
\mathbb{R}
), such that for every arc with endpoints
1\leq x<y\leq 2m-1
we have
\theta(y)=\theta(x)-\pi
(resp.
\theta(y)=\theta(x)+\pi
). For each arc diagram in
A_{\theta}^{+}
(resp.
A_{\theta}^{-}
), we give the arc hitting
\infty
the orientation pointing towards (resp. away from)
\infty
, then orient all other arcs such that along
\mathbb{R}
the arcs alternately enter and exit
\mathbb{R}
.
By the definition of
\theta
, every meander with winding function
\theta
decomposes into a pair of arc diagrams in
A^{+}_{\theta}\times A^{-}_{\theta}
, see Figure
14
. We claim that conversely, every pair of arc diagrams in
A^{+}_{\theta}\times A^{-}_{\theta}
gives a meander with winding function
\theta
. Indeed, any pair of arc diagrams in
A^{+}_{\theta}\times A^{-}_{\theta}
form a collection of one or more loops in
\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
; suppose for the sake of contradiction there is more than one loop. Pick a loop not containing
\infty
. By the definitions of
A_{\theta}^{+},A_{\theta}^{-}
, when tracing the loop, each clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) arc should decrease (resp. increase) the winding function by
\pi
, so since the winding function at the start and end of the traversal is the same, there must be an equal number of clockwise and counterclockwise half-turns. This is topologically impossible: Hopf’s Umlaufsatz states that the total curvature of a simple smooth loop is
\pm 2\pi
, meaning
\#\text{clockwise turns}-\#\text{counterclockwise turns}=\pm 2
. Thus, every pair in
A_{\theta}^{+}\times A_{\theta}^{-}
gives a single loop from
\infty
to
\infty
. Each such loop has winding function
\theta
by the definition of
A_{\theta}^{\pm}
.

We conclude that the set of all meanders with winding function
\theta
is in bijection with
A_{\theta}^{+}\times A_{\theta}^{-}
, giving the desired conditional independence.
∎

Remark B.2
.

A
meandric system
is defined in a similar manner to a meander, except that one allows there to be multiple loops.
For a meandric system, the arc diagrams above and below the real line are exactly independent, rather than just conditionally independent. It is conjectured in
[
BGP22
]
that uniform meandric systems converge in the scaling limit to the LQG sphere with central charge
{\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{L}}}=28
together with an SLE
8
curve and an independent CLE
6
. Since
{\mathbf{c}}(\kappa=8)=-2=26-28
, the LQG and the SLE
8
curve have “matched” parameter values. The fact that we have conditional independence, rather than exact independence, in Proposition
B.1
is analogous to the fact that we have conditional independence, rather than exact independence, in the main results of this paper, as stated in Section
1.3
.

We expect that it is possible to extend the results of this paper to get a continuum analog of Proposition
B.1
. See Problem
5.7
and the discussion just after. We plan to explore discrete analogs of the results of this paper in the setting of meanders and meandric systems further in future work.

Appendix C
Dubédat’s proof of locality

In this appendix we explain the proof of Theorem
3.10
. It is a special case of Theorem
C.1
below, which was proved by Dubédat who used it to establish his seminal SLE-GFF coupling
[
Dub09b
]
. It is not stated as a theorem in
[
Dub09b
]
, but its discrete analog is stated in the last equation of Section 4.1 there.

In the setting of Section
3.2.1
, we have four simply connected domains
D_{ij}
having a common sub-domain
B
bounded by a pair of cross cuts and common boundary point
x\in\partial B\cap\partial D_{ij}
.
Recall that
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}
in
(D_{ij},x)
as defined in Definition
1.7
can be viewed as a probability measure on fields in
D_{ij}
modulo
2\pi\chi(\mathbf{c})
; let
\Psi
be a sample with its additive constant fixed by requiring that its boundary value infinitesimally counterclockwise of
x
in
\partial D_{ij}
lies in
[0,2\pi\chi(\mathbf{c}))
. Let
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}^{B,ij}
be the law of
\psi=\Psi|_{B}
. Note that here,
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}^{B,ij}
is a measure on the space of generalized functions and not
\mathbf{c}
-IG fields; this is a slight change in notation from Theorem
3.10
.

Theorem C.1

(
[
Dub09b
]
)
.

We have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{11}}){\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{22}})}{{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{21}}){\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{12}})}}}\right)^{-\mathbf{c}/2}\frac{d\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}^{B,11}}{d\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}^{B,21}}(\psi)\frac{d\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}^{B,22}}{d\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}^{B,12}}(\psi)=1\qquad\text{ for }\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}^{B,ij}\text{-a.e. }\psi

(C.1)

where
{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D})
is the zeta regularized determinant of the Dirichlet Laplacian.

We note that Theorem
C.1
suggests the partition function of
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}
in a domain
D
should be
{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D})^{-{\mathbf{c}}/2}
.

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

Set
\mathbf{c}=0
in Theorem
C.1
.
∎

Let
D
be one of the domains
D_{ij}
and let
m=m_{ij}
be the harmonic function on
D_{ij}
whose boundary values agree with those of a sample from
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}
in
(D_{ij},x)
with additive constant fixed as before. Note that
m_{ij}
is continuous on
\partial D_{ij}
except at
x
where there is a jump of size
2\pi\chi(\mathbf{c})
. Let
\delta=\partial B\cap D_{ij}
be the union of the pair of crosscuts bounding
B
. Let
\mu
be the law of
\Psi|_{\delta}
where
\Psi
is a GFF with boundary conditions given by
m
, and
\mu^{0}
the law of
\Psi^{0}|_{\delta}
where
\Psi^{0}
is a zero boundary GFF on
D
.
As explained in
[
Dub09b
, Section 4.3]
,
\mu
and
\mu^{0}
are probability measures on the negative Sobolev space
H^{-s}(\delta)
for any
s>0
. We will write
w
to denote a field on
\delta
. Let
Pw
be the harmonic extension of
w
to
D\backslash\delta
with zero boundary conditions on
\partial D
.

For a function
f
on
D
and a point
z\in\delta
let
\partial_{\ell}f(z):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-1}(f(z+\varepsilon n_{\ell})-f(z))
where
n_{\ell}
is the left-pointing normal unit vector on
\delta
at
w
, and define
\partial_{r}
the same way with left replaced by right. Thus
\partial_{\ell}f
and
\partial_{r}f
are functions with domain
\delta
, and
(\partial_{\ell}+\partial_{r})f=0
at points where
f
is smooth.

Let
m^{\prime}=m-P(m|_{\delta})
. This is the function on
D
such that
m^{\prime}|_{\delta}=0,m^{\prime}|_{\partial D}=m|_{\partial D}
, and
m^{\prime}
is harmonic on
D\backslash\delta
. The following computation is carried out in
[
Dub09b
, Proof of Lemma 6.3]
. We repeat the proof for the reader’s convenience.

Lemma C.2
.

Define the regularized Dirichlet energy

(m,m)_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{(2\pi\chi(\mathbf{c}))^{2}}{\pi}\log\varepsilon+\int_{D_{ij}\backslash B_{\varepsilon}(x)}\nabla m\cdot\nabla m}}\right).

With the following integral performed with respect to arc length on
\delta
, we have

\frac{d\mu}{d\mu^{0}}(w)=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\delta}w(\partial_{\ell}+\partial_{r})m^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}(m^{\prime},m^{\prime})_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}+\frac{1}{2}(m,m)_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}}}\right).

Proof.

Let
m^{\prime\prime}=P(m|_{\delta})
, so
m^{\prime\prime}|_{\delta}=m|_{\delta}
and
m^{\prime\prime}|_{\partial D}=0
.
Let
\Psi^{0}
be a zero boundary GFF on
D
. By Girsanov’s theorem (see e.g.
[
MS17
, Proposition 2.9]
), the Radon-Nikodym derivative of the law of
\Psi^{0}+m^{\prime\prime}
with respect to that of
\Psi^{0}
is

\exp((\Psi^{0},m^{\prime\prime})_{\nabla}-\frac{1}{2}(m^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})_{\nabla}).

By Stoke’s theorem in each of the components of
D\backslash\delta
and since
\Delta m^{\prime\prime}|_{D\backslash\delta}=0
and
\Phi^{0}|_{\partial D}=0
,

(\Psi^{0},m^{\prime\prime})_{\nabla}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\delta}\Psi^{0}(\partial_{\ell}+\partial_{r})m^{\prime\prime}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\delta}\Psi^{0}(\partial_{\ell}+\partial_{r})m^{\prime}.

Here, the second equality follows from
m=m^{\prime}+m^{\prime\prime}
and
(\partial_{\ell}+\partial_{r})m=0
.
Thus,

\frac{d\mu}{d\mu^{0}}(w)=\exp(-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\delta}w(\partial_{\ell}+\partial_{r})m^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}(m^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})_{\nabla}).

(C.2)

Finally, we need to show that
(m^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})_{\nabla}=(m^{\prime},m^{\prime})_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}-(m,m)_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
m_{\varepsilon}
be a harmonic function on
D
with continuous boundary data.
With
m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}=P(m_{\varepsilon}|_{\delta})
and
m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=m_{\varepsilon}-m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}
, Stoke’s theorem gives
2\pi(m_{\varepsilon},m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})_{\nabla}=-\int_{\partial D}m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\partial_{n}m_{\varepsilon}+\int_{D}m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\Delta m_{\varepsilon}
. This is zero because
m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}|_{\partial D}=0
and
\Delta m_{\varepsilon}=0
, so expanding
(m_{\varepsilon},m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})_{\nabla}=0
gives
(m_{\varepsilon},m_{\varepsilon})_{\nabla}=(m_{\varepsilon},m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})_{\nabla}
. This implies

(m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime},m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})_{\nabla}=(m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})_{\nabla}-(m_{\varepsilon},m_{\varepsilon})_{\nabla}
.

We will now choose
m_{\varepsilon}
to approximate
m
then send
\varepsilon\to 0
. Assume the boundary data of
m_{\varepsilon}
agrees with that of
m
in
\partial D\backslash B_{\varepsilon^{2}}(x)
, and assume this boundary data is uniformly bounded for all
\varepsilon
.
Since
m_{\varepsilon}|_{\delta}
converges uniformly to
m|_{\delta}
we have
(m^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon},m^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon})_{\nabla}\to(m^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})_{\nabla}
. Next, since the harmonic measure of
\partial D\cap B_{\varepsilon^{2}}(x)
is
o_{\varepsilon}(1)
uniformly for all
z\in D\backslash B_{\varepsilon}(x)
, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{D\backslash B_{\varepsilon}(x)}\nabla m_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla m_{\varepsilon}-\nabla m\cdot\nabla m}}\right|=o_{\varepsilon}(1)
, and similarly
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{D\backslash B_{\varepsilon}(x)}\nabla m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}-\nabla m^{\prime}\cdot\nabla m^{\prime}}}\right|=o_{\varepsilon}(1)
. Moreover, since Brownian motion started in
D\cap B_{\varepsilon}(x)
hits
\partial D
before
B_{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}(x)
with probability
1-o_{\varepsilon}(1)
uniformly in the starting point, and the harmonic functions
m_{\varepsilon}
in
D
and
m^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
in
D\backslash\delta
have the same boundary data on
\partial D\cap B_{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}(x)
, we conclude
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{D\cap B_{\varepsilon}(x)}\nabla m_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla m_{\varepsilon}-\nabla m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\cdot\nabla m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}}\right|=o_{\varepsilon}(1)
. To summarize,
(m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime},m_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})_{\nabla}-(m_{\varepsilon},m_{\varepsilon})_{\nabla}\to(m^{\prime},m^{\prime})_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}-(m,m)_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Therefore
(m^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})_{\nabla}=(m^{\prime},m^{\prime})_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}-(m,m)_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, which with (
C.2
) completes the proof.
∎

The following identity follows from setting
(a,b)=(0,\chi(\mathbf{c})/\sqrt{2\pi})
in
[
Dub09b
, Proposition 5.2]
.

Lemma C.3

(
[
Dub09b
, Proposition 5.2]
)
.

There is a constant
\lambda
such that

e^{-\frac{1}{2}(m,m)_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}}=\lambda{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D})^{3\chi(\mathbf{c})^{2}}.

Now we state two other results from
[
Dub09b
]
. Let
m^{l}(D,;K_{1},K_{2})
be the mass of Brownian loops in
D
intersecting both
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
, as introduced in
[
LW04
]
.

Lemma C.4

(
[
Dub09b
, Proposition 2.1]
)
.

Let
C
be a collar neighborhood of
\delta
in
D
, that is, a neighborhood of
\delta
which is diffeomorphic to
\delta\times(-1,1)
via a diffeomoephism which takes the intersection of the neighborhood with
\partial D
to
(\partial\delta)\times(-1,1)
. Then

\exp(-m^{l}(D;\delta,D\backslash C))=\frac{{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D}){\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{C\backslash\delta})}{{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D\backslash\delta}){\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{C})}.

For
i,j,\in\{1,2\}
, let
\mu_{ij}
(resp.
\mu^{0}_{ij}
) be the law of
h
sampled from
\mathrm{IG}_{\mathbf{c}}^{K,ij}
(resp. zero boundary GFF on
D_{ij}
) restricted to
\delta
. Let
P_{ij}w
be the function on
D_{ij}
which is harmonic on
D_{ij}\backslash\delta
, equals
w
on
\delta
and equals zero on
\partial D_{ij}
.

Lemma C.5

(
[
Dub09b
, Lemma 4.4]
)
.

Let
C
be any common collar neighborhood of
\delta
for
D_{ij}
and
D_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
. Then

\frac{d\mu_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}^{0}}{d\mu_{ij}^{0}}(w)=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi((P_{ij}w,P_{ij}w)_{\nabla}-(P_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}w,P_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}w)_{\nabla})+\frac{1}{2}(m^{l}(D_{ij};\delta,D_{ij}\backslash C)-m^{l}(D_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}};\delta,D_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\backslash C))}}\right).

We turn to the proof of Theorem
C.1
. It is essentially done in
[
Dub09b
, Proof of Lemma 6.3]
.

Proof of Theorem
C.1
.

All Radon-Nikodym derivative identities in this proof hold for
\mu_{ij}
-a.e.
w
.
We have

\frac{d\mu_{11}}{d\mu_{21}}\times\frac{d\mu_{22}}{d\mu_{12}}=\frac{d\mu_{11}}{d\mu_{11}^{0}}\frac{d\mu_{11}^{0}}{d\mu_{21}^{0}}\frac{d\mu_{21}^{0}}{d\mu_{21}}\times\frac{d\mu_{22}}{d\mu_{22}^{0}}\frac{d\mu_{22}^{0}}{d\mu_{12}^{0}}\frac{d\mu_{12}^{0}}{d\mu_{12}}.

Let
P_{ij}w
denote the harmonic function on
D_{ij}
with boundary conditions of
w
on
\delta
and zero boundary conditions on
\partial D_{ij}
. Then
m_{ij}^{\prime}:=m_{ij}-P_{ij}(m_{ij}|_{\delta})
is the harmonic function on
D_{ij}\backslash\delta
with boundary conditions on
\partial D_{ij}
agreeing with those of
m_{ij}
, and zero boundary conditions on
\delta
.
Notice that the boundary values of
m_{ij}
in the left subdomain of
D_{ij}
do not depend on
j
since the boundary values near
x
do not depend on
j
and the boundary values along
\partial\mathbb{D}
in the clockwise direction from
x
vary according to the total curvature (winding) of the boundary.
Thus
m_{ij}^{\prime}
restricted to the left subdomain of
D_{ij}
does not depend on
j
, and likewise
m_{ij}^{\prime}
restricted to the right subdomain does not depend on
i
. Thus, by Lemma
C.2

\frac{d\mu_{11}}{d\mu_{11}^{0}}\frac{d\mu_{21}^{0}}{d\mu_{21}}\frac{d\mu_{22}}{d\mu_{22}^{0}}\frac{d\mu_{12}^{0}}{d\mu_{12}}=\exp(\frac{1}{2}((m_{11},m_{11})_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}-(m_{21},m_{21})_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}+(m_{22},m_{22})_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}}-(m_{12},m_{12})_{\nabla}^{\mathrm{reg}})),

since all terms involving
m^{\prime}_{ij}
cancel. Next, since
P_{ij}w
restricted to the left (resp. right) subdomain of
D_{ij}
does not depend on
j
(resp.
i
), we have

(P_{11}w,P_{11}w)_{\nabla}-(P_{21}w,P_{21}w)_{\nabla}+(P_{22}w,P_{22}w)_{\nabla}-(P_{12}w,P_{12}w)_{\nabla}=0.

Thus, by Lemmas
C.4
and
C.5
, we have

\frac{d\mu_{11}^{0}}{d\mu_{21}^{0}}\frac{d\mu_{22}^{0}}{d\mu_{12}^{0}}=\sqrt{\frac{{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{11}}){\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{22}})}{{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{21}}){\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{12}})}}.

Combining the above identities and Lemma
C.3
, and using
-\frac{1}{2}+3\chi(\mathbf{c})^{2}=-\frac{\mathbf{c}}{2}
, we conclude

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{11}}){\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{22}})}{{\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{21}}){\det}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{D_{12}})}}}\right)^{-\mathbf{c}/2}\frac{d\mu_{11}}{d\mu_{21}}\frac{d\mu_{22}}{d\mu_{12}}=1.

By the domain Markov property of the Gaussian free field, this implies (
C.1
).
∎
